24.0 Early humic acid-induced eustress triggers biostimulant responses in Arabidopsis thaliana: RBOHC-derived H2O2 drives growth promotion while RBOHD enables nutritional reprogramming.
Rubio Á, Olaetxea M, Erro J … García-Mina JM.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-09-29
matched: ROS, H2O2, Rboh*, NADPH oxidase*, signaling, auxin*, ABA, cytokinin*, jasmon*, nutrient*, plasma membrane, root
abstract

Root-applied humic acids (HAs) promote plant development, yet the early signaling mechanisms underlying their biostimulant action remain incompletely understood. Here, we show that treatment of Arabidopsis thaliana roots with a sedimentary humic acid (SHA) triggers a transient eustress-like response, characterized by early inhibition of shoot and root growth and increased H2O2 accumulation in root hairs and apical roots. This initial oxidative phase was followed by enhanced shoot and root growth, supporting the view that SHA acts as an adaptive plant modulator through a controlled stress-related signal. Pharmacological H2O2 scavenging and genetic analysis of NADPH oxidase mutants revealed that SHA-induced growth promotion depends on RBOHC/RHD2-mediated H2O2 production in roots. In rhd2 plants, SHA failed to induce the characteristic H2O2 increase and did not promote shoot or root growth. By contrast, rbohd plants retained SHA-induced growth stimulation but showed impaired activation of nutrient transporter and plasma membrane H+-ATPase genes, limited improvement in shoot nutrient use efficiency, and partial root architectural remodeling. These results indicate that RBOHC-derived H2O2 is required for the growth-promoting response, whereas RBOHD cooperates with RBOHC to engage broader nutritional and developmental programs. SHA also induced extensive hormonal reprogramming in roots and shoots; however, disruption of individual ABA, auxin, jasmonate, or cytokinin signaling components did not abolish growth promotion, suggesting partially redundant hormonal regulation downstream of ROS. Overall, our findings identify an RBOHC-H2O2 module as a primary entry point for SHA-mediated biostimulant action and support a model in which root-localized oxidative eustress coordinates hormonal, developmental, and nutritional reprogramming in Arabidopsis.

21.5 A calcium-fingerprint atlas infers the signaling modes of complex elicitors and reveals a salt-like response to a humic biostimulant
Zupunski, M., Brugman … G.
preprintKinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-14
matched: calcium, Ca2+, cytosolic calcium, salt sens*, signaling, receptor*, MAMP*, DAMP*, elicitor*, root, roots, shoot*
abstract

A central challenge in plant signaling is to infer, from an observed response, the mechanism that produced it, including perception and signaling components involved. Cytosolic calcium (Ca2+) is well suited to this reverse-engineering problem: it is a universal early messenger whose spatiotemporal pattern, the Ca2+ signature, is shaped by the specific channels and receptors a stimulus activates. We hypothesize that multiparametric analysis of Ca2+ signals can identify the main signaling pathways activated by complex or poorly defined elicitors such as biostimulants. Here, we establish a quantitative framework to resolve Ca2+ dynamics in roots of Arabidopsis seedlings by transforming spatiotemporal Ca2+ transients into multidimensional signatures. Using a normalization and feature extraction approach (Crestline), we define descriptors capturing the onset time, location along the root axis, duration, and propagation of Ca2+ signals tip- or shootwards, enabling robust comparison across stimuli. Integration of these descriptors reveals stimulus-specific "calcium fingerprints" that allow systematic classification of responses to biotic and abiotic cues within a structured signaling space. To test whether projecting an uncharacterized input into this space can predict its signaling mode, we analyzed a complex humic biostimulant. Its response fell within the fast-onset region of signaling space shared by DAMP and salt responses, clearly apart from MAMP responses, and was altered in moca1, a mutant impaired in salt sensing. Together, these findings establish Ca2+ dynamics as an interpretable encoding layer for environmental information and provide a scalable framework to resolve complex biological inputs.

19.0 A calcium-independent CDPK positively regulates drought resistance in maize by modulating the ABA signaling pathway.
Fang T, Han Y, Chen Y … Gong Z.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressJournal of genetics and genomics = Yi chuan xue bao · 2026-10-09
matched: calcium, CPK*, CDPK*, kinase*, phosphoryl*, phosphatase*, signaling, abscisic acid, ABA, drought, stoma*, growth
abstract

Calcium-dependent protein kinases (CDPKs/CPKs) play crucial roles in plant responses to drought stress. In this study, we reveal that ZmCPK1, an atypical CDPK whose kinase activity is calcium-independent, acts as a positive regulator of drought resistance in maize. ZmCPK1 is a constitutively active kinase interacting with and phosphorylates multiple components of the abscisic acid (ABA) signaling pathway, including ZmSLAC1, ZmSnRK2.10, and ZmPP2C11. In the absence of ABA, ZmCPK1 may phosphorylate ZmSnRK2.10 and the N-terminus of ZmSLAC1. ZmPP2C11 reverses this phosphorylation by directly dephosphorylating the N-terminus of ZmSLAC1 rather than suppressing ZmCPK1 kinase activity. ABA treatment and dehydration enhance ZmCPK1-mediated phosphorylation of the N-terminus of ZmSLAC1 at the conserved serine 54 (S54) without affecting ZmCPK1 autophosphorylation, consistent with reduced dephosphorylation resulting from the inhibition of ZmPP2C11 by ABA-bound pyrabactin resistance 1-like (PYL) proteins. Notably, ZmCPK1 phosphorylates ZmPP2C11 at S277, a modification that potentially inhibits its phosphatase activity, and phosphorylates multiple sites on ZmSnRK2.10 to activate it. Thus, ZmCPK1 acts as a constitutive kinase; under optimal growth conditions, it interacts with ZmPP2C11, which dephosphorylates the targets phosphorylated by ZmCPK1. Under drought stress, ZmPP2C11 is inhibited, and phosphorylated downstream targets are activated to promote stomatal closure, thereby positively regulating drought resistance in maize.

18.5 CML20 integrates phytochrome and calcium signaling pathways to optimize seed germination under abiotic stress.
Kang J, Li J, Liu X … Shi H.
Kinases and signal transductionGrowthNature communications · 2026-09-04
matched: calcium, Ca2+, calcium signal*, calmodulin*, CML*, signaling, seed*, germination
abstract

Plants integrate multiple environmental cues to decide the timing of seed germination. Light promotes germination primarily through photoreceptor phytochrome B (phyB), which eliminates the action of transcription factor PHYTOCHROME-INTERACTING FACTOR 1 (PIF1). Conversely, abiotic stresses typically elevate cytosolic Ca2+ and inhibit germination. However, the coordination of light and calcium signaling during germination under stress conditions remains poorly understood. Here, we demonstrate that salt and osmotic stresses rapidly increase cytosolic Ca2+ in imbibed seeds, and that the calcium sensor CALMODULIN-LIKE 20 (CML20) is crucial for germination tolerance to these stresses. CML20 directly interacts with PIF1 in a Ca2+-dependent manner, suppressing PIF1 self-association, oligomerization, and DNA-binding capacity. Moreover, phyB interacts with CML20 and PIF1 independently, promoting the assembly of the CML20-PIF1 complex and further reducing PIF1's transcriptional activity. Genetic analyses reveal that PIF1 functions downstream of CML20, and that CML20 and phyB operate within the same pathway. Together, our findings define a phyB-CML20-PIF1 module, providing a rapid, reversible, and non-proteolytic mechanism to optimize seed germination under stress conditions.

17.5 AtCAMTA4 negatively regulates salinity stress response via regulation of ROS and Na+/K+ homeostasis.
Chithung TA, Kansal S, Raghuvanshi U … Raghuvanshi S.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-02
matched: calcium, ROS, reactive oxygen species, calmodulin*, defence, salt stress, salinity, stress respon*, vacuol*, growth
abstract

Calmodulin-binding transcription activators (CAMTAs) are key components of calcium-mediated regulation of diverse developmental and stress-responsive processes in plants. Although the functions of several Arabidopsis CAMTA family members have been investigated, the role of CAMTA4 remained largely unexplored. In the present study, we demonstrate that CAMTA4 acts as a negative regulator of salinity tolerance. Integrated physiological, transcriptomic, metabolomic and small RNA analyses revealed that camta4 mutants exhibit reduced membrane damage, lower Na+ and reactive oxygen species (ROS) accumulation, and extensive transcriptional reprogramming under salt stress. Reduced Na + accumulation appears to result primarily from enhanced SOS1-mediated Na + exclusion and attenuated induction of HKT1;1. While stable levels of AKT1 accompanied with elevated levels of TPK1 suggests complementary mechanisms to maintain cellular Na+/K+ homeostasis by preferential mobilization of vacuolar K+ reserves. Lower ROS levels were associated with differential expression of CAT2 and CAT3, together with increased accumulation of proline, glutamine and lysine, indicating coordinated regulation of antioxidant defence and osmo-protective metabolism. In addition, CAMTA4 substantially reshaped the sRNA landscape by regulating conserved salinity-responsive miRNA modules, including miR156-SPL9, miR169-NF-YA and miR408-LAC thereby extending its regulatory influence into post-transcriptional gene regulation. Although CAMTA3/4/6 have all been implicated in salinity responses, their mechanisms appear to be distinct. Unlike the pleiotropic camta3, camta4 mutants exhibited no obvious developmental abnormalities under normal growth conditions, suggesting that CAMTA4 has evolved a specialized regulatory niche. The apparent absence of major developmental defects also identifies CAMTA4 as a promising target for engineering salinity tolerance with minimal pleiotropic effects.

17.5 Decoding Drought: The CBL9–CIPK11 Phosphoswitch in Guard Cells
Hasan MM, Alabdallah NM
Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesPlant, Cell & Environment · 2026-10-14
matched: calcium, calcium signal*, CBL*, CIPK*, phosphoryl*, signalling, drought, cytoskelet*, actin, stoma*, guard cell*
abstract

Summary statement The study identifies a phosphorylation‐dependent calcium signalling module, PuCBL9–PuCIPK11, that remodels the guard‐cell actin cytoskeleton in Populus ussuriensis to accelerate stomatal closure. This work bridges a long‐standing gap between calcium decoding and cytoskeletal dynamics in trees, and opens a new translational avenue for forest improvement.

17.0 P2K1 P2K2 Coordinate Early, Structure-Specific Mechanical Adaptations to Osmotic and Cell Wall Stress in Arabidopsis Roots
Amanda, D., Badias … T.
preprintKinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-16
matched: extracellular ATP, P2K1, receptor*, cell wall*, plasma membrane, vacuol*, roots, microscop*
abstract

The cell wall and plasma membrane (PM) form the interface between a plant cell and its environment yet how their mechanical properties change in response to stress is poorly understood. Here we investigate how the mechanical properties of the cell wall, plasma membrane, and vacuole change in vivo in Arabidopsis thaliana roots in response to hyperosmotic stress and cell wall damage (CWD) using a solvatochromic dye, molecular rotors, and Brillouin microscopy. We observe initially dynamic changes in mechanical properties of the PM that are sensitive to both hyperosmotic stress and CWD, while the cell wall changes are limited and the vacuole adapts only after several hours. In the p2k1 p2k2 double mutant, lacking receptors for extracellular ATP perception, sorbitol-induced effects on PM mechanostasis and early isoxaben-induced increases in PM order are lost. Our results indicate that P2K1 and P2K2 are required to modulate adaptive changes in mechanical properties of the PM induced by hyperosmotic stress and CWD.

15.5 Peptide Signalling as a Control Layer for Signal Prioritisation in Plant Stress Networks
Ali B, Poczai P, Imin N
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityPlant, Cell & Environment · 2026-10-19
matched: calcium, cytosolic calcium, reactive oxygen species, kinase*, signalling, receptor*, RALF*, ABA, hormone*, immun*, drought, nutrient*
abstract

ABSTRACT Plants in the field rarely encounter single stresses, and simultaneous stresses impose conflicting physiological demands that cannot be satisfied at once. Responses to such combinations are frequently non‐additive and cannot be predicted from single‐stress responses, a result that crosstalk models struggle to accommodate because they describe which pathways interact without specifying which prevails. This review proposes that peptide signalling functions as a control layer governing signal prioritisation. Under this hypothesis, secreted peptides act above the shared modules of cytosolic calcium, reactive oxygen species, and mitogen‐activated protein kinase cascades, and determine which competing input is permitted to dominate their behaviour. Evidence for this proposal spans several peptide families. CEP, CLE and RALF signalling illustrate systemic demand signalling, developmental stabilisation, and receptor complex reorganisation respectively, and the same logic extends to PEP, IDA and PSK signalling across immune, developmental, and drought contexts. Combined drought and nitrogen limitation, resolved through ABA and CEP, provides a worked case in which a global signal sets operating boundaries within which a local signal refines the response, supported by evidence now extending from Arabidopsis into cereals. We derive falsifiable predictions tested against hormone‐pathway negative controls and graded drought intensities rather than single treatments, which allows loss of prioritisation to be distinguished from general signalling fragility. We also identify a trade‐off constraining crop application, since enhancing peptide‐mediated nutrient foraging may reduce drought resilience. Substantial gaps remain in detecting peptide signals directly and in ranking peptide families under field conditions. Reframing plant signalling as a decision system rather than an input‐output relay offers a testable foundation for improving stress resilience and nutrient use efficiency in crop breeding.

14.5 γ-aminobutyric acid is associated with calcium, reactive oxygen species, and nitric oxide responses and enhanced cadmium tolerance in Salvadora persica.
Perveen S, Haider I, Khalili A … Shalaby TA.
Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressGrowthPlant signaling & behavior · 2026-10-14
matched: calcium, Ca2+, ROS, reactive oxygen species, signaling, cadmium, root, shoot*
abstract

Backgroundγ-Aminobutyric acid (GABA) accumulates under stress, but its association with calcium (Ca2+), reactive oxygen species (ROS), nitric oxide (NO), and thiol responses during cadmium (Cd) exposure remains unclear in medicinal shrubs.MethodsRooted Salvadora persica L. cuttings were grown in sand-soil substrate. Screening selected 50mg Cd kg-1 substrate and 0.5 mM root-applied GABA. Eleven treatments had five independent pots each. Ca2+-sensitive Fluo-3 AM and NO-associated DAF-FM DA fluorescence and H₂O₂ were assessed in separate, destructively sampled plants at five post-application times. The 0-min value was a post-application reference; probe fluorescence was not calibrated to analyte concentration. Pot means were independent units, with time treated as a between-pot factor. A core Cd × GABA × time model and a separate categorical endpoint model were specified. The exact p-values, adjusted q-values, and model diagnostics could not be verified without pot-level records. Correlation and principal component analyses were exploratory; no path model was fitted.ResultsCd reduced shoot dry weight from 3.04 to 1.89 g plant-1 (37.8%) and increased malondialdehyde from 4.37 to 9.86 nmol g-1 FW (125.6%). Relative to Cd alone, GABA increased the shoot dry weight to 2.62 g plant-1 (38.6%) and reduced the malondialdehyde to 4.34 nmol g-1 FW (56.0%). GABA was associated with a 2.2-fold greater Ca2+-sensitive fluorescence response amplitude at 30 min and 2.7-fold greater NO-associated amplitude at 360 min. LaCl₃, EGTA, DPI, DMTU, and cPTIO attenuated GABA-associated protection. Inhibitor-only and rescue controls were absent, and inhibitor phytotoxicity pre-screening was undocumented. GABA also increased total phenolics from 22.77 to 26.54 mg GAE g-1 DW (16.6%) and flavonoids from 13.21 to 15.78 mg QE g-1 DW (19.5%) relative to Cd alone.ConclusionGABA was associated with improved Cd tolerance and differences in sampled fluorescence, H₂O₂, and thiol responses. These data cannot establish a continuous signaling sequence, upstream GABA action, or target-specific pharmacological effects. The attenuation is compatible with network involvement and feedback, but pathway direction and pharmacological specificity require inhibitor-only controls, rescue experiments, and independent, well-controlled temporal validation in future studies.

14.0 Arsenic Resistance Protein BcArs2 Modulates Endogenous ROS Homeostasis in Infection Cushions to Enhance the Virulence of Botrytis cinerea Toward Host Plants.
Zhang Y, Feng H, Chen X … Qin QM.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesMolecular plant pathology · 2026-10-01
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, H2O2, pathogen*, Botrytis, stress respon*, nucleus, growth, development, microscop*
abstract

Botrytis cinerea triggers destructive grey mould with substantial economic losses, yet the functions of numerous pathogenicity genes remain largely unclear. Here, by screening a T-DNA insertion library, we identified BcARS2, a key component of the Microprocessor complex. BcARS2 is constitutively expressed, localizes to the nucleus, and modulates gene expression. The ΔBcars2 mutants exhibited impaired vegetative growth, enhanced conidiophore sporulation, increased conidiation, reduced sclerotial size, and increased sensitivity to multiple stresses. Pathogenicity assays showed that different inoculation methods led to distinct virulence phenotypes in ΔBcars2 mutants. Microscopic observations revealed that although the ΔBcars2 mutant formed normal appressoria and successfully invaded host cells, its invasive hyphae exhibited limited expansion. Furthermore, exogenous cAMP did not restore the impaired development of infection cushions (ICs), resulting in defective infection. Multitranscriptome analyses demonstrated that BcARS2 positively regulates BcPDI1 and BcCGF1, two key genes responsible for reactive oxygen species (ROS) homeostasis and highly expressed during the IC developmental stage. Correspondingly, deletion of BcARS2 upregulated catalase gene expression, elevated catalase activity, reduced H2O2 accumulation, disturbed ROS balance, and ultimately inhibited IC formation. In addition, most toxin biosynthesis-related genes, which are normally upregulated during IC development, were significantly downregulated in the ΔBcars2 mutant. Collectively, BcARS2 modulates the virulence of B. cinerea by regulating IC development, vegetative hyphal growth, and stress responses.

14.0 PuWRKY46 drives root system architecture and enhances salt tolerance in poplar
Zhao H, Zhao S, Sun T … Yang J
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressNew Phytologist · 2026-10-16
matched: apoplast*, salt sens*, kinase*, phosphoryl*, signaling, abscisic acid, ABA, salt stress, cell wall*, root, roots, lateral root*
abstract

Summary The developmental plasticity of lateral roots (LRs) is an essential adaptive strategy for optimizing root system architecture under salt stress. However, the molecular mechanisms by which salt stress modulates LR plasticity through cell wall remodeling remain largely elusive, particularly in woody species. This study investigates the abscisic acid (ABA)‐responsive transcription factor PuWRKY46 as a critical regulator of LR developmental plasticity under salt stress in Populus ussuriensis . This study integrated phenotypic characterization of transgenic overexpression and RNA interference (RNAi) poplar lines, molecular biochemical assays, protein–protein interaction analyses, transcriptomics, DNA affinity purification sequencing (DAP‐seq), phosphorylation assays, and cell wall biochemical analyses. Compared with wild‐type plants, PuWRKY46 overexpression promotes LR emergence and enhances salt tolerance, whereas PuWRKY46‐ RNAi lines exhibit pronounced salt sensitivity. Furthermore, the mitogen‐activated protein kinase PuMPK15 interacts with PuWRKY46, and the resulting phosphorylation stabilizes PuWRKY46 to enhance salt tolerance. The PuMPK15–PuWRKY46 module targets cell wall‐loosening gene EXPANSIN‐A11 ( PuEXPA11 ) to promote localized apoplastic acidification and relax hemicellulose cross‐linking, thereby driving root cell wall remodeling and facilitating LR development. The PuMPK15–PuWRKY46 signaling cascade regulates salt‐adaptive LR architectural plasticity in poplar. This study reveals a conditional‐plasticity regulatory mechanism and provides a valuable genetic resource for breeding salt‐tolerant woody plants.

13.5 Anthocyanins in woody ornamental plants: biosynthesis, regulatory networks, and trade-offs in stress adaptation.
Zhang H, Yao Y, Ni M … Tong Z.
reviewKinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-28
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, signaling, pathogen*, defense, salinity, drought, heavy metal*, leaf, woody
abstract

Anthocyanins are important flavonoid metabolites that are instrumental to plant adaptation to adverse environmental conditions. Increasing evidence has shown that both biotic and abiotic stresses can markedly affect anthocyanin biosynthesis and accumulation, thereby influencing plant defense capacity, stress tolerance, and metabolic reprogramming. In addition to serving as pigments, anthocyanins contribute to plant protection through multiple mechanisms, including reactive oxygen species (ROS) scavenging, metal chelation, photoprotection, and the modulation of stress-related signaling pathways. In ornamental plants, particularly woody ornamentals, anthocyanins not only determine key aesthetic traits such as flower and leaf coloration, but also serve as critical components of the stress defense system. However, compared with herbaceous model plants, the regulatory networks and functional roles of anthocyanins in these species remain insufficiently studied. This review comprehensively explores the molecular regulatory mechanisms of anthocyanin accumulation in woody plants under various stresses. These include biotic stresses (e.g., pathogenic bacteria and insect pests) and abiotic stresses (e.g., drought, salinity, extreme temperatures, heavy metals, and light stress). Furthermore, the dynamic response characteristics of anthocyanins under combined multiple stresses and their potential applications in stress-tolerance breeding are also discussed, providing a theoretical foundation for developing anthocyanin-based strategies to enhance stress tolerance in ornamental horticulture.

12.0 The cation/H+ exchanger ChCAX3 fine-tunes fruit ripening by modulating cytosolic Ca2+ homeostasis in Cerasus humilis
Shan Q, Miao C, Zhang B … Song X
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesCell biology and organellesJournal of Experimental Botany · 2026-10-13
matched: calcium, Ca2+, kinase*, signaling, ethylene, hormone*, cell wall*, vacuol*, stem
abstract

Abstract Cerasus humilis is a calcium-rich fruit crop with high nutritional value, but its synchronized ripening causes concentrated harvest and short shelf life. In this study, ChCAX3, a vacuolar cation/H+ exchanger, was identified as a key regulator of fruit ripening. ChCAX3 showed high expression in fruit and stem tissues and was upregulated by CaCl2 and ethephon treatments. however, repressed during fruit ripening by ChMYB16. Yeast complementation and Arabidopsis thaliana assays validated its Ca2+ transport activity. Overexpression of ChCAX3 in tomato increased fruit Ca2+ content, delayed coloration, reduced softening, and extended the ripening period by approximately 14 days. ChCAX3-mediated vacuolar Ca2+ sequestration may maintain low cytosolic Ca2+ levels, thereby potentially attenuate the expression and activity of a presumptive Ca2+ sensor ChCBL10 and its interacting kinase ChCIPK3 at the vacuolar membrane. This attenuation of the ChCBL10-ChCIPK3 module was accompanied by reduced expression of ethylene biosynthesis genes (ChACS1, ChACO1) and cell wall degradation-related genes (ChPG1, ChPG2), which may collectively delay the ethylene burst and preserving fruit firmness. Conversely, repression of ChCAX3 by ChMYB16 during late ripening may elevate cytosolic Ca2+, potentially activate the ChCBL10-ChCIPK3 signaling cascade, and accelerated senescence. Overall, these findings demonstrated that ChCAX3 positively regulates Ca2+ accumulation and delays fruit ripening via Ca2+ signaling-mediated modulation of ethylene pathways, providing new insights into Ca2+-hormone crosstalk in fruit physiology and identifying a potential target for extending shelf life and enhancing nutritional quality.

11.5 The Role of Endoplasmic Reticulum Stress in Extrinsic Skin Aging and Protective Strategies of Natural Plant Products.
Liu Y, Huang H, Ji X … Zhu M.
reviewKinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthSkin pharmacology and physiology · 2026-10-07
matched: Ca2+, ROS, reactive oxygen species, signaling, endoplasmic reticulum, ER, autophagy, epiderm*, development
abstract

BackgroundThe complicated process of extrinsic skin aging is brought on by long-term exposure to ultraviolet (UV) radiation, air pollution, and other environmental stressors. Its primary manifestations include deep wrinkles, skin laxity, abnormal pigmentation, epidermal barrier dysfunction, chronic inflammation, and telangiectasia. According to recent research, endoplasmic reticulum stress (ERS) is an important stress mechanism involved in extrinsic skin aging. Through the production of reactive oxygen species (ROS), disruption of Ca2+ homeostasis, and defects in protein folding, extrinsic environmental stressors can activate ERS and cause the unfolded protein response (UPR).SummaryERS-induced UPR activation initially functions as a protective mechanism that restores endoplasmic reticulum (ER) homeostasis by enhancing protein-folding capacity, promoting ER-associated degradation (ERAD), and attenuating global protein translation. However, under persistent or severe ER stress, sustained UPR signaling may shift from a homeostatic response toward a detrimental response and contribute to the development of characteristic cutaneous phenotypes of extrinsic skin aging, including wrinkles, hyperpigmentation, epidermal barrier dysfunction, chronic inflammation, and telangiectasia, through increased oxidative stress, amplification of inflammatory responses, and induction of cellular senescence and apoptosis. An increasing amount of evidence suggests that natural plant products can prevent or alleviate extrinsic skin aging by regulating ERS and related processes, including apoptosis, autophagy, and oxidative stress.Key messagesThis review summarizes the major molecular mechanisms of ERS in extrinsic skin aging and its associations with various aging phenotypes, outlining potential intervention strategies using natural plant products.

11.5 The photoperiod regulator E1 family negatively modulates salt tolerance by directly repressing GmCIPK25 expression in soybean.
Zhang M, Wu A, Deng Z … Sun Z.
Abiotic stressJournal of genetics and genomics = Yi chuan xue bao · 2026-10-12
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, hydrogen peroxide, salt sens*, salt stress, salinity, stress respon*
abstract

Soybean (Glycine max [L.] Merr.) is an important crop whose productivity is severely constrained by salinity. Here, we identify the photoperiod regulator E1 family as negative regulators of soybean salt tolerance. Under salt stress, the e1 e1la e1lb triple mutant (e1-3m) show higher antioxidant enzyme activities and proline accumulation, together with reduced hydrogen peroxide and malondialdehyde levels, whereas E1 overexpression increases salt sensitivity. Integrated RNA-seq and ChIP-seq analyses identify GmCIPK25, a positive regulator of salt tolerance, as a direct target of E1. E1, E1La, and E1Lb bind directly to the GmCIPK25 promoter and repress its transcription. Knockdown of GmCIPK25 attenuates the enhanced salt tolerance of e1-3m, while loss of GmCIPK25 function in the wild-type background increased salt sensitivity. Thus, the E1 family negatively regulates salt tolerance by repressing GmCIPK25, which compromises reactive oxygen species (ROS)-scavenging capacity and osmotic adjustment. These findings link photoperiodic regulation to salt-stress responses and identify GmCIPK25 as a potential target for improving soybean salt tolerance.

11.5 Zerumbone alleviates TRESK- and TRPV1-mediated ion currents and reduces excitability of human iPSC-derived nociceptor-like neurons.
Hoerdler N, Grell A, Chatterjee S … Dräger O.
Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthMolecular pain · 2026-01-01
matched: calcium, calcium signal*, membrane potential, depolari*, signaling, receptor*, peroxisom*, stem, stem cell*
abstract

Secondary plant metabolites are widely used in traditional and naturopathic medicine to treat pain, infection, and inflammation. The sesquiterpenoid zerumbone, derived from the edible ginger Zingiber zerumbet, has been shown to exert analgesic, antioxidant, antitumoral, and antimicrobial effects in preclinical models, acting through multiple receptor systems including cannabinoid (CB) receptors, peroxisome proliferator-activated receptors, N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptors, and the serotonergic system. Zerumbone also modulates inflammatory and nitric oxide-cGMP-K+ATP signaling pathways implicated in antiallodynic responses, yet its direct impact on nociceptor-associated ion channels like the Transient receptor potential vanilloid 1 (TRPV1) and the Twik-related spinal cord potassium channel (TRESK) in human cellular systems remains poorly characterized. In the present study, the antinociceptive properties of zerumbone were investigated in human HEK293-based and induced pluripotent stem cell (iPSC)-derived nociceptor models. Here, the zerumbone treatment reduced cell viability and proliferation in a concentration-dependent manner without altering TRPV1 or TRESK protein expression. Whole-cell patch-clamp recordings revealed that zerumbone reversibly inhibited TRESK-mediated outward potassium currents upon direct application, whereas bath-applied zerumbone increased outward currents, suggesting context-dependent modulation. In TRPV1-expressing HEK293 cells, SH-SY5Y neuroblastoma cells, and human iPSC-derived nociceptors, zerumbone reduced TRPV1-mediated ion currents and capsaicin-evoked calcium signals, indicating a direct or competitive inhibitory effect on TRPV1 activity. In iPSC-derived nociceptors, zerumbone decreased action potential firing at moderate depolarizing currents, shifted the resting membrane potential, and increased rheobase, consistent with reduced nociceptor excitability, while no significant changes in TRESK-mediated currents were detected. These data suggest that zerumbone attenuates human iPSC-derived nociceptor-like neuron excitability largely via TRPV1 inhibition, with a more complex, concentration- and application-method-dependent modulation of TRESK. The findings provide a mechanistic basis for the antiallodynic effects of zerumbone observed in rodent models and highlight the need for further studies in human-derived systems to clarify the precise channel-level interactions and their relevance to inflammatory and neuropathic pain.

11.0 A natural brake tunes early calcium signaling and salt tolerance in rice
Kobayashi Y, Fujita M, Fujita Y
Kinases and signal transductionDevelopmental Cell · 2026-10
matched: calcium, calcium signal*, signaling
11.0 Antioxidative Defence Mechanisms in Tomato Plants Induced by Biopriming with Trichoderma virens and Jasmonic Acid Against Fusarium Wilt and Damping-Off.
Sood M, Kumar V, Kukreja S.
HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthIndian journal of microbiology · 2026-08-03
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, H2O2, jasmon*, JA, pathogen*, defence, leaf, seed*
abstract

In plants, pathogenic infection causes oxidative damage to the host plants and eventually leads to the destruction of the plant's metabolism due to the oxidation of essential biomolecules. In this investigation, we monitored the effects of fusarium wilt and damping-off mediated generation of Reactive oxygen species (ROS) and their effective disposal by the antioxidative defence system in tomato plants. The main aim of the current research work was to assess the seed priming remedial effects of Trichoderma virens (Tv) (1 × 107 spores/mL) as a biocontrol agent and jasmonic acid (JA) (1 μM) as a chemical inducer against the pathogen- induced oxidative stress in diseased plants. Thirty days old primed and unprimed tomato seedlings were inoculated with Fusarium oxysporum f. sp. lycopersici (Fol) (1 × 106 spores/mL) and Rhizoctonia solani (Rs) (2% w/v) in a different set of treatments, and after thirty days of pathogen inoculation, leaf sampling was done for twelve treatments randomly to record various physiochemical parameters. A significant increase in the oxidative stress markers measured in terms of MDA (Malondialdehyde) and H2O2 contents was observed in Fol and Rs-infected tomato plants compared with non-inoculated ones. Moreover, pre-treatment of tomato seeds with ameliorative agents lowered the extent of oxidative damage in the case of both pathogens. Consequently, to combat these ROS in comparison to the control, a marked enhancement in the antioxidative defence system was observed, as evidenced by increased ascorbic acid (AsA), tocopherol, glutathione (GSH), phenolic, and flavonoid contents, and hence, free radical scavenging activity. This physicochemical analysis further revealed that seed priming with T. virens and JA in combination in pathogenically stressed plants in the treatments, T8 (Fol + Tv + JA) and T12 (Rs + Tv + JA) has the most remedial influences when compared with non- primed pathogen-inoculated plants under the treatments, T5 (Fol) and T9 (Rs). These findings support the priming-induced disease suppressive influences of T. virens and JA on tomato plants through the enhanced activation of antioxidative defence mechanisms against Fusarium wilt and damping off diseases.

10.0 Histone deacetylase complex 1 modulates sepal length through the ethylene-ROS module.

matched: ROS, phosphatase*, signaling, ethylene, cell elongation, growth, development
abstract

Key messageHDC1 represses the expression of protein phosphatase genes to promote the activity of key ethylene biosynthetic enzymes, which in turn enhances ROS accumulation during sepal maturation, ultimately influencing sepal length. Organ size is precisely regulated during development to ensure proper function. The sepal must maintain an appropriate size throughout its growth to protect the developing flower bud until blooming. 9.5 Combined application of melatonin and salicylic acid improves drought tolerance in pepper (Capsicum annuum) through coordinated physiological and biochemical responses.
Köklü Ardıç Ş.
HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressFunctional plant biology : FPB · 2026-10-01
matched: reactive oxygen species, hydrogen peroxide, salicylic acid, defence, drought, growth
abstract

Drought stress is a major environmental constraint limiting growth, water relations, and metabolic stability in pepper (Capsicum annuum). This study evaluated the individual and combined effects of melatonin (MEL) and salicylic acid (SA) on drought tolerance by assessing growth, water status, photosynthetic pigments, oxidative stress markers, osmotic adjustment, and antioxidant defence systems. Plants were grown under non-stressed and drought-stressed conditions and treated with MEL, SA, or their combination. Drought stress significantly reduced fresh weight, relative water content, and photosynthetic pigments, while increasing accumulation of hydrogen peroxide and malondialdehyde, indicating severe oxidative damage. These findings demonstrate that the greater drought tolerance conferred by the combined treatment is associated with coordinated regulation of water balance, redox homeostasis, osmotic adjustment, and photosynthetic stability. 9.0 Cross-species transcriptomics uncovers shared responses to water deficiency in plants.

abstract

Deepening our knowledge of conserved molecular mechanisms governing stress responses in plants is fundamental for identifying new core genes and processes involved in tolerance to adverse environmental conditions. For this purpose, we developed a bioinformatic pipeline to retrieve and integrate stress-related RNA-seq transcriptomic data from species of interest and identify core species-specific and cross-species conserved genes and processes involved in stress responses. In conclusion, the developed approach proved effective in identifying core drought-responsive genes and processes and has the potential to be applied to other plant species and types of stress.

9.0 Genome-wide identification and expression profiling of the calmodulin-binding transcription activator gene family in apple (Malus × domestica) under biotic and abiotic stress conditions

abstract

Abstract Calmodulin-binding transcription activators (CAMTAs) are pivotal regulators that integrate calcium signaling with plant developmental and stress-response programs. This study identified eight MdCAMTA genes in the apple ( Malus domestica ) genome, which encode 13 isoforms through alternative splicing. Phylogenetic analysis indicated that MdCAMTA proteins cluster into two ancient clades, with a divergence predating the speciation of dicots. 9.0 Phosphorylation and epigenetic remodeling mediate BpEBP1 ‐dependent drought resistance in birch
Sun S, Wang P, Yang X … Wang Y
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityNew Phytologist · 2026-10-16
matched: reactive oxygen species, phosphoryl*, ethylene, immun*, drought, cell wall*
abstract

Summary BpEBP1, an ethylene‐responsive factor VII transcription factor, coordinates stress resilience in birch ( Betula platyphylla ), but its molecular mechanisms underlying drought response remain unclear. Using transgenic birch lines (overexpression and knockout), chromatin immunoprecipitation (ChIP)‐seq, biochemical assays, and protein interaction studies, we dissected the multilayered regulatory network of BpEBP1. BpEBP1 enhances drought tolerance in birch by directly activating key lignin and anthocyanin biosynthesis genes ( BpMYC2 , BpCOMT , and BpPOD5 ), thereby promoting lignin deposition and anthocyanin accumulation, which reinforce cell wall rigidity and boost reactive oxygen species scavenging capacity. Beyond the canonical GCC‐box cis ‐element, BpEBP1 binds to novel Noyes and ZNF449 motifs and uses these cis ‐elements to drive expression of the previously described target genes. BpEBP1 functions as a central regulatory hub that unites transcriptional activation of anthocyanin and lignin‐related genes, BpJMJ25‐mediated H3K9me2 epigenetic remodeling, and BpEDR1‐dependent Thr148 phosphorylation. These two regulatory layers act independently yet synergistically, collectively activating drought‐adaptive metabolic pathways and conferring robust drought resistance in birch.

9.0 The Arabidopsis TELLURITE RESISTANCE C is required for the functionality of both photosystems by supplying manganese and calcium.

matched: calcium, Ca2+, chloroplast*, seed*, growth
abstract

The oxygen-evolving complex (OEC) of photosystem II (PSII) contains a Mn4CaO5 cluster that catalyzes the light-driven oxidation of water. In Arabidopsis thaliana, optimal PSII function requires the thylakoid membrane-localized manganese transporter PHOTOSYNTHESIS AFFECTED MUTANT 71 (PAM71). 8.5 Mechanistic insights into the potential role of botanical drugs and plant-derived metabolites in mitigating oxidative stress in varicocele.
Wang A, Colonnello E, Ma D … Wang F.
reviewKinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressFrontiers in pharmacology · 2026-09-28
matched: ROS, MAPK*, signaling, pathogen*, hypoxia, stress respon*, ER, mitochondri*
abstract

Varicocele (VC) is a prevalent male urogenital disorder associated with scrotal discomfort, impaired semen quality, and endocrine dysregulation, in which oxidative stress (OS) is repeatedly implicated as a key pathogenic axis linking venous stasis, hypoxia, hyperthermia, inflammation, mitochondrial dysfunction, and germ-cell injury. This review critically evaluates experimental and clinical evidence on defined botanical drugs and plant-derived metabolites investigated in VC or VC-relevant OS models, with an emphasis on mechanistic plausibility and translational limitations. Across in vivo VC models, toxin-/heat-/hypoxia-induced spermatogenic injury models, and selected clinical studies, multiple interventions have been reported to improve semen parameters, testicular histology, and redox indices (e.g., ROS, MDA, SOD/CAT/GPx, GSH/GSSG), alongside modulation of apoptosis and stress responses. Reported molecular nodes converge on redox- and stress-responsive pathways, including Keap1-Nrf2-ARE/HO-1, PI3K-Akt, MAPK/JNK-p38, HIF-1-related signaling, ER-stress responses, mitochondrial apoptosis (e.g., Bax/Bcl-2/caspases), and, in some models, ferroptosis-related regulation. However, the overall evidence base is constrained by heterogeneity in models and endpoints, limited extract/compound chemical characterization and pharmacological standardization, frequent reliance on in silico predictions, small or non-randomized clinical cohorts, and incomplete reporting of taxonomic and product quality details. Future work should prioritize rigorously designed, adequately powered clinical trials with standardized outcomes, well-defined interventions, and mechanism-aligned biomarkers to clarify which botanical drugs/metabolites offer clinically meaningful benefit as adjuncts in VC management.

8.0 Molecular Mechanisms of Plant Secondary Metabolites in Modulation of ROS-mediated Microglial Activation and Neuroinflammation: Special Emphasize to Quercetin.
Sahakyan N, Minasyan A, Cherkaoui-Malki M, Andreoletti P.
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesBiochimie · 2026-10-14
matched: ROS, MAPK*, signaling, immun*, pathogen*, mitochondri*, peroxisom*, organelle*
abstract

Neuroinflammation is a central pathogenic mechanism in numerous neurological disorders, with microglia playing a pivotal role in coordinating immune responses through tightly regulated redox, metabolic, and inflammatory signaling pathways. While transient microglial activation supports tissue homeostasis and repair, persistent activation promotes excessive production of reactive oxygen and nitrogen species (ROS/RNS), pro-inflammatory mediators, and lipid-derived inflammatory signals, driving chronic neuroinflammation and neurodegeneration. Accordingly, modulation of microglial activation has emerged as an attractive therapeutic strategy. Plant-derived extracts and phytochemicals represent a remarkable source of bioactive molecules such as quercetin, which are able to concomitantly modulate multiple pathways involved in microglial activation. Over the past two decades, numerous studies have investigated their effects using immortalized murine BV-2 cells and, to a lesser extent, primary microglia. Despite differences between these experimental models, current evidence reveals a striking convergence of molecular mechanisms. Plant-derived metabolites consistently attenuate microglial activation through inhibition of the NF-κB and MAPK signaling pathways, activation of the Nrf2/HO-1 antioxidant axis, suppression of inducible nitric oxide synthase (iNOS), cyclooxygenase-2 (COX-2), and pro-inflammatory cytokine production, while limiting oxidative stress and lipid peroxidation. Emerging evidence further indicates that these compounds regulate microglial phenotypic plasticity, mitochondrial function, immunometabolic reprogramming, and, potentially, peroxisomal metabolism and mitochondria-peroxisome crosstalk, thereby extending their actions well beyond conventional antioxidant activity. This review critically examines the molecular mechanisms by which plant-derived extracts and phytochemicals regulate microglial function, with particular emphasis on the interconnected networks linking redox homeostasis, inflammatory signaling, lipid metabolism, and organelle communication.

8.0 Plasma membrane proteomics reveals mechanism of low potassium tolerance in two contrasting maize genotypes.
Zhong M, Chen S, Yang X … Garcia-Caparros P.
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-24
matched: Ca2+, signal transduction, signaling, defense, stress respon*, plasma membrane, root, roots, growth
abstract

Potassium (K) deficiency is a major constraint on plant growth and crop productivity. To elucidate the genotypic mechanisms underlying maize adaptation to K deficiency, we comprehensively compared the physiological responses and 8-plex iTRAQ (4 biological replicates) based plasma membrane (PM) proteomes of two maize genotypes with contrasting K efficiency. Under low-K conditions, the K-efficient genotype (K-ef) maintained greater growth and root vitality and exhibited significantly higher K concentration and accumulation than the K inefficient genotype (K-inef). These DAPs were involved in transport, signal transduction, lipid metabolism, cellular structure, and redox/defense. Integrating the physiological and proteomic data revealed that K-ef may adopt an "energy-saving" strategy by prioritizing K+ transport, redox defense, and Ca2+ signaling, whereas K-inef falls into an energy-wasting strategy and indiscriminate stress response. Our study provides valuable insights into physiological and molecular mechanisms underlying low K stress in maize roots and providing a basis to further study K nutrition in other crop species.

7.5 Catabolism of 4-aminobutyrate (GABA) and proteinogenic amino acids sustains the tricarboxylic acid cycle in Arabidopsis thaliana (L.) Heynh. plants under salinity stress.
Flaherty EJ, Hoover GJ, Shelp BJ.
Abiotic stressGrowthPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-05
matched: calcium, calmodulin*, salinity, roots, shoot*
abstract

Glutamate decarboxylases1,2,4 (GAD1,2,4) are calcium/calmodulin-activated enzymes that produce 4-aminobutyrate (GABA) and eventually lead to succinate (GABA shunt), which can theoretically be incorporated into the tricarboxylic acid cycle (TCAC) during abiotic stress. We tested the hypothesis that the GABA shunt is involved in the adaptive response of the TCAC to salinity stress. Quantitative analyses of amino and organic acids, as well as GAD1,2,4 expression, in shoots and roots of soil-grown, late-vegetative-stage Arabidopsis wild-type plants and GAD1,2,4 single, double and triple loss-of function mutants provided a general understanding of the metabolic response to salinity. Salinity induced similar metabolic changes in shoots and roots, though the magnitude of the response was greater in shoots. Several mechanisms could be responsible for the non-cyclic nature of the TCAC with salinity, but inhibition of 2-oxoglutarate dehydrogenase probably resulted in the diversion of 2-oxoglutarate from the TCAC to the GABA shunt, though activation of this pathway was insufficient to sustain the succinate level observed in the absence of salinity, especially in roots. Both transcriptional and post-transcriptional regulation of GAD could have contributed to activation of the GABA shunt. Inhibition of GAD-mediated GABA production by genetic means was associated with changes in levels of free proteinogenic amino acids, as well as elevated succinate, regardless of salinity stress. Overall, GABA and proteinogenic amino acids serve an important role in ensuring flexible generation of respiratory substrate for TCAC operation and energy production.

7.5 Interfacial Electron-Proton Dual Modulation of Fe Single-Atom Catalyst by α-MoC for O2 Activation and Lignin Depolymerization.
Shi R, Wu J, Song B … Wu Y.
Trees and woody plantsAngewandte Chemie (International ed. in English) · 2026-10-07
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, birch, lignin*
abstract

Efficient O2 activation under mild conditions is central to oxidative biomass valorization, yet single-atom catalysts often suffer from sluggish proton-coupled oxygen intermediate conversion, particularly because of overly strong *OH adsorption. Inspired by the cooperative electron and proton management in cytochrome c oxidase, we report an α-MoC/Fe-N4 catalyst in which α-MoC nanoparticles are interfaced with Fe-N4 single-atom sites on a nitrogen-doped carbon scaffold. The α-MoC component plays a dual role: it withdraws electron density from Fe sites to weaken oxygen-intermediate adsorption and promotes water dissociation to provide local protons for *OH-to-H2O conversion. As a result, α-MoC/Fe-N4 exhibits a 2.8-fold higher Vmax for O2 activation than Fe-N4 and proceeds through a nearly four-electron oxygen reduction pathway with negligible reactive oxygen species (ROS) generation. This interfacial strategy is generalizable to various M-N-C single-atom catalysts, including Mn, Co, Ni, Cu, Sn, Ir, and Pt. Enabled by efficient O2 activation, α-MoC/Fe-N4 depolymerizes native birch lignin under mild alkaline conditions, substantially decreasing its molecular weight and cleaving β-O-4, β-β, and β-5 linkages. This work establishes an electron-proton dual-modulation strategy for constructing high-performance single-atom catalysts for biomass conversion.

7.5 Transcriptional and post-transcriptional regulation of OsNHX2 encoding a tonoplast-localized transporter controls intracellular K+ accumulation to regulate cold tolerance in rice.

matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, signaling, cold stress, tonoplast, shoot*, seed*
abstract

Low-temperature stress significantly reduces rice yield and grain quality. Identifying major cold-tolerance genes and deciphering their regulatory networks is therefore essential for rice molecular breeding for cold tolerance. Here, through a genome-wide association study (GWAS) in a multi-parent advanced generation intercross (MAGIC) population, we identified OsNHX2, a tonoplast-localized K+ transporter that enhances cold tolerance at the seedling stage. 7.0 Catalase import into peroxisomes in Arabidopsis
Hu, Z., Tremulot … R.
preprintCell biology and organellesbioRxiv · 2026-10-13
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, peroxisom*
abstract

Summary In eukaryotes, catalases are peroxisomal antioxidant enzymes that maintain cellular reactive oxygen species (ROS) homeostasis. Plant catalases lack canonical peroxisomal targeting signals (PTS), and the mechanism underlying their PEX5-dependent peroxisomal import remains poorly understood. Here, we expressed CAT2 with N- and C-terminal GFP fusions in an Arabidopsis cat2 mutant background and found that only GFP-CAT2 completely restored wild-type function despite comparable catalase activity for both CAT2-GFP and GFP-CAT2. Only GFP-CAT2 localized to peroxisomes, indicating that altered subcellular localization prevented CAT2-GFP functional complementation. These findings define a minimal C-terminal targeting motif for plant catalases that is sufficient for their peroxisomal import.

7.0 Photooxidation in interveinal tissues of magnesium-deficient leaves is associated with sugar accumulation.
Ye X, Xu K, Yang C … Lu J.
GrowthPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-09
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, leaf, leaves, phloem, growth, development
abstract

Magnesium (Mg) deficiency often leads to leaf photooxidation, typically manifested as interveinal chlorosis. This symptom arises from an imbalance between light energy absorption and its utilization, resulting in excessive excitation energy, reactive oxygen species (ROS) accumulation and subsequent photodamage. However, the spatial heterogeneity of Mg deficiency responses within a leaf remains poorly understood, particularly the physiological basis for the greater severity of photooxidation in interveinal tissues compared with vein-adjacent areas. Here, a hydroponic culture system was employed to investigate tissue-specific responses of rapeseed (Brassica napus L.) leaves to varying Mg supply levels. Mg deficiency significantly inhibited leaf growth, with symptoms increasing as leaves aged. Interveinal tissues exhibited pronounced chlorosis, elevated ROS accumulation, and a sharp decline in photosynthetic capacity relative to vein-adjacent tissues. Despite comparable Mg concentrations between the two leaf regions, interveinal tissues accumulated significantly higher levels of soluble sugars under Mg deficiency, suggesting that localized carbohydrate accumulation may contribute to their greater susceptibility to oxidative stress. To further test this hypothesis, a complementary experiment showed that foliar application of fructose markedly aggravated chlorosis in Mg-deficient leaves, providing direct evidence that leaf yellowing is closely associated with carbohydrate accumulation. To elucidate the underlying mechanisms, further analyses revealed that Mg deficiency suppressed the expression of sugar transporter genes, reduced Mg2+-ATPase activity, and decreased vascular bundle area, with these effects being particularly pronounced in interveinal tissues. These changes likely impair phloem loading and carbohydrate translocation, leading to localized sugar retention in interveinal regions. Collectively, our results indicate that Mg deficiency disrupts sugar transport and vascular function, resulting in carbohydrate accumulation that intensifies photooxidative stress and ultimately drives the development of interveinal chlorosis.

6.5 Convergent evolution of an oxidative damage tolerance mechanism preserves replication fidelity in plant organellar DNA polymerases
Molina-Jiménez M, Baruch-Torres N, Díaz-Quezada C, Brieba LG
Cell biology and organellesPlant Physiology · 2026-10
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, chloroplast*, mitochondri*
abstract

Abstract Plant mitochondria and chloroplasts are sources of reactive oxygen species (ROS) that trigger oxidative DNA lesions. Among these, 8-oxo-7,8-dihydroguanine (8-oxodG) is particularly mutagenic because it exhibits ambiguous base pairing, as DNA polymerases efficiently incorporate adenine opposite 8-oxodG, leading to G·C→T·A transversions. Despite exposure to oxidative stress, plant organellar genomes exhibit some of the lowest nucleotide substitution rates in nature, implying that their DNA replication machinery may have evolved mechanisms to cope with oxidative lesions such as 8-oxodG. In family-A DNA polymerases, including yeast and animal mitochondrial DNA polymerases, the balance between faithful dCMP incorporation and mutagenic dAMP incorporation opposite 8-oxodG is governed by a bulky or positively charged residue. Surprisingly, although the equivalent position in the Arabidopsis thaliana organellar DNA polymerases AtPolIA and AtPolIB is occupied by glycine—a residue predicted to promote mutagenic dAMP incorporation—both enzymes nevertheless preferentially incorporate accurate dCMP opposite 8-oxodG. Our structure–function analyses demonstrate that AtPolI´s fidelity opposite 8-oxodG is conferred by a lysine located near the templating base, which generates steric and electrostatic constraints that favor the 8-oxodG(anti):dCTP pairing while disfavoring the mutagenic 8-oxodG(syn):dATP pair. This mechanism represents an example of convergent evolution, as the fidelity-determining lysine in plant organellar polymerases is in a different position than the analogous residues in yeast-animal mitochondrial polymerases but achieves the same function. Together, these findings suggest that plant organellar DNA polymerases have evolved specialized properties that promote accurate bypass of oxidative DNA lesions, which may contribute to the maintenance of the exceptionally low mutation rates observed in plant organellar genomes.

6.5 Decoding ferroptosis via integrated multi-omics analyses in rice blast.
Liang Z, Shen Q, Nguyen NK … Deng YZ.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthiMeta · 2026-10-07
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, immun*, pathogen*, development
abstract

The rice-Magnaporthe oryzae pathosystem is presented as a host-pathogen ferroptosis model. Multi-omics insights connect ferroptosis to several cellular processes, such as iron homeostasis, reactive oxygen species (ROS)/redox imbalance, and polyunsaturated fatty acid-containing phospholipid (PUFA-PL) peroxidation, under multiple regulators in rice and M. oryzae, respectively. Interestingly, ferroptosis drives two opposite biological outcomes: fungal infection-related development and rice immunity.

6.5 Fusaric acid as a central hub of virulence: Reprogramming host physiology during Fusarium infection.
Zhang J, He D, Wang H, Yan Z.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityMicrobiological research · 2026-10-05
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, signalling, hormone*, pathogen*, stress respon*, plasma membrane, chloroplast*, mitochondri*
abstract

Fusarium spp. include major soilborne pathogens that cause vascular wilt diseases in diverse crops. Fusaric acid (FA) is a non-host-specific toxin produced by some Fusarium species and contributes to virulence in several, but not all, host-pathogen systems. Traditionally, FA has been regarded as a broad-spectrum phytotoxin. However, emerging evidence indicates that FA acts beyond direct cellular damage, promoting pathogen colonization through the systematic reprogramming of host physiological processes. Herein, we proposed a pathogenic model with three interacting layers: (i) perturbation of plasma membrane function and ion homeostasis, including effects on H+-ATPase activity and ion transport; (ii) amplification through reactive oxygen species (ROS), lipid peroxidation and mitochondrial and chloroplast dysfunction; and (iii) modulation of hormone-redox signalling and programmed cell death (PCD). The strength and sequence of these responses depend on FA concentration, exposure time, host genotype and pathosystem. FA biosynthesis is controlled primarily by the FUB gene cluster and by broader transcriptional, nutritional and stress-responsive networks, but the relationship between FA production and virulence is context dependent. This review integrates current evidence on FA biosynthesis, stage-associated functions during infection, and cellular toxicity. Building upon these mechanistic insights, we discuss emerging mitigation strategies, including microbial degradation, targeted inhibition of FA biosynthesis, enhancement of host resistance, and metal ion antagonism. Collectively, this work establishes a conceptual framework for understanding Fusarium pathogenesis and provides a foundation for developing sustainable strategies for disease management.

6.5 High-Value Utilization of Phosphate Tailings: Phosphorus Recovery and In-Situ Synthesis of Layered Double Hydroxides for Stable Immobilization of Heavy Metals.
Du J, Dong B, Ning P, Xu Z.
Abiotic stressGrowthEnvironmental research · 2026-10-06
matched: calcium, Ca2+, heavy metal*, cadmium, growth
abstract

High-value utilization of phosphate tailings (PT) is of great significance for addressing environmental issues while advancing circular economy goals. This study proposed an integrated acid leaching-precipitation-synthesis strategy for high-value utilization of PT. After recovering phosphorus (P) via acid leaching and precipitation, a functional layered double hydroxide (PLDH) was successfully synthesized by recycling the remaining leachate. Results showed that the leaching efficiencies of P, calcium (Ca), and magnesium (Mg) reached approximately 100% at 25 °C using 0.4 M HCl at a liquid-to-solid ratio of 50 mL/g for 60 min. The recovered P primarily occurred as hydroxyapatite, with P2O5 content as high as 31.2%. The synthesized PLDH exhibited excellent mineralization performance toward cadmium, copper, zinc, and lead ions (Cd2+, Cu2+, Zn2+, and Pb2+, respectively) in aqueous solutions, with maximum capacities of 561.28, 349.65, 340.14, and 775.54 mg/g, respectively, surpassing those of most reported sorbents. Mechanistic investigations revealed that Cd2+, Cu2+, and Zn2+ were mineralized through isomorphic substitution at Ca2+ sites within the PLDH lattice, whereas Pb2+ was mineralized through precipitation to form Pb3(CO3)2(OH)2. An exploratory soil column experiment further suggested that PLDH could effectively limit heavy metal migration under soil-column conditions. After 42 d of soil remediation, 4% PLDH reduced heavy-metal bioavailability by 59.9-77.3% and TCLP-leachable concentrations by 49.5-83.6%. Wheat growth experiments further showed no apparent phytotoxicity of 4% PLDH under the tested conditions. Lastly, techno-economic analysis indicated the economic potential of the proposed strategy, providing strong support for industrial applications.

6.5 Infection mechanisms of Rhizoctonia cerealis in wheat.
Ren Y, Liu T, Zhao H … Chen F.
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityiMeta · 2026-10-05
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, ubiquitin*, effector*, pathogen*
abstract

Rhizoctonia cerealis (Rc) as a major pathogen causing sharp eyespot (SE) seriously affects wheat production worldwide. We collected 1683 wheat plants exhibiting SE symptoms from 30 locations in China over 7 years. We next constructed high-quality genome maps by de novo sequencing of five representative Rc isolates and established an atlas of genomic variation by resequencing 207 isolates. We identified a key effector, RcPL3, conferring Rc pathogenicity. RcPL3 increased the protein accumulation of histone deacetylase TaHDA9 in wheat cytoplasm to promote Rc infection. TaHDA9 inhibited the E3 ubiquitin ligase TaRHC1A through deacetylation. RcPL3 indirectly inhibited the accumulation of TaRHC1A by mediating TaHDA9. TaRHC1A promoted the degradation of TaRBOH3 synthesizing reactive oxygen species (ROS) to cause wheat SE resistance. We further discovered an antagonist HY-19741 to prevent Rc infection. The high-quality genome maps of Rc and a SE RcPL3-TaHDA9-TaRHC1A-TaRBOH3 regulatory cascade will be valuable for SE prevention in wheat.

6.5 Research progress and prospect of β-carotene hydroxylase gene mediating plant carotenoid metabolism in response to abiotic stress.
Gao Y, Zhang L, Rui W … Xie J.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-05
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, phosphoryl*, ubiquitin*, ABA
abstract

Carotenoid metabolism plays a significant role in plants response to abiotic stress. The β-carotene hydroxylase gene (BCH) is an important gene located downstream of the carotenoid metabolic pathway. Its main function is to hydroxylate β-carotene to form zeaxanthin, which is the direct valve regulating the carotenoid xanthophyll cycle and also an important controller in the upstream of ABA synthesis. Most carotene components contain a molecular structure with conjugated double bonds, which determines that they have a powerful antioxidant function. When the BCH gene is overexpressed, Reactive oxygen species (ROS) can be quenched, Non-photochemical quenching (NPQ) is elevated, the excess oxidation energy is released in the form of heat, thereby playing a role in protecting plant tissues and cells. At the transcriptional regulation level, a large number of positive and reverse regulatory transcription factors have been screened out to regulate the BCH gene. In addition, miRNA directly or indirectly regulates the BCH gene through transcription factors. However, there are few reports on the research of the BCH gene at the level of protein translation modification. In summary, this article reviews the functional research, transcriptional regulation and miRNA regulation progress of the BCH gene, and at the same time makes prospects at the protein translation modification level (ubiquitination and phosphorylation), with the aim of laying a foundation for in-depth exploration of the BCH gene's function at the epigenetic level.

6.0 A defined isothiocyanate mixture suppresses platelet signaling and metabolism to limit thrombosis in vivo.
Buchholz JY, Shoykhet M, Darius AC … Wollenberg B.
Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesMethodsFrontiers in immunology · 2026-09-30
matched: calcium, phosphoryl*, signaling, mitochondri*, microscop*
abstract

Pathological platelet activation drives thrombosis and is a major contributor to morbidity and mortality. Current antiplatelet therapies reduce thrombotic risk but target a limited range of signaling pathways, have minimal impact on established platelet aggregates, and are frequently associated with bleeding complications. These drawbacks have prompted interest in alternative antiplatelet strategies. Isothiocyanates (ITCs), phytochemicals from cruciferous plants, have been reported to inhibit platelet function; however, their mechanisms and translational relevance remain incompletely defined. The effects of a defined mixture of allyl-, benzyl-, and phenethyl-isothiocyanate on platelet function were evaluated using ex vivo human platelets and an in vivo murine model of carrageenan-induced thrombosis. Platelet aggregation, intracellular signaling, calcium mobilization, redox balance, and mitochondrial function were assessed using functional assays, kinome profiling, flow cytometry, respirometry, and electron microscopy. The ITC mixture was administered before or after the carrageenan challenge. The ITC mixture inhibited platelet activation and aggregation induced by multiple agonists. It reduced peptide phosphorylation across multiple platelet signaling pathways, reduced intracellular calcium mobilization and the activation-associated oxidant signal, and impaired mitochondrial function. When the ITC mixture was added shortly after activation, aggregometry signals consistent with destabilization of early platelet aggregates were observed whereas granule secretion was irreversible once initiated. In vivo, ITC mixture administration before or after the carrageenan challenge reduced thrombus formation and tissue injury, prolonged bleeding times, and reduced tail-necrosis length to a similar extent as the heparin reference group in this model. These findings identify the ITC mixture as a modulator of platelet signaling and metabolism, provide mechanistic insight into ITC-mediated antithrombotic effects, and support further evaluation of ITC-based interventions in thrombotic disease.

6.0 Ecophysiology of soursop seedlings fertilized with calcium silicate under different irrigation frequencies
Amorim PEC, Mendonça V, Reis Td … Sá FVdS.
preprintGrowthTrees and woody plantsResearch Square · 2026-10-14
matched: calcium, leaf, leaves, stoma*, seed*, growth, development, tree
abstract

Abstract Propose: The soursop tree ( Annona muricata L.) is a tropical fruit tree cultivated in the semi-arid region of Brazil; however, it is sensitive to water deficit. The application of calcium silicate can promote plant growth and development under water stress. Therefore, the objective was to evaluate the ecophysiological and biochemical changes in soursop seedlings fertilized with calcium silicate under different irrigation frequencies. Methods: The experiment was conducted in a greenhouse using a randomized block design with a 4 × 2 factorial scheme and four replications. The treatments consisted of four irrigation frequencies: TR1 – daily irrigation; TR2 – irrigation every two days; TR3 – irrigation every four days; TR4 – irrigation every six days, combined with the presence or absence of calcium silicate (CaSiO₃), applied via soil, at a dose of 3.5 g plant⁻¹. Results: Soursop seedlings subjected to TR3 associated with CaSiO₃ showed improved gas exchange, including transpiration, stomatal conductance, and photosynthetic rate. Fertilization with CaSiO₃ regulated proline synthesis, increased leaf biomass and silicon and calcium content in leaf tissues, conferring greater tolerance to water deficit. Treatments TR2 and TR3 were similar to TR1 in plant height, number of leaves, leaf area, biomass accumulation, chlorophyll a fluorescence, chlorophyll content, and total soluble sugars, whereas TR4 significantly reduced these variables, regardless of CaSiO₃ fertilization. Conclusions: The application of CaSiO₃ under moderate stress (TR3) increases soursop seedlings' tolerance to deficit irrigation regimes.

matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, heat stress, stress respon*, growth
abstract

Heat stress (HS), exacerbated by global climate change, severely limits crop productivity and quality. Although γ-aminobutyric acid (GABA) can alleviate heat stress, the mechanisms by which it maintains crop quality remain largely unclear. In this study, using pea (Pisum sativum L.), we demonstrated that exogenous GABA pretreatment significantly alleviated HS-induced inhibition of photosynthesis and growth, while effectively improving nutritional and edible quality by attenuating HS-mediated cellular damage. matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, pathogen*, nucleus, nuclear, development
abstract

In plant-pathogen interaction, plants trigger reactive oxygen species (ROS) bursts to defend against pathogen invasion, while pathogens evolved antioxidant systems to scavenge ROS for infection. Our study reveals a regulatory mechanism of ROS adaptation and scavenging in the rice blast fungus Magnaporthe oryzae. We found that KatG1, a bifunctional catalase-peroxidase, acts as a ROS-responsive regulatory enzyme. 18.5 Receptor-like kinase RLK15 binds a fungal RNase effector to enhance autophosphorylation and activate host defense
Li, L., Lian … C.

abstract

Fungal pathogens secrete diverse effectors to suppress host immunity, yet the plant receptors that perceive these virulence factors often remain elusive. 17.5 SRF6, a heat-inducible receptor-like kinase, regulates thermotolerance in Arabidopsis thaliana.
Chang HY, Lai CC, Huang JC … Jinn TL.

abstract

Key messageSRF6 is a heat-inducible, non-RD receptor-like kinase that contributes to thermotolerance in Arabidopsis by modulating heat-, ER stress-, ABA-, and ROS-responsive pathways. Receptor-like kinases (RLKs) are central regulators of plant growth, development, and stress perception, yet their roles in heat stress (HS) responses remain largely unclear. 14.5 SRF3 couples cellular iron homeostasis and defense responses through ubiquitin-dependent nano-organization.
Platre MP, Brent L, Gleason MF … Busch W.
Ca2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressScience advances · 2026-10-14
matched: reactive oxygen species, kinase*, receptor kinase*, signaling, receptor*, ubiquitin*, immun*, pathogen*, defense, nutrient*, plasma membrane, endocytosis
abstract

Iron homeostasis is a central component of host defense responses. In mammals, iron availability is rapidly adjusted through receptor mediated pathways, whereas it remains unclear how plants regulate iron homeostasis during the earliest stages of immune activation. Here, we identify a rapid iron regulatory mechanism centered on the plasma membrane receptor kinase STRUBBELIG RECEPTOR FAMILY 3 (SRF3), which integrates bacterial immune signaling with ferrous iron homeostasis. Perception of flagellin triggers a burst of reactive oxygen species that induces a rapid and transient depletion of the labile ferrous iron pool. This state promotes SRF3 ubiquitination and clathrin-dependent endocytosis, a process required to restore intracellular ferrous iron levels while dampening immune responses. Using super resolution microscopy, we show that this functional integration relies on ubiquitin dependent nano organization of SRF3 at the plasma membrane, enabling distinct signaling outputs from a single receptor. Thereby, SRF3 modulates both immune signaling and the proliferation of commensal and pathogenic bacteria. Together, our findings uncover a fast-acting iron homeostasis pathway in plants that dynamically couples nutrient availability with immunity, thereby shaping host microbe interactions at the onset of infection.

12.0 RALF peptides regulate herbivore-induced defense responses in Arabidopsis leaves.
Desaki Y, Ninomiya N, Yamamura A … Arimura GI.

matched: kinase*, receptor kinase*, signaling, receptor*, RALF*, FERONIA, defense, leaf, leaves
abstract

Plants employ small secreted peptides to mediate intercellular and intertissue communication. Among these peptides, rapid alkalinization factors (RALFs) participate in diverse signaling pathways, yet their roles in herbivore-induced defense responses remain poorly understood. In this study, we investigated the roles of RALF peptides in Arabidopsis thaliana leaves damaged by Spodoptera litura larvae.
