11.5 Whole-genome duplication reshapes gene–miRNA regulatory networks underlying improved salt stress tolerance in Medicago sativa
Santoro DF, Del Regno C, Anderson AW … Rosellini D
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressGrowthPlant Physiology · 2026-10-12
matched: kinase*, receptor-like kinase*, phosphoryl*, signaling, receptor*, ubiquitin*, defense, salt stress, salinity, stoma*, shoot*
abstract

Abstract Whole-genome duplication (WGD) is a recurrent feature of plant evolution and crop improvement, yet its direct contribution to abiotic stress tolerance remains difficult to disentangle from hybridization effects. Using full-sibling diploid (2x) and neotetraploid (4x) Medicago sativa genotypes generated through bilateral sexual polyploidization, we investigated how WGD alone reshapes physiological performance and gene–miRNA regulatory networks under salt stress. Neotetraploid plants displayed enhanced tolerance to salinity, maintaining higher chlorophyll content, stomatal conductance, and shoot integrity, alongside reduced visual damage compared with diploid siblings. Transcriptome profiling revealed that ploidy level, rather than salt treatment per se, was the dominant driver of gene expression variation. Indeed, WGD resulted in extensive and largely constitutive transcriptional reprogramming, with more than 2,000 differentially expressed genes (DEGs) distinguishing 4x from 2x plants under both control and saline conditions. By contrast, under salt stress, 705 DEGs specifically differentiated tetraploids from diploids and were enriched in receptor-like kinases, phosphorylation pathways, ubiquitin-mediated proteolysis, flavonoid biosynthesis, and WRKY- and MYB-centered regulatory modules. Co-expression network analysis identified salt-associated modules linking signaling and antioxidant pathways with stress-related physiological traits. Small RNA sequencing further showed that WGD reshapes post-transcriptional regulation, with limited salt-induced miRNA changes but the emergence of ploidy-dependent miRNA hubs, including miR2593e and a novel miRNA, targeting signaling and defense-related genes. We hypothesize that WGD establishes a stress-ready regulatory architecture in which coordinated transcriptional and post-transcriptional network rebalancing acts as a buffering system that improves alfalfa physiological performance under salinity, without strong induction of canonical salt-responsive genes.

10.0 A pair of chitosan receptors confer broad-spectrum fungal resistance
Huang K, Wu J, Cao Y … Wang Y
Ca2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesScience · 2026-10-15
matched: apoplast*, kinase*, receptor-like kinase*, phosphoryl*, receptor*, immun*, pathogen*, cell wall*, plasma membrane, growth
abstract

Chitosan, a major fungal cell wall component, activates plant immunity, yet the underlying perception mechanism remains unknown. In this study, we identified OsFID1, an apoplast-localized DUF26 domain protein, as a chitosan-binding receptor essential for chitosan-triggered immunity. OsFID1 forms a receptor complex with OsFID2, a plasma membrane–localized DUF26 receptor-like kinase, to mediate chitosan sensing and immune activation, requiring OsFID2 phosphorylation for function. The OsFID1-OsFID2 complex confers broad-spectrum resistance to fungal pathogens. OsFID1 homologs are conserved in land plants, which show chitosan-binding activity in both monocots and dicots. Ectopic expression of OsFID1 in wheat and maize enhanced fungal resistance without growth penalty. These findings uncover a conserved receptor complex for direct chitosan sensing, advancing our understanding of plant-fungus interactions and providing a potential strategy for engineering durable fungal resistance crops.

9.5 NRT1.1 as central signaling hub orchestrating plant growth and stress trade-offs.
Liu R, Wang S, Wang Y, Song J.
reviewCa2+HormonesAbiotic stressFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-23
matched: calcium, kinase*, phosphoryl*, signaling, receptor*, ABA, nitrate, nutrient*, root, root architecture, shoot*, growth
abstract

Plants must continuously balance nitrogen-dependent growth with adaptation to environmental stresses. The nitrate transporter AtNRT1.1 in Arabidopsis thaliana and its rice ortholog OsNRT1.1B have been reported to integrate nitrogen- and stress-related signals in their respective species. This review synthesizes how NRT1.1 orthologs coordinate nitrate and stress signaling. Experimentally characterized NRT1.1 forms a homodimer, and phosphorylation of a key threonine residue (Thr101) acts as a molecular switch, toggling between high- and low-affinity nitrate transport modes. OsNRT1.1B also functions as a high-affinity ABA receptor in rice. NRT1.1 orthologs may integrate nitrate and ABA signals via allosteric regulation and ligand competition. Signaling triggers cellular responses via calcium signatures and kinase cascades, driving transcriptional reprogramming through NLP transcription factors, including AtNLP2/7 in Arabidopsis and OsNLP3/4 in rice. These responses include root architecture remodeling, rhizosphere homeostasis, nitrogen-phosphorus coordination, and shoot-root developmental synchronization. This review also discusses the evolution of the NRT1.1 signaling, highlighting experimentally supported core functions and lineage-specific innovations. The nitrate transport/signaling module discussed here is supported primarily by studies of AtNRT1.1 in Arabidopsis, whereas the ABA-receptor module is supported mainly by studies of OsNRT1.1B in rice. However, whether mechanisms established in Arabidopsis are conserved and agronomically relevant in other crop species remains to be fully established. Although nitrate- and ABA-related NRT1.1 functions have both been investigated in rice, direct experimental evidence that the two proposed modules operate coordinately within a single species remains limited. Accordingly, this model should be viewed as a comparative and hypothesis-generating framework rather than a universally validated mechanism. Understanding this network may provide a basis for identifying targets to improve nutrient use efficiency, stress resilience, and yield stability, pending validation in target crops.

8.5 Diverse signaling proteins recruit RCC1-Like Domain proteins to the plasma membrane through a common structural framework.
Nishimura, T., Hirano … M. T.
preprintCell biology and organellesGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-16
matched: kinase*, signal transduction, signaling, plasma membrane, phototrop*, stoma*, hypocotyl*, growth
abstract

Plants translate diverse environmental cues into spatially regulated growth responses through intracellular signaling mechanisms that remain incompletely understood. Here, we identify the BREVIS RADIX (BRX) domain of RCC1-LIKE DOMAIN (RLD) proteins as a versatile interaction module that recognizes multiple signaling proteins in Arabidopsis. We identified members of the PHYTOCHROME KINASE SUBSTRATE (PKS) and BIG GRAIN (BG) families as novel RLD interactors families, in addition to the previously characterized LAZY1-LIKE (LZY) proteins and BREAKING OF ASYMMETRY IN THE STOMATAL LINEAGE (BASL). Structural, biochemical, and cell-based analyses demonstrate that these diverse partners, despite having divergent interaction sequences, bind a common surface on the RLD BRX domain to recruit RLD to the plasma membrane. This interaction relies on a shared β-strand-based recognition mode, in which distinct peptide conformations accommodate binding to the same BRX-domain surface. Furthermore, we show that RLD2 and RLD3 contribute to hypocotyl phototropism, and that disrupting the conserved PKS interaction motif abolishes PKS4 function in this response. Together, our findings identify the RLD BRX domain as a molecular platform for diverse protein interactions and establish a PKS-RLD module that operates in phototropism. Furthermore, these findings strongly suggest that RLD recruitment to the plasma membrane via PKS underlies phototropic signal transduction.

8.5 Phenotype-genotype association for the identification of resistance to stem borers (Diatraea spp., Lepidoptera: Crambidae) in sugarcane.
Echeverri-Rubiano C, Saavedra-Díaz C, Prias-Blanco M … Aguilar FS.
Ca2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-22
matched: calcium, kinase*, receptor-like kinase*, signaling, receptor*, immun*, defense, nutrient*, stem
abstract

Sugarcane (Saccharum spp.) is a major crop for sugar production, but its productivity is affected by stem borers (Diatraea spp.). These insects cause significant losses by damaging the stem and disrupting the transport of photosynthates and nutrients. Although biological control has been effective in Colombia, the identification of genetic resources for the resistance would provide complementary long-term crop protection. This study aimed to identify markers associated with the resistance to insects. For this purpose, a population of 348 genotypes was evaluated by infesting 30 stalks per genotype with four 9-day-old larvae. The proportion of bored internodes, insect survival, and the Damage Survival Index were recorded for each stem. Genotypic data was obtained using GBS, RADseq, and WGS. A total of six marker-model associations, which includes 4 unique markers, were significantly associated with DS Index, but only 2 were effectively validated. Candidate genes were searched within a 353.1 kb window surrounding the validated markers, revealing 48 genes involved in signaling pathways, receptor-mediated recognition, metabolic responses, direct defense mechanisms, and structural modifications. The DS Index variable integrates plant damage and insect performance, providing a robust metric to study resistance. These results provide a foundation for the identification of genetic resources for Diatraea spp., with key components including receptor-like kinases involved in herbivore recognition, calcium-dependent signaling cascades, and regulatory elements that finely balance immune activation through both positive and negative mechanisms. The validated SNPs, together with the functional annotation of nearby genes, represent an innovative approach to incorporate molecular tools into sugarcane breeding such as genomic selection, genome editing and/or genetic transformation.

8.5 Soil-derived protein corona modulates the behavior and biological effects of nanoplastics in rice.
Han Y, Li J, Sun Y … Luan Y.
Ca2+HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityEnvironmental research · 2026-10-05
matched: Ca2+, RLK*, MAPK*, signaling, hormone*, PRR*, defense, stress respon*, root, leaf, growth
abstract

Nanoplastics are emerging contaminants widely distributed in terrestrial environments. Upon entering soil, their surfaces can adsorb soil-derived proteins to form protein coronas, potentially altering their environmental behavior and biological effects. In this study, amino- and carboxyl-modified polystyrene nanoplastics (PSN and PSC) were incubated with soil-extracted proteins to construct protein coronas, and their uptake, distribution, and phytotoxicity were evaluated in rice. Under controlled exposure conditions, corona-coated nanoplastics exhibited distinct growth inhibition patterns compared with pristine nanoplastics. After exposure, PSC with protein corona (PSC-PC) exhibited the strongest toxicity, reducing root length, leaf length, and total fresh weight by 18.50%, 23.73%, and 11.95%, respectively. Protein corona formation modulated nanoplastic-induced oxidative stress responses. Transcriptomic analysis identified differential expression and enrichment of genes associated with PRR/LRR-RLK-related perception, Ca2+- and MAPK-related signaling, and stress- and hormone-responsive transcriptional programs. Distinct surface functional groups induced divergent molecular responses. PSN-PC was associated with defense- and stress-related transcriptional responses and downregulation of genes involved in energy metabolism, whereas PSC-PC was associated with more pronounced alterations in growth-related processes. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that soil-derived protein coronas can modify nanoplastic behavior during plant uptake and alter their phytotoxic effects, highlighting the importance of considering protein corona formation in environmental risk assessments of nanoplastics.

6.5 The energy sensor SnRK1 promotes starvation-induced autophagosome formation by modulating ATG13a phosphorylation in Arabidopsis
Wang LN, Wang W, Zhang HM … Xiao S
Abiotic stressCell biology and organellesGrowthThe Plant Cell · 2026-10-15
matched: kinase*, phosphoryl*, ubiquitin*, nutrient*, autophagy, leaf
abstract

Abstract In eukaryotes, autophagy is an evolutionarily conserved cellular degradation and recycling process that is tightly associated with cellular nutrient status. The energy sensor SNF1-RELATED KINASE 1 (SnRK1) is thought to sense nutrient starvation and induce autophagy, but how it regulates autophagosome formation in plant cells remains largely unknown. Here, we show that the SnRK1 α-catalytic subunit KIN10 regulates autophagosome formation in Arabidopsis (Arabidopsis thaliana) by interacting with and phosphorylating AUTOPHAGY-RELATED PROTEIN 13a (ATG13a), a key component of the ATG1a–ATG13a protein kinase complex involved in autophagy initiation. KIN10 directly phosphorylates ATG13a in vitro and in vivo, inhibiting its ubiquitination and increasing its abundance. Moreover, we identified ten serine residues in ATG13a that are important for KIN10-mediated phosphorylation. Transgenic plants accumulating a nonphosphorylatable ATG13a variant with mutations of these ten residues were less tolerant of starvation than ATG13a-overexpressing plants and displayed accelerated leaf senescence and lower autophagic flux. The KIN10-mediated phosphorylation of ATG13a did not affect ATG1a–ATG13a complex formation; instead, it facilitated ATG1a phosphorylation, thereby promoting autophagosome formation. Therefore, our findings suggest that the KIN10–ATG13a module modulates SnRK1-mediated autophagosome formation, likely representing a key step in integrating energy sensing and autophagy initiation in plant cells.

6.0 COI1 phosphorylation status drives redox state-mediated thermotolerance in plants
De Smet I, Zhu T, Williams C … Goossens A.
preprintHormonesAbiotic stressGrowthResearch Square · 2026-10-14
matched: phosphoryl*, signalling, receptor*, jasmon*, JA, phytohormone*, hormone*, heat stress, growth
abstract

Abstract High temperature increasingly threatens crop growth and yield under climate change. Plants rely on phytohormone signalling to adapt to heat stress, yet the underlying mechanisms remain poorly understood. One of the plant hormone receptors, the jasmonate receptor CORONATINE-INSENSITIVE 1 (COI1), plays an essential role in conferring plant thermotolerance. However, to date, the molecular mechanisms by which COI1 mediates plant high temperature tolerance remain enigmatic. Here, we characterise moderately high temperature-regulated differential phosphorylation of TaCOI1-1D, one of the wheat COI1 homeologous proteins. We show that TaCOI1-1D is a functional component of the Skp1–Cullin–F-box COI1 (SCF COI1 ) complex and that its heat-regulated phosphosite, Ser16, is conserved with Thr16 in Arabidopsis COI1. Mutating this site enhances the COI1– ARABIDOPSIS SKP1 HOMOLOGUE 1 (ASK1) interaction in both wheat and Arabidopsis. We further demonstrate that Arabidopsis COI1 interacts with the nucleocytosolic monothiol glutaredoxin glutaredoxin S17 (GRXS17). The enhanced COI1–ASK1 binding stabilizes COI1 and promotes the release of GRXS17, allowing the formation of GRXS17 oligomers, a possibly essential state for AtGRXS17’s function in regulating H 2 O 2 scavenging and enhancing thermotolerance in Arabidopsis. Our findings reveal a mechanism in which a moderately high temperature-induced modification of the JA receptor simultaneously stabilizes the receptor and orchestrates redox-dependent thermotolerance.

6.0 High temperature disrupts sporocyte development in Camellia meiocarpa.
Zhang T, Xiao W, Liu X … Han Z.
GrowthTrees and woody plantsTree physiology · 2026-10-03
matched: kinase*, MAPK*, signaling, embryo*, development, woody
abstract

High temperature has a significant impact on plant reproductive development, driving the formation of 2n gametes by interfering with the meiosis and cytokinesis of sporocytes. This study used Camellia meiocarpa as the material, integrating cytological and molecular biology approaches to reveal the cytological effects and regulatory mechanisms of high temperature on sporocyte development. The results showed that meiosis in microsporocyte and megasporocyte of C. meiocarpa exhibits pronounced spatiotemporal heterogeneity. High temperature markedly disrupted sporocyte development and induced abnormal phenomena, inducing disordered cytokinesis, leading to the formation of dyads, triads in microsporocyte, as well as multinucleate cells occurring during the development of megaspores into mature embryo sacs. RNA-seq sequencing analysis indicates that the CoNPK1 gene may regulate meiotic and cytokinetic behavior in response to high temperature. Meanwhile, heterologous transformation in tobacco verified that the CoNPK1 gene induces the formation of dyads and triads through upregulated expression, which further leads to the production of 2n gametes. We speculate that the CoNPK1 gene regulates cell plate synthesis via the mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) signaling pathway, thereby affecting cytokinesis and generating 2n gametes. This lays a theoretical foundation for ploidy breeding of woody oil crops.

6.0 OsGSK2-OsBSK3 feedback loop controls rice BR signaling
Tian X, Zhang W, Fu L … Bu Q
HormonesGrowthPlant Physiology · 2026-10-16
matched: kinase*, phosphoryl*, signaling, brassinosteroid*, hormone*, leaf, growth, development
abstract

Abstract Brassinosteroids (BRs) are essential plant hormones that regulate growth and development, including leaf angle regulation and grain size determination. Maintaining appropriate BR signaling requires precise coordination between pathway activation and attenuation. BR signaling is initiated by OsBRI1 activation, which triggers a phosphorylation cascade that ultimately inhibits OsGSK2 kinase activity, releasing OsBZR1 from repression and activating downstream responses. However, whether additional regulatory mechanisms fine-tune BR signaling remains unclear. Here, we identify OsBSK3 as a direct substrate of OsGSK2 and uncover a phosphorylation-dependent feedback mechanism within the BR signaling pathway. OsGSK2-mediated phosphorylation enhances the interactions of OsBSK3 with OsBRI1, OsGSK2, and OsPPKL1 while attenuating the regulatory activity of OsBSK3. Genetic analyses revealed that overexpression of wild-type OsBSK3 or the phosphorylation-deficient variant OsBSK3SA enhances BR responses and fully rescues the leaf angle and grain size defects of the osbsk3 mutant, whereas the phosphomimetic variant OsBSK3SD fails to restore these phenotypes. Together, our findings establish a phosphorylation-dependent OsGSK2-OsBSK3 negative feedback mechanism that fine-tunes BR signaling homeostasis in rice.

6.0 Transcriptomic and Metabolomic Analyses Provide Insights into Foliar Vitamin C Application Compromises Resistance to Blast in Foxtail Millet
Zhao Y, Cai W, Liu J … Li Y.
preprintHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityResearch Square · 2026-10-13
matched: kinase*, receptor-like kinase*, signaling, receptor*, jasmon*, JA, chitin, pathogen*
abstract

Abstract Blast disease is a major fungal disease constraining foxtail millet production. Exogenous vitamin C is widely applied to alleviate abiotic stresses in crops, yet its regulatory function in foxtail millet resistance against fungal pathogens remains unclear. After foliar spraying of 8 mM vitamin C on foxtail millet cultivar Jingu 21, the plant resistance to blast disease was markedly reduced. Transcriptomic screening identified a total of 734 differentially expressed genes (DEGs). Enrichment analyses revealed significant perturbations in the jasmonic acid (JA) signaling pathway and the plant-pathogen interaction pathway, with prominent downregulation of core JA pathway genes and chitin recognition receptor LysM receptor-like kinases (LYK) genes. A total of 137 differential metabolites were characterized via metabolomic profiling, among which disease-resistant secondary metabolites including jasmonic acid, indole-4-carboxaldehyde and tremulacin exhibited distinctly reduced abundances. From a multi-omics perspective, this study uncovers the molecular basis underlying the negative regulation of foxtail millet fungal resistance by vitamin C, and provides theoretical support for rational field application of vitamin C and eco-friendly management of foxtail millet blast disease.

20.0 Redox regulation of plant hormone signaling transduction.
Jing PW, Li SQ, Chen BH … Liu WC.
reviewCa2+Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressJournal of plant physiology · 2026-09-30
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, kinase*, phosphatase*, signaling, receptor*, second messenger*, auxin*, abscisic acid, ABA, cytokinin*, ethylene
abstract

Plants need to precisely coordinate growth and development with stress responses under constantly changing environmental conditions, and the interaction between redox signaling and plant hormone signaling is an important regulatory mechanism underlying this balance. Reactive oxygen species (ROS) are not only oxidative metabolic products generated under stress conditions, but also important second messengers that modulate protein functions through oxidative post-translational modifications (oxi-PTMs). Recent studies have shown that ROS can directly regulate plant hormone signaling transduction through oxi-PTMs. By altering the activity, stability, subcellular localization, or protein interactions of hormone receptors, protein kinases, phosphatases, transcription factors, or hormone metabolic enzymes through redox modifications, ROS regulate the biosynthesis and signaling outputs of multiple hormones, including auxin, abscisic acid (ABA), brassinosteroids (BR), jasmonic acid (JA), salicylic acid (SA), ethylene (ET), and cytokinin (CK). In turn, plant hormones can reshape ROS homeostasis by regulating ROS-producing or -scavenging systems, thereby forming complex bidirectional feedback networks. ROS can also act as common signaling nodes among different hormone pathways to integrate plant growth and development with stress responses. This review summarizes the molecular mechanisms by which redox signals regulate plant hormone signaling transduction, with particular emphasis on the roles of protein redox modifications in hormone perception, signal transmission, and multi-hormone interactions, and discusses the potential of exploiting key redox-sensitive sites for precise crop improvement, with the aim of providing a new theoretical basis for understanding plant environmental adaptation and breeding stress-resistant, high-yielding crops.

12.0 Ethylene as a Stomatal Signalling Hub: Integrating Hormonal and Stress Signals to Optimize Stomatal Responses
Khan TA, Yi J, Shi Y … Chen Q
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionGrowthPlant, Cell & Environment · 2026-10-14
matched: ROS, Rboh*, signalling, abscisic acid, ABA, ethylene, hormone*, stoma*, growth
abstract

ABSTRACT The gaseous plant hormone ethylene (ET) regulates diverse growth responses and adaptive processes to environmental stimuli. Although the role of ET in stress signalling is well established, its effects on stomatal responses remain paradoxical. ET induces stomatal closure in some contexts yet antagonizes abscisic acid‐mediated closure in others. While diverse studies have reported that ET regulates stomatal responses, the differential role of ET in stomatal signalling has not been reconciled into a unified framework. Here, we synthesize current evidence into a bidirectional model of ET‐dependent stomatal regulation, in which ET signalling produces two antagonistic outputs: a closure pathway driven by Gα‐dependent, RBOHD/F‐mediated H 2 O 2 production and downstream NO and H 2 S signals acting via SLAC1‐type anion channels; and an opposing pathway in which EIN2‐dependent flavonol accumulation scavenges guard‐cell ROS, thereby antagonizing stomatal closure. Additionally, the differential role of ET across species, dosages and stress conditions, as well as the compensatory role of ET in stomatal closure when ABA signalling is compromised is also discussed. This review offers novel insights into the function of ET in stomatal regulation and provides a mechanistic foundation for harnessing ET signalling to optimize the trade‐off between plant growth and stress resilience.

10.5 A CRC–MYB chromatin loop controls auxin-mediated nectary growth in Arabidopsis
Iimura H, Takahashi M, Ohta R … Yamaguchi N
Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthNature Communications · 2026-10-17
matched: signaling, auxin*, PIN\d+, phytohormone*, nucleus, growth, development, organogenesis, spatial transcriptom*
abstract

Abstract Floral nectaries, typically located at stamen bases, secrete sugar-rich nectar containing diverse secondary metabolites. Despite their defined position and specialized function, the mechanisms governing nectary formation remain unclear. Using publicly available single-nucleus RNA-seq data and newly generated spatial transcriptomic data, we identify a putative ordering of transcriptional states associated with nectary development and a spatial regulatory network controlling nectary growth in Arabidopsis thaliana . We further show that CRABS CLAW (CRC) is central to this network, integrating transcriptional regulation with spatial auxin transport and pathways controlling phytohormone signaling, sugar transport and terpene biosynthesis. CRC interacts with MYB21, MYB24 and MYB57 to form a complex that binds the 5′ and 3′ regulatory regions of MACCHI-BOU 4 ( MAB4 ), promoting a chromatin loop that enhances MAB4 expression and supports proper nectary size. MAB4 reinforces PIN-FORMED 1-mediated auxin transport to establish auxin maxima at the nectary tip and regulates PIN6 localization to maintain auxin gradients. These findings link transcriptional regulation and auxin transport with nectary organogenesis and metabolic function.

9.0 An importin-α IMA8 facilitates nuclear import of COI1 to promote jasmonate-mediated defense responses in Arabidopsis.
He J, Su Y, Liu X … Wang J.
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesPlant communications · 2026-10-10
matched: signaling, receptor*, jasmon*, JA, phytohormone*, defense, nucleus, nuclear, growth
abstract

Jasmonate (JA) is an essential phytohormone that coordinates plant defense, growth, and developmental processes in response to diverse environmental cues. The JA receptor CORONATINE INSENSITIVE 1 (COI1) is a core component of JA signaling and functions in the nucleus to regulate JA-responsive transcription. However, how newly synthesized COI1 in the cytoplasm is imported into the nucleus remains unclear. Here, we identified an importin IMPORTIN ALPHA ISOFORM 8 (IMA8) as a nuclear transport receptor (NTR) for COI1. We found that IMA8 positively regulates JA responses. Biochemical analyses confirmed an interaction between IMA8 and COI1. Both in vitro transport assay and in vivo nucleo-cytoplasmic fractionation experiment demonstrated that IMA8 facilitates the nuclear import of COI1. Furthermore, the hypersensitive phenotypes triggered by COI1 overexpression were markedly suppressed in the ima8 mutant, indicating that COI1 function depends on IMA8-facilitated nuclear import. Together, our results reveal a previously unrecognized regulatory step in JA signaling, and provide new insights into the spatial organization of plant defense pathways.

9.0 Pepper RING‐Type E3 Ligase, CaAIAR1, Negatively Modulates ABA Signaling and Drought Stress Response by Controlling CaADBZ1 Stability
Choi J, Baek W, Lim CW … Lee SC
Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressGrowthPlant, Cell & Environment · 2026-10-18
matched: signaling, ubiquitin*, abscisic acid, ABA, drought, stress respon*, leaves, seed*
abstract

ABSTRACT The ubiquitin–proteasome pathway plays a crucial role in plant abiotic stress responses by regulating protein stability. Our previous work demonstrated that the pepper bZIP transcription factor CaADBZ1 regulates abscisic acid (ABA) signaling and drought stress responses. However, the regulatory factors controlling CaADBZ1 protein stability remain unknown. Here, we performed a yeast two‐hybrid assay to identify the E3 ubiquitin ligase involved in regulating CaADBZ1 stability. The pepper RING‐type E3 ligase CaAIAR1 ( Capsicum annuum ABA‐Induced Ankyrin repeat RING‐type E3 ligase 1) interacts with CaADBZ1 both in vitro and in vivo. CaAIAR1 ubiquitinates CaADBZ1 and exhibits E3 ligase activity that depends on its RING domain. A cell‐free degradation assay revealed that CaADBZ1 was unstable under non‐stress conditions, and CaAIAR1 accelerated its degradation via the 26S proteasome pathway. CaAIAR1 expression was significantly induced by various abiotic stresses and was associated with altered phenotypic responses to drought stress and exogenous ABA treatment. Moreover, CaAIAR1 ‐silenced pepper plants exhibited enhanced drought tolerance compared to control plants, as evidenced by reduced transpiration rates and increased ABA sensitivity in leaves. Conversely, CaAIAR1 ‐overexpressing Arabidopsis plants showed decreased drought tolerance compared to control plants and exhibited reduced ABA sensitivity at the seedling stage. In addition, co‐silencing CaAIAR1 and CaADBZ1 increased drought sensitivity, producing a phenotype opposite to that of CaAIAR1 silencing alone, indicating that CaAIAR1 functions upstream of CaADBZ1 in drought stress. Collectively, our findings suggest that CaAIAR1 negatively regulates the drought response and ABA signaling by modulating CaADBZ1 protein stability.

9.0 Three PRR proteins interact with ARF10 and ARF16 to enhance ABI5 activation and auxin-stimulated ABA responses during Arabidopsis seed germination
Lou Y, Han X, Yang M … Hu Y

8.5 Thirty years of plant farnesylation research: Few substrates, many gaps, and a clear path forward.
Daszkowska-Golec A, Sundaresan AP, Distefano MD.
reviewKinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressJournal of plant physiology · 2026-09-28
matched: signalling, abscisic acid, ABA, cytokinin*, brassinosteroid*, immun*, drought, meristem*, development
abstract

Protein farnesylation - the covalent attachment of a 15-carbon farnesyl isoprenoid to a C-terminal CaaX motif by protein farnesyltransferase (PFT, FTase) - is one of the most functionally important post-translational modifications in eukaryotic biology. In mammals, the biomedical significance of farnesylation is well established. The FTase inhibitor (FTI) lonafarnib treats Hutchinson-Gilford progeria syndrome. FTase remains an oncology target, and chemical proteomic strategies have mapped more than 100 farnesylated substrates at single-protein resolution. In plants, genetic evidence establishes that FTase governs abscisic acid (ABA) signalling, brassinosteroid (BR) homeostasis, cytokinin (CK) partitioning, meristem development, thermotolerance, innate immunity, nitrogen sensing, and drought resilience across Arabidopsis thaliana, barley (Hordeum vulgare), canola (Brassica napus), wheat (Triticum aestivum), soybean (Glycine max), and rice (Oryza sativa). Yet fewer than fifteen substrates have been biochemically confirmed in any plant species after nearly three decades of research, and no crop species has been subjected to prenylome-scale chemical proteomics. This review synthesizes the plant and animal farnesylation literature and argues that this disconnect is methodological rather than biological. We trace the arc from FTase enzymology through the emerging catalogue of confirmed plant substrates, exemplified by the HSP40 co-chaperones J2/J3, the cytokinin-biosynthetic enzyme AtIPT3, and plasmodesmal HIPPs, to crop engineering outcomes. We propose a spatial-switch model to organize the confirmed substrate data into a unifying mechanistic framework, while acknowledging that this model awaits systematic experimental testing. Finally, we outline the chemical biology tools - alkyne-isoprenoid probes, quantitative prenylomics profiling - that must be adapted for plant systems to close the gap between substrate prediction and biochemical validation. Farnesylation exemplifies how a conserved eukaryotic modification can be harnessed for crop improvement once the underlying substrate biology is sufficiently understood.

8.0 Rhizosphere functional microbiome remodeling by Streptomyces and jasmonic acid mitigates cadmium accumulation in rice.
Yan C, Liu J, Ma S … Liu H.
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressMicrobiological research · 2026-10-10
matched: signal transduction, signaling, jasmon*, JA, microbiome*, defense, cadmium, growth
abstract

Cadmium (Cd) contamination poses a serious threat to rice production and food safety because of its high mobility in soil and its accumulation in edible rice tissues. Although beneficial microorganisms and plant signaling regulators have individually shown potential for alleviating Cd stress, whether their combined application can reduce Cd accumulation by restructuring rhizosphere microbial functions remains poorly understood. This study investigated the effects of two Cd-tolerant, plant growth-promoting Streptomyces strains, alone or in combination with jasmonic acid (JA), on Cd stress, Cd accumulation, and rhizosphere microbial communities and functions in rice. The results indicated that Streptomyces co-inoculation improved rice growth, photosynthetic performance, antioxidant defense, and osmotic regulation. It also reduced soil Cd bioavailability and altered Cd partitioning within rice plants. JA alone exerted relatively limited effects on the rhizosphere microbiome but strengthened Streptomyces-mediated physiological and microbial responses when applied in combination. Metagenomic analysis showed that the treatments altered microbial community composition and enriched COG functions associated with inorganic ion transport, signal transduction, and cell envelope biogenesis. KEGG analysis further revealed changes in pathways associated with environmental sensing and Cd detoxification, particularly the two-component system and glutathione metabolism. BacMet analysis identified 37 Cd-related resistance genes. Streptomyces co-inoculation broadly enriched resistance modules involved in metal sensing, transport, efflux, and detoxification, whereas the combined treatment selectively enriched core resistance genes, including czcA, czcP, zntA, and nccB. These changes were consistent with the reduced bioavailable Cd in rhizosphere soil and lower Cd accumulation in rice. Rather than increasing the number of responsive genes, the addition of JA to Streptomyces co-inoculation was associated with a more focused Cd resistance gene response, particularly involving metal transport and efflux. Overall, the reduction in Cd accumulation was associated with coordinated changes in rhizosphere microbial communities, metabolic pathways, and Cd resistance genes. These findings highlight the potential of combining Streptomyces co-inoculation with JA as a microbiome based strategy for rice cultivation in Cd contaminated soils.

8.0 Waterlogging and hypoxia in plant abiotic stress.
Kumar A, Kumar M, Kumar N … Sinha S.
reviewPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressGrowthFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-21
matched: auxin*, abscisic acid, gibberellin*, ethylene, defense, hypoxia, flooding, root, root development, shoot*, development, CRISPR
abstract

Waterlogging is a major abiotic stress affecting 12-27% of global cultivated land and causing average yield losses of 20-33% in cereals and legumes. This poses a serious threat to food security in flood-prone regions. Soil saturation reduces gas diffusion nearly 10,000-fold compared to air. This rapidly induces hypoxia or anoxia in the root zone within hours to days. This oxygen limitation forces a metabolic shift from efficient aerobic respiration (approximately 36 ATP per glucose) to anaerobic fermentation (approximately 2 ATP per glucose). This shift results in severe energy deficits, cytoplasmic acidosis, and accumulation of toxic metabolites such as ethanol, acetaldehyde, and organic acids. Plants exhibit diverse adaptive responses to flooding stress, including morphological modifications such as aerenchyma formation, adventitious root development, hypertrophied lenticels, and shoot elongation in deepwater rice. These modifications facilitate internal oxygen transport and gas exchange. At the biochemical level, fermentative enzymes including alcohol dehydrogenase and pyruvate decarboxylase are upregulated, while antioxidant defense systems mitigate oxidative damage during reoxygenation. Hormonal crosstalk among ethylene, abscisic acid, auxin, and gibberellins regulates the balance between escape and quiescence strategies. At the molecular level, oxygen sensing is primarily mediated by the N-degron pathway through Group VII Ethylene Response Factors (ERF-VIIs). Plant cysteine oxidases regulate oxygen-dependent degradation under normoxia, while hypoxia stabilizes ERF-VIIs to activate anaerobic gene expression. Nitric oxide further modulates ERF-VII proteolysis and stability. The Sub1A locus in rice exemplifies genetic adaptation, conferring 14-18 days of submergence tolerance and significant yield advantages in flood-prone environments. Despite these advances, major crops such as wheat, maize, and soybean remain highly susceptible, underscoring the need for translating mechanistic insights into field-level solutions. Integrating CRISPR/Cas-based gene editing, genomic selection, multi-omics approaches, and improved agronomic practices offers promising avenues for developing flood-resilient crop varieties under changing climatic conditions.

7.5 Expression profiling of the gibberellin 2-oxidase gene CsGA2ox2 (Cucumis sativus L.) reveals stage- and tissue-specific transcript accumulation during fruit development.
An X, Liu Y, Liu Z, Wang W.
Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthMolecular biology reports · 2026-10-08
matched: phosphoryl*, abscisic acid, gibberellin*, GA, actin, meristem*, development
abstract

BackgroundGibberellins (GAs) regulate fruit set and expansion, and their endogenous levels are controlled in part through catabolic inactivation. GA2ox enzymes convert bioactive GAs into inactive forms, but the expression of GA2ox genes during cucumber fruit development has not been characterised in a tissue-resolved manner.MethodsCucumber (Cucumis sativus L.) '9930' fruit were sampled at 0, 7, 14, 21 and 28 days after pollination. The inner fleshy tissue (pulp) and the outer fruit wall (pericarp) were separated, and CsGA2ox2 transcript abundance was quantified by quantitative real-time PCR using the 2^-ΔΔCt method with Actin (LOC101215469) as the internal reference gene. The protein encoded by CsGA2ox2 was characterised in silico, including sequence homology, physicochemical properties, signal peptide and transmembrane prediction, secondary and tertiary structure modelling, phosphorylation site prediction, and annotation of promoter cis-acting elements.ResultsCsGA2ox2 was transcribed at every developmental stage examined. Transcript abundance was low at 0 and 7 days after pollination, increased significantly by 21 days after pollination, and was consistently and significantly higher in pulp than in pericarp at all stages examined. All protein-level results presented here are computational predictions and provide no direct functional evidence. The data are descriptive and correlative and are intended as a foundation for future functional studies on GA catabolism in Cucurbitaceae fruit development.

6.5 Environmental, developmental, and genetic factors differentially affect rapid ethylene growth response kinetics in dark-grown Arabidopsis thaliana hypocotyls.
Bakshi A, Patel V, Ward A … Binder BM.
Abiotic stressGrowthPloS one · 2026-10-06
matched: ethylene, hormone*, nutrient*, hypocotyl*, seed*, growth
abstract

Studies on dark-grown wild-type Arabidopsis thaliana seedlings have demonstrated that the hypocotyls respond to the application and removal of the hormone ethylene with stereotypical growth kinetics. This includes a rapid decrease in growth rate when ethylene is applied that stabilizes at a low growth rate approximately 75 min after the addition of saturating levels of ethylene. Hypocotyl growth rate recovers to pre-treatment levels approximately 80 minutes after removal of ethylene. The growth of plants is influenced by environmental conditions that can vary between laboratories as well as genetic backgrounds and developmental factors. To address the importance of these variables on ethylene responses, experiments were conducted to determine the effects of composition of growth medium, seed size, seedling age, and accession on ethylene growth response kinetics. These variables led to only minor alterations in growth inhibition kinetics when ethylene was added with the largest effect being an acceleration in growth inhibition when nutrients were excluded from the medium. By contrast, growth recovery kinetics upon ethylene removal showed more plasticity. Sucrose accelerated growth recovery, whereas other environmental variables delayed recovery compared to controls. Other variables that are often overlooked, such as seed size and seedling age, also affected growth recovery where older seedlings recovered more slowly and plants from larger seeds recovered faster than plants from smaller seeds. Accessions from more northern latitudes tended to recover faster than those from more southern latitudes. Our data indicate that these factors need to be carefully controlled for optimal reproducibility of responses to ensure accurate interpretation of data and make sure important traits are not masked.

Križnik M, Lukan T, Zagorščak M … Gruden K

matched: apoplast*, signaling, auxin*, salicylic acid, immun*, effector*, pathogen*, defense, development
abstract

Summary Hypersensitive response (HR) is an effector‐triggered immune response leading to pathogen restriction and local cell death. Small RNAs (sRNAs), mediating RNA silencing, have a well‐established role in antiviral immunity; however, their role in HR has not been addressed to date. We applied a spatially resolved sRNA‐omics approach to uncover changes at the sRNA level and the dynamics of sRNA–gene regulatory networks in potato ( Solanum tuberosum L.) HR to potato virus Y (PVY). We show that miR160 is repressed in cells adjacent to HR lesions in the resistant cultivar, but not in PVY‐sensitive, salicylic acid (SA)‐depleted plants. Beyond its canonical regulation of StARF10 and StARF17 , miR160 controls a broader regulatory network spanning auxin signaling, cell‐wall remodeling, redox homeostasis, and tuberization. 6.0 Tracing the Evolutionary Origins of Jasmonate Signaling: Co-Evolution of Oxylipin Metabolism and Hormone Perception in the Green Lineage
Chini A, Jimenez-Aleman GH, Solano R
Kinases and signal transductionPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthJournal of Experimental Botany · 2026-10-17
matched: signaling, receptor*, jasmon*, JA, hormone*, defense, development
abstract

Abstract Jasmonates constitute a central class of lipid-derived signaling molecules that regulate plant defense, development, and environmental adaptation. Although jasmonic acid (JA) and its bioactive conjugate jasmonoyl-L-isoleucine (JA-Ile) are well characterized in angiosperms, recent evolutionary, biochemical, and structural studies have revealed that jasmonate signaling predates the origin of land plants. Jasmonates occur in streptophyte algae but are absent from chlorophytes, indicating that jasmonate biosynthesis evolved in the last common ancestor of streptophytes. However, the molecular nature, perception mechanisms, and biological functions of jasmonates have undergone profound changes during plant evolution. During terrestrialization, the recruitment of a pre-existing transcriptional regulatory network by a newly evolved COI1 receptor module established the canonical COI1/JAZ signaling system. Early diverging land plants employed dn-OPDA, a JA precursor, as the ancestral ligand, which was progressively replaced by JA amino acid conjugates such as JA-Ile in vascular plants. Here, we synthesize current evidence supporting a stepwise evolutionary model progressively shaping the COI1/JAZ-dependent hormone pathway. We discuss how (bio)chemical diversification of oxylipins, co-evolution of receptors and ligands, and the emergence of JA-Ile in vascular plants enabled the expansion, specificity, and systemic integration of jasmonate signaling as a requirement for plant terrestrialization.

19.5 The Arabidopsis outward K+ channel GORK mediates flg22-induced K+ efflux and stomatal closure
Wang, L., Yue … C.
preprintKinases and signal transductionGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-13
matched: kinase*, phosphoryl*, signaling, receptor*, immun*, PTI, PAMP*, flg22, pattern recognition receptor*, PRR*, pathogen*, Botrytis
abstract

Transient K+ efflux occurs rapidly following pathogen-associated molecular pattern (PAMP) perception by pattern recognition receptors (PRRs) as part of pattern-triggered immunity (PTI). However, the molecular identities of the K+ channels responsible for PTI-associated K+ efflux and the mechanisms linking immune signaling to K+ channel regulation remain largely unknown. Here, we show that the channel GUARD CELL OUTWARD RECTIFYING K+ (GORK) is required for K+ efflux and stomatal closure induced by flg22, a model PAMP derived from bacterial flagellin. Together, this study identifies GORK as the key K+ channel executing rapid PTI-associated K+ efflux and associated stomatal closure, thus shedding light on the mechanisms of early immune signaling.

18.5 Loss of Arabidopsis ferroxidases LPR1 and LPR2 enhances plant immunity by promoting leaf iron deposition.
Qiao Y, Wang Z, Xu Z … Zhang M.
Ca2+Abiotic stressGrowthPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-08
matched: ROS, reactive oxygen species, immun*, PTI, ETI, PAMP*, effector*, pathogen*, Pseudomonas syringae, defense, nutrient*, root
abstract

Iron (Fe) is a critical nutrient for both plants and their pathogens, and its distribution and homeostasis critically influence the outcome of plant-pathogen interactions. Low Phosphate Root 1 (LPR1) and LPR2 are ferroxidases that regulate Fe translocation in the xylem, but their role in plant immunity remains unknown. Here, we report that loss of LPR1 and LPR2 in Arabidopsis thaliana confers enhanced resistance to both virulent (Pseudomonas syringae pv. Tomato DC3000, Pst) and avirulent (Pst-avrRpt2) bacterial strains. This enhanced resistance is associated with excessive Fe deposition in the leaves of the lpr1 lpr2 double mutant. This resistance is further enhanced by exogenous Fe supplementation and attenuated under Fe-limited conditions. Mechanistically, this iron deposition leads to a more robust reactive oxygen species (ROS) burst, accelerated hypersensitive response (HR) cell death, and enhanced callose deposition upon pathogen challenge. Furthermore, the lpr1 lpr2 mutant exhibits constitutively elevated expression of defense genes, including both effector-triggered immunity (ETI) and PAMP-triggered immunity (PTI) markers, and displays a primed state upon infection with either Pst-avrRpt2 or Pst. Collectively, our findings uncover LPR1 and LPR2 as novel negative regulators of plant immunity that limit defense activation by preventing excessive Fe deposition in leaves and maintaining a normal cellular iron homeostasis. This work establishes a critical link between iron distribution/homeostasis and defense activation and provides a potential strategy for enhancing disease resistance through Fe modulation.

16.5 Bacterial extracellular vesicles as emerging biostimulants in plant microbe signaling and stress tolerance.
Maqbool H, Ullah N, Fazil I … Naz I.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressWorld journal of microbiology & biotechnology · 2026-10-07
matched: signaling, receptor*, phytohormone*, immun*, PTI, pattern recognition receptor*, PRR*, pathogen*, defense, salinity, drought, nutrient*
abstract

Bacterial extracellular vesicles (BEVs) are nanosized membrane-bound nanoparticles naturally secreted by Gram-negative and Gram-positive bacteria. These vesicles mediate intercellular communication through delivery of diverse bioactive cargo, including proteins, lipids, nucleic acids, metabolites, and signaling molecules. Initially recognized as virulence determinants in pathogenic bacteria, BEVs are increasingly recognized as multifunctional platforms with potential applications in sustainable agriculture. This review examines BEV biogenesis, cargo composition, plant uptake, and emerging roles in plant growth promotion, stress adaptation, and disease resistance. Emphasis is placed on BEV-mediated modulation of plant immunity through pattern recognition receptors (PRRs), pattern-triggered immunity (PTI), and induced systemic resistance (ISR). Potential roles of vesicle-associated phytohormones, ACC deaminase, siderophores, extracellular enzymes, and regulatory RNAs in nutrient acquisition, phytohormone homeostasis, and stress responses are critically evaluated. Emerging evidence linking BEVs to drought, salinity, heat, heavy metal, and nutrient-deficiency responses is examined, with emphasis on antioxidant defense, osmotic adjustment, ion homeostasis, and stress-responsive signaling. The review also discusses engineered BEVs as potential biostimulants, nano-biofertilizers, biocontrol agents, and precision delivery systems, including CRISPR-based engineering, synthetic vesicles, AI-guided cargo optimization, and smart nanoformulations. Major challenges include scalable production, standardization, biosafety, regulation, and field validation. However, critical knowledge gaps remain regarding BEV perception, cellular uptake, cargo delivery, and the causal basis of plant responses, together with uncertainties surrounding biosafety, reproducibility, and performance under field conditions. Collectively, BEVs represent promising biological nanocarriers for climate-resilient and sustainable agriculture, but further mechanistic, methodological, and field-based research is required to establish their reliability and practical agricultural potential.

abstract

Chitin deacetylases (CDAs) are essential for phytopathogenic fungi to evade plant immune defenses by deacetylating chitin oligomers into chitosan. 13.5 Structural basis for size-selective perception of chitin in plants
Gysel K, Hansen SB, Rübsam H … Andersen KR
Kinases and signal transductionScience · 2026-10-15
matched: kinase*, receptor kinase*, signaling, receptor*, immun*, MAMP*, chitin, pattern recognition receptor*, elicitor*
abstract

Plants detect microbes through pattern recognition receptors that perceive conserved microbial surface motifs known as microbe-associated molecular patterns (MAMPs). Lysin motif (LysM) receptors recognize and mediate downstream responses to chitinous MAMPs. In this study, we elucidated the mechanism for the specific recognition of long-chain chitin oligomers and identified a hallmark bridge domain characteristic of the CHITIN PERCEPTION (CHIP) receptor class. 13.0 Pathogen-Induced Cell Wall Remodeling and Immune Signaling in Plants.
Jeon D, Bae SH, Kim T, Lim GH.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesCell biology and organellesThe plant pathology journal · 2026-10-01
matched: kinase*, signaling, receptor*, hormone*, immun*, effector*, pathogen*, elicitor*, defense, cell wall*, growth
abstract

The plant cell wall is a primary barrier to pathogen invasion and a dynamic extracellular interface involved in defense signaling. During infection, pathogens remodel the wall through cell wall-degrading enzymes and effectors, whereas plants reinforce the wall and perceive wall-derived damage-associated molecular patterns that activate immune responses. Recent studies have refined this view by challenging a simple wallassociated kinase-centered model of oligogalacturonide perception, linking MIK2-associated peptide-receptor pathways to pattern-triggered immunity, and showing that pathogens can chemically modify wall-derived fragments to alter their elicitor activity. In addition to chemical cues, pathogen-induced wall perturbation can also affect physical properties such as wall stiffness, porosity, membrane tension, and localized deformation, although how these inputs are integrated during infection remains incompletely understood. This review summarizes current understanding of pathogeninduced cell wall remodeling, wall-derived immune signaling, receptor-associated wall integrity pathways, and their implications for crop disease resistance. We highlight emerging connections among wall chemistry, wall mechanics, peptide signaling, hormone regulation, and growth-defense trade-offs as important directions for understanding wall-mediated immunity.

12.0 Rhizobial type III effectors as regulators of root-nodule symbiosis in legumes
Camuel A, Teulet A, del Cerro P
Kinases and signal transductionGrowthJournal of Experimental Botany · 2026-10-17
matched: kinase*, signalling, receptor*, immun*, effector*, pathogen*, rhizobi*, symbio*, root, organogenesis
abstract

Abstract The rhizobia-legume nitrogen-fixing symbiosis begins when plants perceive rhizobial Nod factors (NF), activating the Common Symbiosis Signalling Pathway (CSSP) that drives nodule organogenesis. Rhizobia also inject Type III effectors (T3E) into host cells through a Type III Secretion System (T3SS) related to those of pathogenic bacteria. These effectors were long viewed only as suppressors of plant immunity and determinants of host compatibility, but this picture has changed. Recent studies revealed that rhizobial effectors (known as Nodulation Outer Proteins, Nops) such as NopT, NopM, NopL, NopP and NopZ directly target NF receptors or associated kinases, thereby regulating CSSP activation. The effector-triggering nodulation proteins (ET-Nods), a separate class of T3Es found in various Bradyrhizobium species, can activate nodule organogenesis independently of NF perception by rewiring host transcriptional and SUMOylation-based signalling. We trace the evolutionary origins of these activities, which may derive either from redirection of ancestral immune-targeting effectors toward symbiotic functions or from rhizobium-specific innovations. We ask whether the T3SS contributed to nodulation. We then consider what this interference means for the host, ranging from reinforced partner choice to partial loss of host control, and argue that rhizobial effectors have evolved as specialised modulators that converge on the signalling hubs governing symbiosis.

11.5 Asymptomatic colonization by Botrytis cinerea reveals polarized plant-fungus interfaces before widespread disease
Ines, T., Perrine … F.-X.
preprintHormonesCell biology and organellesGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-13
matched: jasmon*, hormone*, chitin, pathogen*, Botrytis, plasma membrane, actin, seed*, growth, development, microscop*
abstract

Botrytis cinerea is a necrotrophic pathogen whose destructive growth is typically studied under conditions that rapidly generate visible disease. Yet symptomless associations are well documented, the fungal and host processes operating before widespread tissue collapse remain poorly resolved. We developped an intact Arabidopsis thaliana seedling pathosystem that extends the interval between infection establishment and widespread visible disease. Fungal colonization was quantified using qPCR and a constitutively bioluminescent reporter strain, together with longitudinal plant phenotyping, hormone profiling and multiscale fluorescence microscopy. Intact seedlings therefore provide a powerful system for resolving plant-pathogen interactions that are largely obscured in rapid disease assays.

abstract

Although diverse pathogens have evolved effectors that suppress host RNA silencing, whether powdery mildew fungi exploit this strategy and the underlying molecular mechanisms remain unknown. 10.0 Beyond phosphate uptake: PT4/PT11 as key functional components linking phosphate acquisition, nutrient exchange, and arbuscule maintenance in arbuscular mycorrhizal symbiosis.
Yi X, Ma Z.
reviewCa2+Kinases and signal transductionHormonesFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-28
matched: calcium, signaling, hormone*, mycorrhiz*, symbio*, nutrient*, root, growth, development, single-cell, spatial transcriptom*
abstract

Phosphorus is an essential macronutrient for plant growth and development, yet its availability in many soils is limited by strong chemical fixation and low mobility. Arbuscular mycorrhizal (AM) symbiosis provides an important biological strategy for improving plant phosphate acquisition by establishing specialized arbuscules within root cortical cells, where reciprocal nutrient exchange occurs across the periarbuscular membrane (PAM). Among the phosphate transporter families involved in this process, the mycorrhiza-specific PHT1 transporters PT4 in Medicago truncatula and PT11 in Oryza sativa are major components of the mycorrhizal phosphate uptake pathway and are required for efficient phosphate acquisition and the maintenance of functional arbuscules. Genetic and physiological studies further indicate that disruption of PT4/PT11-mediated phosphate transport is closely associated with premature arbuscule degeneration and impaired symbiotic performance, highlighting a functional link between phosphate uptake, nutrient exchange, and arbuscule maintenance. In this review, we summarize current knowledge of the molecular mechanisms underlying PT4/PT11 function and regulation during AM symbiosis. We first compare mycorrhiza-specific phosphate transporters across plant species and discuss their localization, transport properties, and contributions to the mycorrhizal Pi uptake pathway. We then examine the multilayered regulatory network controlling PT4/PT11 expression, including the common symbiosis signaling pathway, calcium-dependent signaling, CYCLOPS-RAM1-centered transcriptional regulation, AP2/ERF-family regulators, hormone-associated regulation, and systemic phosphate-sensing pathways. Particular attention is given to the functional coupling between PT4/PT11-mediated phosphate uptake and other components of reciprocal nutrient exchange, including carbon and lipid transfer, proton extrusion, and arbuscule maintenance. We further discuss how impairment of phosphate transport through PT4/PT11 affects arbuscule integrity and the physiological conditions of the plant-fungus interface. Based on available genetic and physiological evidence, we propose a working model in which PT4/PT11-mediated phosphate uptake contributes to maintaining a favorable interfacial phosphate environment and efficient nutrient exchange, thereby supporting arbuscule function and longevity. Importantly, current evidence does not demonstrate that PT4/PT11 possess a transport-independent function or directly transmit signals through interactions with downstream signaling components. The mechanisms linking transporter activity, local phosphate status, nutrient exchange, and arbuscule maintenance therefore remain to be experimentally resolved. Finally, we highlight emerging approaches, including single-cell and spatial transcriptomics, proximity labeling, transport-defective mutants, and genome editing, that may distinguish transport-dependent from potential transport-independent functions of PT4/PT11. Understanding these mechanisms may provide a conceptual and practical foundation for exploiting AM symbiosis to improve phosphorus-use efficiency, reduce dependence on mineral phosphate fertilizers, and develop more sustainable crop production systems.

10.0 Transcriptional programs controlling sporulation in Phytophthora respond to nutrition, humidity, and cryptochrome-mediated light signals.
Vu AL, Harris TA, Judelson HS.
Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesPLoS pathogens · 2026-10-12
matched: signal transduction, pathogen*, Phytophthora, oomycete*, nutrient*, trafficking, vesicle*, root, leaf, leaves, germination, development
abstract

Many filamentous pathogens employ environmental signals to regulate sporulation, maximizing the likelihood that their spores survive to colonize a new host. In the oomycete Phytophthora, little is known of the transcriptional program that underlies sporangia formation and how it is influenced by environmental inputs such as light, humidity, and nutrients. By studying time courses of the potato blight pathogen P. infestans on artificial media and tomato leaves, we identified over 4000 genes up-regulated during sporulation. These are associated with signal transduction, cell structure, vesicle trafficking, metabolism, flagellar assembly, and other processes required for sporangium formation and germination. Most of their mRNAs remain abundant in mature sporangia. Media manipulation and metabolomic studies indicated that sporulation is triggered by nitrogen limitation, with asparagine and possibly other amino acids playing the main role. Light, which delays sporulation in P. infestans, apparently influences a later regulatory step since while many sporulation-induced transcripts oscillate with a day/night cycle, others are light-neutral. Both blue and red wavelengths result in the light response, which is likely mediated by a cryptochrome. One cryptochrome was shown to be required for sporulation based on gene silencing experiments. Humidity appears to regulate a late checkpoint, since while spore development requires high humidity, low humidity has only limited effects on sporulation-associated transcription. Comparative studies with P. capsici, which unlike P. infestans requires light for sporulation, yielded evidence of substantial rewiring of the transcriptional program between the species. The contrasting light responses within the genus correlate with whether species are primarily leaf colonizers such as P. infestans or root colonizers such as P. capsici.

9.5 A Blast Fungus Effector Targets a Tubby-like F-box Protein to Suppress Plant Immunity
Saado, I., Bennett … M. J.
preprintKinases and signal transductionHormonesCell biology and organellesbioRxiv · 2026-10-16
matched: signalling, jasmon*, JA, immun*, effector*, pathogen*, plasma membrane, nucleus
abstract

The blast fungus Magnaporthe oryzae is one of the most destructive pathogens of rice, secreting effector proteins that reprogram host cells to enable infection. Identifying plant targets of these fungal effectors is central to understanding how blast disease is established, but the targets and mechanisms of most effectors remain unknown. Here, we show that a Zinc finger (ZiF)-family effector, Tie1, targets the rice Tubby-like protein (TLP) family, which are membrane-associated plant immunity regulators whose exact functions have not been defined. Tie1 binds a range of rice TLPs and specifically engages with the Tubby-like domain of OsTLP7. abstract

abstract

8.5 An infection assay recapitulating Xanthomonas translucens pv. translucens natural entry into barley hydathodes
Jauneau, A., Cerutti … L. D.
preprintGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-13
matched: pathogen*, Xanthomonas, leaf, leaves, stoma*, guard cell*, xylem
abstract

Pathogenic bacteria navigate biological host landscapes to enter and establish infection. How Xanthomonas phytopathogenic bacteria enter specific plant tissues is believed to directly determine the systemic vs. local outcome of infection. Non-vascular Xanthomonas infect leaf mesophyll locally from stomatal pores, while vascular ones enter through hydathode water pores on the leaf margin before invading the xylem for systemic movement. The pathovar Xanthomonas translucens pv. translucens (Xtt) is associated with barley infection, causing both bacterial leaf streak and bacterial blight symptoms by colonizing both the mesophyll spaces through stomatal guard cells and the plant vasculature, respectively. How Xtt reaches the vasculature in barley leaves is yet to be established. In this study, we determined the anatomy of barley hydathodes. A protocol allowing efficient hydathode infection by Xtt was established and caused bacterial blight symptoms, closely mimicking the natural infection process. This study establishes Xtt as one of the few demonstrated hydathode pathogens.

8.5 Suppression of plant immune responses enhances Agrobacterium-mediated transient expression in Nicotiana benthamiana.
Yu K, Fen X, Ge B … Gaiping Z.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-14
matched: MAPK*, signaling, salicylic acid, immun*, pathogen*, defense, osmotic stress, CRISPR
abstract

Agrobacterium tumefaciens-mediated transient expression in Nicotiana benthamiana is widely used for recombinant protein production and gene-function studies. However, agroinfiltration can activate host stress and defense responses, thereby limiting heterologous protein accumulation. To distinguish Agrobacterium-associated transcriptional responses from effects of the infiltration procedure, we performed RNA sequencing (RNA-seq) using an infection group (GV3101 carrying empty p1300), a buffer-only mock-infiltration group (Mock), and untreated wild-type (WT) plants. Relative to the mock group, the infection group contained 3,529 upregulated and 2,487 downregulated differentially expressed genes (DEGs), with enrichment of defense- and stress-associated pathways, including "response to stress," "plant-pathogen interaction," and "plant MAPK signaling pathway". Together, these results indicate that host immune responses can constrain transient expression and identify EDS1, PTI5/PTI6, and salicylic acid (SA)-related signaling as useful genetic targets for improving N. benthamiana as a transient expression chassis.

abstract

8.0 N-glycosylation code diversifies paralogous chitinases to balance plant immunity and growth.
Li C, Lin XX, Tian L … Li JF.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesNature plants · 2026-10-05
matched: apoplast*, signalling, immun*, chitin, cell wall*, growth
abstract

Balancing immunity and growth is a fundamental challenge for plants. Surprisingly, fungal cell-wall chitin elicits robust immune responses without causing growth inhibition, yet constitutive intracellular chitin signalling severely impairs growth. How plants resolve this paradox remains unclear. Here we identify two chitin-inducible, paralogous apoplastic chitinases in Arabidopsis thaliana, MIC1 and MIC2, both possessing direct antifungal activity through chitin hydrolysis. MIC2 generates immunogenic chitin oligomers that activate chitin-triggered immunity, whereas MIC1 ultimately degrades chitin into non-immunogenic dimers and monomers, thereby dampening growth-inhibitory signalling. This functional divergence is dictated by Asn137 glycosylation in MIC1. Removing this N-glycan converts MIC1 into a MIC2-like enzyme. MIC1-like chitinases are widespread in plants, and Oryza sativa Osmic1 mutants exhibit enhanced fungal resistance without growth defects. Our work defines an N-glycosylation code that governs the functional specialization of paralogous chitinases and provides a conserved molecular target for breeding crops with improved fungal resistance and uncompromised growth.

Datta T, Shromona A, Kumar RS … Trivedi PK.

matched: immun*, pathogen*, defense, heavy metal*, cell wall*, lignin*
abstract

Plant microRNAs (miRNAs) function as critical regulators of various developmental and physiological processes by downregulating the expression of their target genes. miRNA-encoded peptides (miPEPs) represent an additional regulatory layer by enhancing the transcription of their cognate miRNAs. . Although miPEP408 has been implicated in heavy-metal tolerance through regulation of sulfur assimilation, its role in plant immunity remains unknown. abstract

Plant-pathogen interactions are strongly influenced by host developmental stage, yet many Arabidopsis thaliana-Botrytis cinerea assays remain centred on mature rosette leaves and rely on inoculum loads that may exceed those involved in a primary infection. We established a miniaturized in vitro A. thaliana-B. cinerea seedling pathosystem initiated with a very low conidial dose (nominally ~25 conidia per plate) compatible with both destructive molecular sampling and repeated, non-destructive RGB imaging. 7.0 Cell-surface redox regulation coordinates chemotropism and hyphal fusion for root colonization in a vascular wilt pathogen.
Guastaferro VM, Staiti A, Ranesi M … Turrà D.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesMicrobiological research · 2026-10-06
matched: H2O2, signalling, pathogen*, cell wall*, root, roots, leaves
abstract

Soilborne fungal pathogens must locate host roots, stably attach to their surface and assemble a persistent mycelial network before invading the plant, yet how host perception is coupled to these cellular outputs remains poorly understood. Here, we characterize HAM-7, a GPI-anchored cell wall protein of Fusarium oxysporum and orthologue of the Neurospora crassa cell wall integrity sensor, and show that it is required for two mechanistically distinct processes essential for root colonization. Targeted deletion of ham-7 strongly impaired stable mycelial retention on tomato roots while leaving germling adhesion to abiotic surfaces and biofilm formation unaffected, indicating a defect in fusion-dependent cohesion rather than in surface adhesion. This defect is coupled with a complete loss of vegetative hyphal fusion (23% WT vs. 0% ham-7Δ), preventing the formation of interconnected mycelial networks that promote stable retention of fungal biomass on the root surface. In parallel, ham-7Δ mutants are severely defective in chemotropism toward plant peroxidases but retain intact responses to synthetic α-pheromone, revealing a specific requirement for HAM-7 upstream of the redox-dependent branch of host cue perception. Consistent with this, ham-7Δ mutants constitutively downregulate nox genes under basal conditions and fail to upregulate noxB and noxR upon exposure to tomato root exudates. Accordingly, exogenous H2O2 fully rescues the chemotropism defect but leaves the fusion deficiency intact, demonstrating that HAM-7 acts upstream of two separable downstream pathways. Combined, these defects significantly attenuate virulence on tomato plants, identifying HAM-7 as an upstream actor of Nox-dependent signalling.

Ferrusquía-Jiménez NI, Martínez-Camacho JE, Godínez-Mendoza PL … Guevara-González RG.

abstract

The use of microorganisms as biological control agents (BCAs) in agriculture has regained prominence in response to escalating concerns over environmental pollution, human health, and the global food security crisis. Trichoderma species promote plant growth, improve nutrient utilization efficiency and enhance plant resistance to mention a few. Trichoderma asperellum, is a highly effective fungal biocontrol agent, recognized for its high adaptability to different environments. 7.0 Machine Learning Identification of Functional Trait Syndromes Associated with Responsiveness to Arbuscular Mycorrhizal Fungi
Shah, T., Errahmouni … M.
preprintAbiotic stressGrowthMethodsbioRxiv · 2026-10-06
matched: mycorrhiz*, symbio*, nutrient*, root, leaf, growth, machine learning
abstract

Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) are widespread plant symbionts that enhance nutrient acquisition, growth, and stress tolerance, yet plant responsiveness to AMF varies substantially and remains difficult to predict. We developed Trait2Myco, a Random Forest based machine-learning framework that integrates plant functional traits and environmental variables to predict mycorrhizal growth response (MGR). The framework was trained using 80 observations spanning four grass species grown under four precipitation regimes. Predictor variables included leaf and root economic traits together with precipitation, while total MGR served as the response variable. A full model incorporating ten functional traits and precipitation was trained using 80% of observations and evaluated on an independent testing dataset (20%, n = 16). The full model explained 41% of variation in MGR (R² = 0.41), whereas a reduced ecological model containing only specific leaf area, specific root area, root carbon concentration, and precipitation improved predictive performance and explained 51.7% of variation (R² = 0.517). Ten-fold cross-validation of the full model yielded an R² of 0.983, RMSE of 0.046, and MAE of 0.036. Variable importance analysis identified specific leaf area, precipitation, root carbon concentration, and specific root area as the strongest predictors of responsiveness. Partial dependence analysis revealed nonlinear relationships between these predictors and MGR, while principal component analysis showed that highly responsive plants occupied distinct regions of functional trait space characterized by resource-acquisitive trait syndromes. Leaf and root traits contributed nearly equally to model performance, accounting for 41.9% and 41.3% of cumulative importance, respectively, while environmental factors contributed 16.8%. An exploratory analysis using an independent AMF colonization dataset produced similarly strong predictive relationships, suggesting that trait-based machine-learning approaches may generalize across multiple AMF-associated responses. Trait2Myco provides a reproducible framework for identifying plant trait syndromes associated with responsiveness to AMF and offers a practical tool for predicting mycorrhizal benefits across environmental gradients. 7.0 Salinity-adapted and core microbiota along the soil salinity gradients encircling the Tarim Basin.
Liu X, Jia Q, Tang Q … Zhu J.
Ca2+Abiotic stressEnvironmental microbiome · 2026-07-22
matched: Ca2+, microbiome*, microbiota, salinity
abstract

BackgroundSoil salinization affects nearly one-tenth of global drylands and imposes strong environmental filters on microbial communities. The Tarim Basin in Xinjiang, China, is one of the world's largest inland basins and an extreme saline-arid ecosystem, yet a basin-scale understanding of its microbiota remains lacking. In this study, we collected 132 soil samples along a salinity gradient spanning more than 3,000 km encircling the Tarim Basin. By combining amplicon sequencing with measurements of soil physicochemical properties, we aimed to unravel the salinity-adapted microbiota and core microbial taxa characteristic of this extreme environment.ResultsOur results showed that bacterial communities, rather than fungal communities, were strongly shaped by soil salinity. Extremely saline soils exhibited reduced bacterial diversity and were enriched in halophilic and halotolerant taxa, including Natronomonas, Salinimicrobium, Stenotrophomonas, Salinibacter, Halorussus, and Halomicrobium. In contrast, taxa such as Arthrobacter, Rubrobacter, Rubellimicrobium, and Fusarium declined with increasing salinity. Soil cation concentrations (Na+, K+, Ca2+, Mg2+), major anions (Cl-, SO42-), and electrical conductivity all increased significantly along the salinity gradient. Despite this strong environmental filtering, we identified a set of potential core microbiota consistently present across all soils, dominated by Halomonas, Salinimicrobium, Natronomonas, Rubrobacter, and members of the Palleronia-Pseudomaribius group. Their persistent occurrence across the basin suggests that these taxa play key roles in sustaining ecosystem functions within the extreme saline-arid environments of the Tarim Basin.ConclusionsOur basin-scale analysis reveals that soil salinity significantly contributes to microbial community assembly in the Tarim Basin, selecting for specialized halophilic and halotolerant taxa while reducing overall bacterial diversity. The discovery of core microbiota with known salt-adaptation mechanisms highlights the presence of a stable microbial backbone across heterogeneous saline landscapes. These findings provide new ecological insights into how extreme salinity shapes microbiome structure and identify candidate microbial groups potentially contributing to ecosystem stability and plant stress resilience in arid saline environments.

6.5 Isoforms of cysteine peptidases and osmotins instead of chitinases and peptidase inhibitors drive antifungal activity but occur as distinct isoforms in latex and callus tissues of Calotropis procera.
Ramos MV, Silva RF, Carvalho CPS … Freitas CDT.
Ca2+Cell biology and organellesGrowthPlanta · 2026-10-11
matched: reactive oxygen species, immun*, pathogen*, defense, plasma membrane, germination, growth
abstract

Main conclusionPeptidases and osmotins are the primary determinants of antifungal activity, and their expression is tightly linked to tissue specialization in Calotropis procera plants. This study investigated whether callus cultures of two Apocynaceae species, Calotropis procera and Cryptostegia grandiflora, reproduce the antifungal defense system typically found in their latex. Protein fractions obtained from latex and callus tissues were evaluated for antifungal activity against Fusarium solani, F. oxysporum, Colletotrichum lindemuthianum, and C. gloeosporioides, as well as the presence of pathogenesis-related (PR) proteins was investigated. Callus proteins from C. procera (CPCp) exhibited antifungal activity, inhibiting both mycelial growth and spore germination of all tested fungi. In contrast, callus proteins from C. grandiflora (CPCg) showed no detectable antifungal activity. Mechanistic assays demonstrated that CPCp induced plasma membrane permeabilization and the accumulation of reactive oxygen species in fungal spores. Biochemical analyses further revealed that antifungal activity was associated with the presence of peptidases and osmotins but not of peptidase inhibitors and chitinases. Immunological recognition and gene expression analyses indicated that the peptidases and osmotins expressed in callus tissues differ from those present in the latex of C. procera, suggesting the occurrence of distinct protein isoforms. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that peptidases and osmotins are key determinants of antifungal activity in latex plants and reveal that their expression is closely linked to tissue specialization, highlighting the differential regulation of defense protein isoforms between latex and other plant tissues.
