6.5 The RpfF Acyl-ACP thioesterase of major rice pathogen Xanthomonas oryzae pv. oryzae is essential for maintenance of membrane function and virulence.
Luo L, Hu Z, Yan M … Wang H.
GrowthPLoS pathogens · 2026-10-06
matched: pathogen*, Xanthomonas, growth
abstract

In Xanthomonas, RpfF is a bifunctional enzyme having both enoyl-ACP dehydratase and acyl-ACP thioesterase activities catalyzed by a single active site. While the hydratase activity is essential for producing quorum-sensing signals of the diffusible signal factor (DSF) family, the physiological role of the thioesterase activity remained unclear. Singh and coworkers have reported that deletion of the gene encoding RpfF (a ∆rpfF strain) of the rice pathogen X. oryzae pv. oryzae (Xoo) resulted in loss of membrane integrity (Singh et al, 2022). We report that the loss in membrane integrity is due to lack of the RpfF thioesterase activity. This was demonstrated by restoration of membrane integrity in the ΔrpfF strain by expression of a foreign thioesterase. Thioesterases originating in either E. coli or a plant enzyme fully restored virulence in rice, bacterial growth, and tolerance of membrane stress. The thioesterase overcame the defects in membrane lipid synthesis reported previously and restored the lipid components to wild-type levels. Radiolabeling revealed that fatty acid synthesis in the ΔrpfF strain proceeded at only one-third of the wild-type rate, which was rescued by complementation with thioesterase encoding genes. Thus, the acyl-ACP thioesterase activity of RpfF is a key regulator of de novo fatty acid synthesis and membrane integrity. We propose a dual-function model wherein RpfF maintains membrane lipid homeostasis via its thioesterase activity while concurrently producing DSF signals via its hydratase activity to orchestrate quorum sensing.

6.5 The homologous GRAS transcription factors RAM1 and RAD1 coordinate shared and separate gene networks to regulate arbuscular mycorrhiza symbiosis
Zeng T, Alpers J, Boniolo F … Gutjahr C
Abiotic stressGrowthNew Phytologist · 2026-10-16
matched: mycorrhiz*, symbio*, nutrient*, root, roots, growth, development
abstract

Summary Arbuscular mycorrhiza (AM), a symbiosis between plants and Glomeromycotan fungi is based on the exchange of nutrients, which majorly occurs between fungal tree‐shaped arbuscules and root cortex cells. Two homologous plant GRAS transcription factors, RAM1 and RAD1, were previously reported to interact, and both were implicated in regulating arbuscule branching in Lotus japonicus . We revisited the role of L. japonicus RAM1 and RAD1 and asked whether they act together in mediating arbuscule development and gene expression, or whether they act additively. We performed comprehensive genetic, phenotypic, and transcriptome analyses of two allelic Lotus ram1 , rad1 , and ram1 rad1 mutants; and of roots ectopically expressing RAM1 and/or RAD1. We found that RAD1 is required for full root colonization in a RAM1 ‐dependent manner, but in our growth conditions, RAD1 is not required for arbuscule branching. In addition, RAM1 and RAD1 are required for activation of partially common and partially distinct sets of downstream genes. Thus, in contrast to RAM1 , Lotus RAD1 is not essential for arbuscule branching. Furthermore, RAM1 and RAD1 seem to act partially synergistically but seem to participate in additional alternative transcription factor complexes to regulate AM‐induced gene expression and AM development.

6.5 The root knot nematode effector MiEFF186 interacts with a splicing factor to reshape plant transcriptome
Ranty-Roby, S., Mulet … M.
preprintCell biology and organellesGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-14
matched: effector*, nucleus, root, roots, development
abstract

The root-knot nematode Meloidogyne incognita secretes specific effectors to manipulate root cells and induce their redifferentiation into multinucleate feeding giant cells, which are essential for its development. A dedicated screen identified the M. incognita MiEFF186a effector as a regulator of alternative splicing, a crucial mechanism of transcriptional gene regulation whereby a single gene can produce multiple mature mRNA variants, in plants. Together, these results identify GDP1/CIS2 as a host target of MiEFF186a and support a role for these proteins in the modulation of host RNA metabolism during nematode parasitism.

6.0 Effects of winter waterlogging on apple root and soil summer microbiome
McLean H, Mikaberidze A, Deakin G … Papp-Rupar M.
preprintAbiotic stressGrowthTrees and woody plantsResearch Square · 2026-10-07
matched: pathogen*, microbiome*, microbiota, nutrient*, root, growth, trees
abstract

Abstract Background Winter waterlogging is an increasingly common abiotic stress factor in temperate horticulture. While the physiological responses of apple ( Malus × domestica ) to waterlogging during the active growing season have been investigated, the impact of waterlogging on the soil and root microbiome, especially during the winter dormant period, remains unknown. This study investigated the legacy effects of winter waterlogging on apple root and soil microbiota 6–9 months after treatment. Potted MM106 rootstocks were subjected to four weeks of continuous waterlogging in December across two independent experimental replicates, with one cohort subjected to two waterlogging treatments over two consecutive winters. Fungal (ITS) and bacterial (16S rRNA gene) communities were assessed using amplicon sequencing to determine community size, diversity, structure, and taxonomic responses to waterlogging. Results Results indicated that community assembly was primarily driven by experimental replicate and temporal succession rather than the waterlogging treatment. However, a specific anaerobic legacy was identified. The anaerobic bacterial genera Geomonas , Geobacter , and Desulfuromonas were consistently enriched across experiments, with an increase in the abundance of Geomonas associated with single and repeated waterlogging events. Conversely, aerobic saprotrophs were depleted, and potential fungal pathogens, Gibellulopsis and Plectosphaerella , were also depleted. Conclusions These findings suggest that winter waterlogging induces a stable shift in the microbiota characterised by a persistent anaerobic bacterial signature and a simplified fungal community. This legacy effect may influence nutrient cycling and stress resilience when trees resume active growth in the spring.

6.0 Nucleocytoplasmic trafficking of immune signals in plants.
Dou W, Xu F, Cai X, Wang S.
reviewKinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesGrowthPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-05
matched: signaling, immun*, defense, nuclear, trafficking, development
abstract

The nuclear pore complex (NPC) was initially viewed as a passive gateway for molecular transport, but it is now recognized as an active and highly selective signaling hub that orchestrates nucleocytoplasmic communication. In plants, dissection of the NPC's intricate architecture has revealed its critical role in innate immunity: specific nucleoporins selectively gate the nuclear entry of key immune regulators, while plant‑specific components dynamically control pore permeability or form phase‑separated condensates that assemble specialized transcription factories. Strikingly, the NPC recruits cell cycle, RNA processing, and chromatin‑modifying complexes into higher‑order "complexes" that coordinate defense activation with development, RNA metabolism, and epigenetic memory. This emerging paradigm positions the NPC as a central integration platform, opening new opportunities for engineering disease‑resistant crops.

6.0 Pine wilt disease as a holobiont disorder: microbiome assembly as a potential management target
Pires D, Espada M, Proença D, Vicente C.
preprintTrees and woody plantsEcoEvoRxiv · 2026-10-07
matched: pathogen*, microbiome*, pine
abstract

Pine wilt disease (PWD), caused by the pinewood nematode (PWN) Bursaphelenchus xylophilus, continues to expand globally despite decades of pathogen-focused management. Here, we propose that disruption of microbiome assembly within the pine holobiont may contribute to disease progression and help explain variation in disease outcomes and responses to microbial interventions. Drawing on recent advances in microbial ecology, multi-omics, and synthetic biology, we synthesize evidence of PWD-associated changes in host-associated microbial communities and examine how altered functional redundancy and pathobiome formation may contribute to disease progression. We propose that microbiome assembly, shaped by host traits, environmental filters, and biotic interactions, is a plausible and underexplored determinant of disease outcome. Building on this framework, we outline a shift from single-agent biocontrol toward assembly-informed microbiome engineering. To support reproducible design and future validation, we outline criteria for selecting candidate consortium members and assessing synthetic microbial community (SynCom) establishment, persistence and functional stability. We further propose experimentally testable strategies to compare assembly-informed versus conventional interventions and identify measurable indicators of microbiome stability and disease suppression. By reframing PWD as a problem of microbiome assembly and stability, this work provides a testable framework for developing durable, holobiont-level management strategies. Integrating ecological theory with applied microbiome engineering offers a path forward to move beyond inconsistent control outcomes and toward resilient forest health under ongoing environmental change.

18.5 Endophytic Aspergillus niger and Aspergillus oryzae improve growth, antioxidant defense, and ion homeostasis in maize under salinity stress.
Rahman KU, Rauf M, Din JU … Ali S.
Ca2+HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-11
matched: Ca2+, Ca²⁺, IAA, endophyt*, defense, salt stress, salinity, NaCl, root, root growth, stoma*, shoot*
abstract

Salt stress limits maize productivity worldwide, and sustainable strategies are required for stress alleviation. The current study assessed the ability of endophytic fungi Aspergillus niger (MA2) and Aspergillus oryzae (MA5), isolated from Moringa oleifera seeds and identified by rDNA sequencing (GenBank: PV616998, PV616999), to mitigate salinity stress in maize. Both the isolates exhibited considerable production of indole-3-acetic acid, proline, phenolics, flavonoids, and sugars, which was enhanced under saline conditions. The application of 200 mM NaCl significantly (p ≤ 0.05) reduced shoot/root growth, biomass, and photosynthetic pigments, depleted the endogenous IAA and gibberellic acid, disrupted the ionic balance (elevated Na⁺/Cl⁻; reduced K⁺, Ca²⁺, Mg²⁺, N, P), and increased oxidative damage (MDA, H₂O₂) with significant increases in osmolytes, antioxidant enzymes, and secondary metabolites. Inoculation with MA2 and MA5, confirmed by endophytic colonization and stomatal assessment, successfully restored photosynthetic pigments, growth parameters, and hormonal balance, optimized metabolite and antioxidant profiles, and reduced MDA compared to non-inoculated stressed controls. Fungal association also restored ionic homeostasis by reducing Na+/Cl- uptake and increasing K+, Ca2+, Mg2+, N, and P accumulation, thus restoring Na+/K+ and Na+/Ca2+ ratios important for salt tolerance. Principal component analysis (PC1 = 62.40% and PC2 = 24.81%) confirmed these physiological and biochemical changes with clear separation of inoculated and non-inoculated treatments. Collectively, MA2 and MA5 act as powerful biostimulants that promote maize salt tolerance through coordinated physicochemical, hormonal, and ionic regulation, offering a promising strategy to improve crop resilience under saline conditions.

13.5 Pepper RING ‐type E3 ligase, CaANKR1 , negatively regulates drought tolerance by modulating CaMAPK1 stability
Cho J, Lim CW, Yun B … Lee SC
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesCell biology and organellesThe Plant Journal · 2026-10
matched: kinase*, MAPK*, signaling, ubiquitin*, abscisic acid, ABA, salinity, osmotic stress, drought, nucleus, stoma*, growth
abstract

SUMMARY Drought stress severely restricts plant growth and productivity, and abscisic acid (ABA)–mediated signaling plays a central role in coordinating adaptive responses to water deficit. Mitogen‐activated protein kinase (MAPK) cascades are widely involved in plant stress signaling; however, the ubiquitin‐mediated regulation of MAPK stability during drought responses remains poorly understood. In this study, we conducted a yeast two‐hybrid screening and identified CaMAPK1, a pepper ( Capsicum annuum ) MAPK, as an interacting partner of the RING‐type E3 ubiquitin ligase CaANKR1, which was further confirmed via in vitro and in vivo assays. CaMAPK1 expression was induced by dehydration, osmotic stress, salinity stress, and ABA treatment. Furthermore, CaMAPK1 exhibited kinase activity and was predominantly localized to the nucleus. Ubiquitination assays revealed that CaANKR1 promoted K48‐linked polyubiquitination of CaMAPK1 in a RING domain–dependent manner. Consistently, cell‐free degradation assays showed that CaMAPK1 protein stability negatively correlated with CaANKR1 expression levels, indicating that CaANKR1 promotes the proteasome‐dependent degradation of CaMAPK1. Phenotypic assays revealed that CaMAPK1 silencing in pepper reduced drought tolerance by altering ABA‐mediated stomatal responses and water retention, whereas CaMAPK1 overexpression in Arabidopsis produced the opposite phenotype. Collectively, these findings suggest that CaANKR1 negatively modulates ABA‐mediated drought tolerance through ubiquitin‐dependent destabilization of CaMAPK1. Together, they reveal a mechanism underlying ABA‐driven drought responses and highlight a promising target for enhancing crop drought resilience.

12.0 CYSTM1, a drought-responsive cysteine-rich transmembrane module protein, negatively affects drought tolerance in Arabidopsis thaliana
Shoaib, Y., Geyer … F.
preprintHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-19
matched: abscisic acid, ABA, effector*, salinity, osmotic stress, drought, stoma*, seed*, growth
abstract

Drought stress represents a major challenge for plant growth and survival, requiring coordinated regulation of physiological and molecular responses to maintain water balance. The cysteine-rich transmembrane module (CYSTM) family comprises a highly conserved group of small proteins whose functions in plant stress adaptation remain largely unknown. Here, we identified CYSTM1 (AT1G05340) as a drought-responsive gene in Arabidopsis thaliana whose expression increased in response to prolonged water deficit, osmotic stress, high salinity, and abscisic acid (ABA). Analysis of a loss-of-function T-DNA insertion mutant (cystm1) showed no growth phenotype compared to the wild-type under well-watered conditions. 12.0 Melatonin-mediated cross-stress protection in tomato under individual and combined salinity and drought stress.
Huang H, Zhang H, Zeng Q, Wu J.
Ca2+GrowthPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-09-29
matched: ROS, H2O2, hydrogen peroxide, salinity, NaCl, drought, leaf, growth
abstract

Tomato production is increasingly threatened by the co-occurrence of salinity and drought, but the protective mechanisms of melatonin under their combination are not fully understood. This study investigated whether foliar-applied melatonin could mitigate the individual and combined effects of salinity and drought in tomato (Solanum lycopersicum). Plants were exposed to 0 or 100 mM NaCl and maintained at 90 or 60% field capacity. Melatonin (100 μM) was sprayed three times at five-day intervals, beginning one day before stress application, and plants were sampled after three weeks. Salinity and drought separately reduced growth, leaf water status, photosynthetic pigments, photosystem II (PSII) efficiency, gas exchange, and K status, while their combination generally caused the greatest inhibition. Combined stress also produced the greatest Na/K imbalance and the highest accumulation of hydrogen peroxide (H2O2), methylglyoxal, and malondialdehyde (MDA). Melatonin improved growth and photosynthetic performance under all stress conditions; under combined stress, it increased total dry weight, relative water content, and K by approximately 37%, 18.6%, and 50.2%, respectively, and reduced Na and MDA by 39.8% and 39.9%, respectively, relative to untreated stressed plants. These improvements were associated with higher antioxidant and glyoxalase activities, recovery of ascorbate/dehydroascorbate and reduced/oxidized glutathione redox balance, and coordinated changes in anthocyanin biosynthesis, phenolic metabolism, proline, glycine betaine, free amino acids, and soluble sugars. The protective response was especially evident under combined stress, whereas melatonin had little effect under normal conditions. Overall, melatonin provided stress-dependent protection by linking ion homeostasis, ROS and methylglyoxal detoxification, redox recycling, and osmotic and secondary metabolism. These findings support foliar melatonin as a promising approach for improving tomato tolerance to co-occurring salinity and drought.

abstract

Salt stress adversely affects seed germination and lipid mobilization in germinating sunflower seedlings. Notably, recovery in the rate of lipolysis is crucial to promote stress tolerance, thereby imparting longevity to the seedlings. Melatonin has been previously reported to regulate lipolysis in NaCl-stressed sunflower seedling cotyledons. While hemin (oxidized form of heme) is known to function as a metabolic regulator in plants, no substantial reports are available to understand the combined impact of melatonin and hemin application in the regulation of lipid mobilization during seed germination. The present work hypothesizes that melatonin and hemin co-application escalates lipolysis thereby accompanying changes in proteolytic activity and H2S metabolism in NaCl-stressed sunflower seedling cotyledons. Seedlings exhibited reduced activity of lipolytic enzymes (fatty acyl-ester hydrolase, lipoxygenase, and phospholipase) accompanying a slower oil body mobilization under salinity stress. To sum up, the present work bears agronomic importance for the regulatory role of melatonin and hemin in modulating lipid mobilization and salt-sensitivity of oil seed crops.

11.0 Spatial metabolomics reveals brassinosteroid-mediated compartmentalization of saikosaponin biosynthesis in Bupleurum chinense DC. under abiotic stress.
Yang L, Zhou S, Gu X … Dong C.
Ca2+HormonesGrowthPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-09
matched: H2O2, brassinosteroid*, salinity, NaCl, drought, root, xylem, phloem, seed*, growth
abstract

Although the biosynthesis of active ingredients in medicinal plants is co-regulated by diverse environmental stimuli, the endocrine mechanism by which brassinosteroids (BRs) coordinate physiological resilience with the in situ spatial distribution of saikosaponins in Bupleurum chinense DC. under combined drought and salinity remains largely elusive. We hypothesized that exogenous brassinosteroids (BRs) application alleviates oxidative impairment provoked by single and combined osmotic/ionic stresses while orchestrating the tissue-level topological routing of saikosaponin biosynthesis. Here, B. chinense seedlings were exposed to drought (15% PEG6000), salinity (150 mM NaCl), and combined stress with or without root-applied BRs (0.2 mg/L). Plant growth, foliar cytomembrane integrity, antioxidant enzyme kinetics, and key transcriptional profiles were evaluated and integrated with matrix-assisted laser desorption/ionization mass spectrometry imaging (MALDI-MSI)-based spatial metabolomics. Spatial metabolomics demonstrated that common precursors (IPP, FPP, and 2,3-oxidosqualene) displayed an ubiquitous distribution across the whole root cross-section (cork, cortex, phloem, and xylem), whereas bioactive saikosaponins (SS-a, SS-c, SS-e, SS-f, and acetyl-SS-a) were preferentially compartmentalized in the secondary phloem and periderm. Exogenous BRs dynamically intensified precursor flux toward the outer cortical/phloem sink under single stress regimes. In summary, this work systematically characterizes BRs-regulated spatial patterns of saikosaponins under combined stress. It offers theoretical evidence for decoding stress adaptation mechanisms of B. chinense and provides practical support for optimizing its ecological cultivation techniques.

abstract

Collectively, our findings support a regulatory framework in which ANAC087 coordinates processes that limit symplastic Cd accumulation and promote apoplastic Cd sequestration.

9.5 Chitosan Nanoparticles Alleviate Salinity-Induced Growth Inhibition in in Vitro Shoot Cultures of Tobacco (Nicotiana tabacum L.).

matched: hydrogen peroxide, salinity, NaCl, osmotic stress, stress respon*, shoot*, growth
abstract

Salinity stress is a major environmental constraint that limits plant growth and productivity by disrupting ionic balance, inducing osmotic stress, and promoting oxidative damage. Chitosan (Ch), a naturally derived biopolymer, enhances plant tolerance to abiotic stresses, but its effectiveness may be limited by poor solubility and reduced interaction with plant tissues. Chitosan nanoparticles (ChNP) may overcome these limitations through improved bioavailability and cellular interaction. This study evaluated the effectiveness of Ch and ChNP in alleviating salinity stress in in vitro shoot cultures of tobacco (Nicotiana tabacum L.), a valuable model system. Furthermore, ChNP increased chlorophyll a, chlorophyll b, total chlorophyll, and proline contents by 77.9%, 167.5%, 86.8% and 27%, respectively. These findings demonstrate that ChNP effectively enhanced salinity tolerance by strengthening physiological and biochemical stress-response mechanisms in tobacco under in vitro conditions.

9.5 Chronic heavy metal exposure drives mitochondrial remodeling and metabolic reprogramming to enhance sperm velocity in wild tree sparrows.
Zhang M, Miao Y, Su R … Zhang Y.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesJournal of hazardous materials · 2026-10-10
matched: membrane potential, phosphoryl*, heavy metal*, cadmium, mitochondri*, tree
abstract

Environmental pollution is widely assumed to impair reproductive performance, yet whether organisms can sustain reproductive function under chronic stress remains poorly understood. Here, using natural populations of tree sparrows from a heavy metal-contaminated site and a reference site, we reveal a paradoxical increase in sperm velocity despite clear physiological costs. Males from the polluted population exhibited reduced body condition and elevated cadmium and lead accumulation in reproductive tissues, but their sperm velocity parameters were significantly enhanced. Mechanistically, sperm from exposed birds showed coordinated mitochondrial optimization, including elevated mitochondrial membrane potential, enhanced complex I activity, and higher ATP content. In the testis, genes associated with mitochondrial biogenesis (TFAM) and mitophagy (BNIP3) were upregulated, indicating enhanced mitochondrial quality control during spermatogenesis. Functional inhibition using the oxidative phosphorylation uncoupler carbonyl cyanide 4-(trifluoromethoxy) phenylhydrazone (FCCP) confirmed that elevated sperm velocity depends on mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation (OXPHOS). Integrated proteomic and metabolomic analyses further revealed a systemic metabolic reprogramming from glycolysis toward OXPHOS, characterized by upregulation of OXPHOS-related proteins and suppression of glycolytic pathways. Together, our findings demonstrate that chronic heavy metal stress can enhance sperm performance through mitochondrial and metabolic remodeling, revealing a key mechanism by which avian reproductive behavior responds to long-term environmental stress.

8.0 Salt Tolerance Variation among Chenopodium quinoa Accessions of Contrasting Geographic Origins: Germination Performance and Antioxidant Defense under NaCl Stress.
Arraouadi S, Slimani N, Hajlaoui H.
Ca2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthPakistan journal of biological sciences : PJBS · 2026-10-01
matched: H2O2, hydrogen peroxide, defense, salinity, NaCl, osmotic stress, germination
abstract

Background and Objective: Chenopodium quinoa Willd. is a strategic crop for food security in salt-affected regions; however, genotype-specific physiological responses to salinity during early germination remain insufficiently characterized. Understanding variation among geographically distinct accessions is essential for identifying salt-tolerant genotypes. This study aimed to evaluate germination performance and antioxidant defense responses of three quinoa accessions of contrasting geographic origins under salinity stress. Materials and Methods: Three quinoa accessions 18GR (Ames 13724, USA), R-132 (PI 478418, Bolivia) and DE-1 (PI 674266, Ecuador) obtained from the USDA National Plant Germplasm System were evaluated under four NaCl concentrations (0, 100, 200 and 300 mM) under controlled laboratory conditions. Germination percentage, mean germination time (MGT), radicle length and germination kinetics were recorded. Reversibility assays were conducted to differentiate osmotic and ionic effects of salinity. Oxidative stress markers, including Hydrogen Peroxide (H2O2) and malondialdehyde (MDA), were quantified along with antioxidant enzyme activities such as peroxidase (POD), catalase (CAT) and ascorbate peroxidase (APX). Proline accumulation was also assessed as an indicator of osmotic adjustment. Results: Salinity significantly affected germination traits in an accession-dependent manner. At 300 mM NaCl, germination rates were 90, 60 and 33.33% for 18GR, R-132 and DE-1, respectively. Reversibility assays indicated osmotic stress predominance at 200 mM, while ionic toxicity dominated at higher concentrations. NaCl stress increased H2O2 and MDA accumulation in all accessions. POD activity increased consistently across treatments. CAT and APX activities in 18GR showed a biphasic response, being stimulated up to 200 mM and inhibited at 300 mM, indicating stress threshold regulation. Proline accumulation increased with salinity, with 18GR showing the highest osmotic adjustment capacity. Conclusion: Geographic origin significantly influences salt tolerance during germination in quinoa. Accession 18GR exhibited superior stress tolerance and antioxidant regulation, making it a promising candidate for saline agriculture. The contrasting biochemical responses provide a physiological basis for selecting salt-tolerant quinoa genotypes adapted to arid and semi-arid environments.

8.0 The loss of GOX1 and GOX2 function differentially modulates proteomic reprogramming in response to short-term salinity in Arabidopsis.

abstract

Salt stress is a major environmental constraint limiting plant growth and development. Although it induces significant changes in the plant proteome, the regulatory mechanisms underlying these responses remain poorly understood. In this study, a Tandem Mass Tag (TMT)-based proteomic approach was used to investigate the role of glycolate oxidases (GOX1 and GOX2) in regulating the shoot proteome of Arabidopsis thaliana under short-term salt stress. Wild-type (WT) plants and two T-DNA insertion mutants, gox1 and gox2, were exposed to salt treatment for 24 h. A total of 3607 non-redundant proteins were identified. Benczúr K, Gholizadeh F, Pál M … Szalai G.

matched: hormone*, salt stress, salinity, stress respon*, roots, leaves, stems
abstract

Key messageCold acclimation of young maize plants induced a light-dependent, organ-specific reprogramming at the metabolomic and gene expression levels, which significantly altered responses to salinity. It has been shown that light plays a decisive role in cold acclimation in plants. The core objective of the present work was to elucidate how light conditions during cold acclimation affect subsequent salt stress responses by co-regulating various processes among organs maize (Zea mays L.). Plants were cold-acclimated under sufficient light or shade and subsequently exposed to severe cold or salinity. Organ-specific analyses of leaves, stems, and roots combined chlorophyll fluorescence measurements with targeted and untargeted metabolomics, hormone and polyamine profiling, and stress-related gene expression were studied. Cold acclimation under sufficient light established organ-specific metabolic and transcriptional reprogramming that substantially altered subsequent salt stress responses. Light-acclimated plants showed coordinated, pathway-level metabolic adjustments across organs and distinct regulation of key stress-responsive genes, particularly in leaves and roots. Analyses indicated systemic, rather than isolated, metabolic rearrangements. These findings demonstrate that cold acclimation, depending on light intensity, establishes an organ-coordinated acclimated state that modulates subsequent salinity responses, highlighting the importance of environmental history and inter-organ regulation in stress adaptation. This study is the first to systematically reveal the organ-specific and systemic regulatory network underlying the light-temperature-salt interaction in maize.

7.0 Chicken manure and cow manure regulate phytoremediation of cadmium-contaminated alkaline soils through distinct mechanisms.
Mi L, Qin X, Huang Q … Sun Y.
Ca2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthEnvironmental geochemistry and health · 2026-10-09
matched: pH, defense, heavy metal*, cadmium, root, growth, phytoremediation
abstract

Phytoremediation can completely remove harmful heavy metals from soil; however, it is limited by low efficiency and long cycling times. In this study, to further improve the efficiency of phytoremediation of alkaline cadmium (Cd)-contaminated soils, we investigated the enhanced Cd uptake capacity of Tagetes patula L. through manure application and explored its underlying mechanisms. The results indicate that chicken manure significantly increased soil available Cd through pH reduction, thereby enhancing Cd enrichment in T. patula. In contrast, cow manure did not alter soil pH or available Cd. However, it substantially promoted plant biomass, leading to increased total Cd uptake primarily via a biomass-amplification mechanism. Both manures improved plant physiological status and altered root metabolomic profiles, upregulating Cd-related defense pathways. They also enriched beneficial soil microbial taxa (e.g. Bacteroidota and Patescibacteria) and enhanced soil enzyme activity. Overall, livestock manures can enhance phytoremediation of Cd-contaminated alkaline soils but via distinct mechanisms: chicken manure acts through Cd mobilization and direct uptake, whereas cow manure relies on growth promotion and improved plant tolerance. These findings provide a theoretical basis and application value for optimizing manure-assisted phytoremediation strategies: chicken manure is suitable for short-term Cd removal scenarios, while cow manure is better suited for long-term soil remediation combined with sustainable agricultural practices.

7.0 Glutathione and moringa leaf extract improve wheat tolerance to heavy metal-contaminated saline soil by regulation of antioxidant defense system and nutrient balance.
Alamri AM, Rehman HU, Khalid F … Rady MM.
Ca2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthPlant cell reports · 2026-10-13
matched: Ca2+, defense, salinity, nutrient*, heavy metal*, leaf, leaves, seed*, growth
abstract

Key messageIntegrated application of MLE and GSH mitigated salinity and HM stress in wheat via enhancement of antioxidant defense system, nutrient balance, and reduced sodium toxicity, and thereby improved crop performance and grain nutritional quality. Salinity obstructs the uptake of essential minerals by simultaneously increasing heavy metal (HM) concentration to affect the growth, yield, and grain nutritional quality in crop plants. This study evaluated the potential of moringa leaf extract (MLE; 3%) and glutathione (GSH; 1 mM) applied in sequence with H2O as a seed priming (SP) and foliar spray (FS) on antioxidant enzymes activity, GSH, and protein levels, leaf macronutrients as well leaf and grain micronutrients, and HM contents at booting and maturity stages under HM-contaminated saline conditions (9.06 dS m-1) for two growing seasons. Soil salinity induced high Na+ and Cd toxicity, triggered leaf antioxidant enzymes activities (SOD, CAT, GPOX), enhanced GSH levels, and reduced protein content as well as macronutrient (N, P, Ca) and micronutrient (Fe, Mn, Zn) contents. This was associated with increased Na+ (~ 50-52.94%) and HMs (Cu~23.89%, Pb~65.58-86.52%, Cd~36.21-40.5%) as well NO3 and NO2 contents, ultimately reducing the straw (29.92-34.96%) and grain yields (31.59-32.78%) in wheat. Sequenced application of MLE and GSH as SP and FS further enhanced antioxidant enzymes activities (SOD~44.78-49.00%, GPOX~51.4-56.14%), increased total protein (~ 32.16-33.2%) and GSH (~ 60.38-70.4%) contents, macronutrients N (~ 28.01-50.94%), P (~ 47.62-50.0%), K+ (~ 19.6-25.89%), and Ca2+ (~ 33.33-53.34%), and micronutrient contents (Fe~33.33-51.13, Zn~47.72-53.57, Mn~36.18-36.55) in the leaves and grains (Fe~47.1-50.44, Zn~48.57-74.19, Mn~48.57-74.19) of wheat. This increase was with a simultaneous decrease of HM contents (Cu~19.28%, Cd~47.21-50.42, Pb~48.50-49.31) in leaves and grain (Cu~26.47%, Cd~29.00-30.11%, Pb~47.05-53.86%), as well as grain NO3 and NO2 contents under saline HM conditions. This enhanced nutritional crop status, antioxidant defense system, and its homeostasis with reduced leaf Na+ (~ 40.00-45.45%) toxicity, and NO3 (~ 47.32-54.25%) and NO2 (~ 54.10-61.2%) contents, which led to increased wheat straw and grain yields under HM-contaminated saline soil. In conclusion, integrated applications of MLE and GSH can be a promising strategy to reduce the detrimental effects of dual salinity and HM stress with substantial increase in crop performance and grain nutritional quality in wheat.

7.0 Salt stress responses are not salt tolerance: Re-examining a foundational assumption of plant stress biology.
Ganie SA.
reviewKinases and signal transductionGrowthCurrent opinion in plant biology · 2026-10-08
matched: signalling, salt stress, salinity, stress respon*, growth, development
abstract

For decades, salinity research has sought to identify the molecular determinants of salt tolerance by comparing plants under non-saline and saline conditions. This strategy has profoundly advanced our understanding of stress sensing, signalling, and physiological responses. However, the persistent gap between the discovery of salt-responsive genes and the development of crops with improved field performance suggests a need to reconsider how stress responses are interpreted in relation to tolerance. I argue that salinity does not activate a single "tolerance programme". Rather, salt tolerance is an emergent performance outcome arising from the integrated action of multiple physiological and developmental processes that sustain growth, reproduction, and recovery across variable saline environments. Consequently, the magnitude of a plant's response to salinity does not necessarily reflect its capacity to maintain performance and may itself depend on the experimental context. This perspective proposes that binary stress-control experiments remain valuable for uncovering mechanisms of environmental responsiveness but should increasingly be complemented by experimental frameworks that evaluate performance across environmental gradients, developmental stages, and recovery phases. Such a framework aligns stress biology more closely with ecological reality and the mechanisms that ultimately determine agricultural performance.

7.0 TaPSP1 negatively regulates osmotic stress response in wheat.
Wang K, Zhao D, Zhang T … Zhang H.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-05
matched: phosphoryl*, signaling, hormone*, defense, osmotic stress, drought, stress respon*, plasma membrane
abstract

Wheat (Triticum aestivum L.) is a cornerstone of global food security, yet its production is severely threatened by osmotic stress, such as drought-induced water deficit. Identifying and characterizing key genes involved in stress response is crucial for understanding adaptation mechanisms and developing tolerant varieties. In this study, we characterized TaPSP1, a protein containing a peroxiredoxin-like conserved domain, and uncovered its negative regulatory role in the response to osmotic stress. Bioinformatics analysis revealed that TaPSP1 is a hydrophilic, non-secretory protein that is likely localized to the plasma membrane, possessing multiple phosphorylation sites and conserved oxidoreductase domains. Its promoter contains diverse cis-acting elements associated with hormone signaling and environmental responses. Phylogenetic analysis indicated that TaPSP1 is evolutionarily conserved among Poaceae species. Expression profiling showed that TaPSP1 transcript levels gradually decreased under PEG-induced osmotic stress. Functional analysis in transgenic Arabidopsis overexpressing TaPSP1 revealed enhanced sensitivity to osmotic stress, as evidenced by reduced biomass accumulation, lower survival rates, decreased relative water content and proline accumulation, and elevated malondialdehyde levels. Importantly, we further generated TaPSP1-overexpressing transgenic wheat lines, which consistently exhibited an osmotic stress-sensitive phenotype, corroborating the findings from Arabidopsis. Conversely, TaPSP1-RNAi knockdown wheat lines exhibited significantly enhanced osmotic stress tolerance, with increased survival rates, higher proline accumulation, and reduced malondialdehyde content under PEG treatment, providing direct loss-of-function evidence for its negative regulatory role. Transcriptomic analysis further demonstrated that TaPSP1 overexpression broadly reprograms stress-responsive gene expression, suppressing typical defense pathways. Collectively, this study establishes TaPSP1 as a negative regulator of osmotic stress tolerance in wheat, providing a theoretical foundation for improving stress resistance in wheat breeding programs by targeting this negative regulator.

6.5 An integrated WGCNA-XGBoost framework for prioritizing candidate stress-responsive hub genes in soybean under nutrient and heavy metal stress conditions.
Hajibarat Z, Saidi A.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesCell biology and organellesJournal of applied genetics · 2026-10-14
matched: MAPK*, signaling, hormone*, nitrate, nutrient*, heavy metal*, stress respon*, cell wall*, development
abstract

Abiotic challenges such as nutrient shortages and heavy metal toxicity dramatically limit soybean yields worldwide. While many transcriptomic investigations have examined responses to single stresses, few have identified regulatory nodes that function across multiple adverse conditions. In this study, we conducted a multi-dataset transcriptomic meta-analysis integrating six RNA-seq experiments comprising 66 samples across nutrient deficiency and heavy-metal stress conditions. Following batch-effect correction using ComBat and variance-based filtering, weighted gene co-expression network analysis identified stress-associated modules. XGBoost was then applied within these modules to prioritize genes with high predictive importance. Prioritized candidate gene hub genes were defined using the combined criteria of strong module membership (|kME| > 0.8) and high XGBoost feature importance. Comparative synteny analysis across leguminous species further supported the evolutionary conservation of several prioritized candidates. These genes represent high-priority targets for future functional validation and crop-improvement studies aimed at enhancing soybean stress resilience.Model robustness was assessed using 5-fold cross-validation and receiver operating characteristic (ROC) analysis, with several genes achieving area under the curve (AUC) values exceeding 0.90. Gene set enrichment analyses revealed coordinated regulation of hormone signaling pathways, cell wall remodeling, nitrate transport, and MAPK signaling cascades. Further synteny analyses showed that several of the identified hub regions are conserved in related legume species, including Phaseolus vulgaris, Vigna unguiculata, and Vigna angularis, highlighting the evolutionary conservation of these stress-responsive modules. Overall, the integrated WGCNA-XGBoost framework identifies conserved regulatory hubs involved in multi-stress responses and provides a powerful systems-biology strategy for prioritizing candidate genes and guiding the development of stress-tolerant soybean cultivars.

6.0 Decoupling Community Complexity From Efficacy: A Meta-Analysis of Synthetic Microbial Communities for Plant Stress Resilience.
Luo W, Wang B, Ma H, Li H.
HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthPlant, cell & environment · 2026-10-07
matched: IAA, microbiome*, salinity, drought, nutrient*, growth
abstract

Drought, salinity, pollution and nutrient deficiency substantially impair plant growth by disrupting water balance, ion homoeostasis, redox regulation and metabolism. Synthetic microbial communities (SynComs) represent a frontier in microbiome engineering, theoretically offering superior stability over single-strain inoculants through functional redundancy. However, the design rules governing their efficacy - specifically the trade-off between community complexity and phenotypic output - remain empirically unresolved. This meta-analysis evaluated how synthetic microbial communities (SynComs) affect plant growth and stress-related physiological responses under different stress conditions and examined whether their efficacy was associated with community complexity or specific microbial functional traits. Data from 34 studies (433 independent comparisons) were collected. Then, the natural log response ratios (lnRR) of plant morphological and physiological traits were calculated. A random-effects model was used to estimate global effect sizes and quantify between-study heterogeneity. Plant performance was significantly enhanced following SynComs inoculation, evidenced by a 111.93% increase in total biomass (mean lnRR = 0.751). The highest efficacy was observed under biotic stress (total plant dry weight +191.57%) and pollution (underground dry weight +154.23%), with biomass accumulation being more pronounced in belowground traits than in aboveground components. Physiologically, this growth promotion was associated with the alleviation of oxidative stress in plants, as manifested by the significant upregulation of antioxidant enzymes (CAT + 47.09%, POD + 41.55%, and SOD + 44.71%) and a concurrent reduction in lipid peroxidation. Furthermore, the weighted meta-analysis revealed that single-genus communities significantly outperformed multi-genus communities in the present dataset (p = 0.004). However, efficacy was entirely decoupled from community richness, measured as strain number (R2 = 0.000, p = 0.490). These findings suggest that greater taxonomic breadth or higher strain richness was not necessarily associated with improved SynCom performance. Rather, efficacy appeared to be driven by specific microbial functional traits (such as IAA production and phosphorus solubilisation). We propose that the rational design of SynComs should prioritise strain function over community size to maximise agricultural sustainability and reduce metabolic burden.

6.0 Drought Legacies Can Extend to Nutrient Stoichiometry: Relative Potassium Enrichment in Sugar Maple Foliage Outlasts Hydrological Recovery
Simoneau M, Maheu A, Rivest D … Nolet P.
preprintCa2+GrowthTrees and woody plantsResearch Square · 2026-10-12
matched: pH, drought, nutrient*, growth, tree, trees
abstract

Abstract Background and Aims : Drought legacies are well documented for growth, carbon allocation and carbohydrate reserves, but whether they extend to mineral nutrition is largely untested. We asked whether a stoichiometric imprint persists in sugar maple foliage once soil moisture has recovered, and whether it can be traced to soil nutrient supply or to wood composition. Methods : In a sugar maple (Acer saccharum Marsh.) forest in Quebec, Canada, throughfall was reduced by about 75% over four growing seasons in three paired plots. The following growing season, when moisture no longer differed from controls, we sampled foliage and fine-branch wood of mature trees and saplings, along with soil pH and ion supply. Six nutrients (N, P, K, Ca, Mg, Mn) were expressed as five isometric log-ratio (ilr) balances, [NPK|CaMgMn], [NP|K], [N|P], [CaMg|Mn] and [Ca|Mg], analysed with linear mixed and structural equation models. Results : Of the five balances, only [NP|K] retained a treatment signal, at both stages: the geometric mean of N and P relative to K was 17.2% lower in exclusion plots, while [N|P] was unchanged, indicating a relative shift toward potassium. No indirect route to the foliage was supported, through either soil supply or wood composition. Conclusion : A nutrient imbalance persisted after hydrological recovery, without a supported source in either soil or wood, pointing to tree regulation as a plausible explanation. Transfer between wood and foliage did not follow the conventional division between mobile and immobile elements. Nutritional constraints may outlast the hydrological signature of drought.

17.5 Arabidopsis cyclin‐dependent kinases and MAP65 phosphorylation link microtubule dynamics with salt stress adaptation
Qu X, Zou J, Zhang C … Le J
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressNew Phytologist · 2026-10-17
matched: reactive oxygen species, depolari*, kinase*, phosphoryl*, salt stress, stress respon*, plasma membrane, cytoskelet*, microtubul*, cell division, growth
abstract

Summary Plants possess sophisticated regulatory mechanisms to balance growth and stress responses. Cyclin‐dependent kinases (CDKs) are established regulators of cell division and endoreduplication, yet their roles in abiotic stress responses remain poorly understood. Here, using reverse genetics, biochemical, and cell biological approaches, we show that Arabidopsis ( Arabidopsis thaliana ) CDKB1s contribute to salt stress adaptation through a mechanism involving the phosphorylation of MAP65‐1 and MAP65‐2, two microtubule‐associated proteins essential for cytoskeleton dynamics under salt stress. Loss of CDKB1 function enhances salt tolerance, accompanied by attenuated plasma membrane depolarization and reactive oxygen species accumulation. In vitro phosphorylation assays demonstrate that CDKB1;1 directly phosphorylates MAP65‐1 at Ser‐503. Genetic analyses indicate that phospho‐dead MAP65 variants confer enhanced salt tolerance, whereas phosphomimetic variants compromise tolerance even in the cdkb1;1 1;2 background, supporting a critical role for MAP65 phosphorylation status at these sites in salt stress adaptation. We further found that CDKA;1 can similarly interact with and phosphorylate MAP65‐1, suggesting a potential parallel role for CDKA;1 in this process. Collectively, our findings identify a regulatory module involving CDKs, MAP65, and microtubules that links cell cycle kinases and cytoskeleton dynamics with plant salt stress adaptation.

16.0 Protein homeostasis and inter-organellar signaling under salt stress.
Das S, Arif Y, Mousavi H … Sarraf M.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressPlant signaling & behavior · 2026-10-07
matched: phosphoryl*, signaling, ubiquitin*, abscisic acid, phytohormone*, salt stress, salinity, endoplasmic reticulum, ER, nucleus, nuclear, Golgi
abstract

Salinity is a major abiotic constraint on plant growth, productivity, and global food security. It disrupts cellular protein homeostasis (proteostasis) at every step, from synthesis to degradation. Plant survival under salt stress therefore depends on the coordinated regulation of protein synthesis, folding, post-translational modification, trafficking, quality control, and turnover. The individual contributions of the nucleus, ribosomes, endoplasmic reticulum (ER), and Golgi apparatus to salinity responses are increasingly well described, but how these compartments communicate remains far less understood. This review synthesizes current evidence on this inter-organellar signaling network, addressing four questions: how salinity-induced osmotic, ionic, and oxidative stress reprograms nuclear transcription and chromatin state; how ribosomes sustain selective translation of protective proteins while clearing stalled or damaged translational products; how the ER expands its folding capacity through the unfolded protein response and removes terminally misfolded proteins by ER-associated degradation; and how the Golgi sustains glycosylation, sorting, and secretion of the transporters needed for ion and osmotic homeostasis. Beyond these organelle-level responses, the review discusses two signaling layers that couple them into a single circuit: post-translational modifications (phosphorylation, ubiquitination, SUMOylation, and redox- and nitrosative/persulfidation-based marks) and phytohormone/gasotransmitter signals, including abscisic acid, nitric oxide, hydrogen sulfide, carbon monoxide, and melatonin. By framing proteostasis as an emergent property of nucleus-ribosome-ER-Golgi coordination rather than of any single compartment, this review identifies specific inter-organellar signaling nodes as candidate targets for breeding or engineering salinity-resilient crops.

11.0 The class VIII myosin ATM2 stabilizes actin–plasma membrane contacts in tobacco pollen tubes by locally promoting PtdIns(4,5)P2 formation
Wagner VC, Fratini M, Uhlenberg J … Heilmann I
Kinases and signal transductionGrowthMethodsNature Plants · 2026-10-16
matched: kinase*, plasma membrane, actin, nanodomain*, pollen tube*, biosensor*
abstract

Abstract The intersection of actin filaments with the plasma membrane (PM) in plant cells involves lipid–protein interactions by the Arabidopsis class VIII myosin ATM2 that are currently not understood. Using pollen tube cells as a model, we describe how ATM2 enables actin–PM contacts by modulating membrane lipid nano-organization. ATM2 bound anionic PM lipids, including phosphatidylinositol 4,5-bisphosphate (PtdIns(4,5)P 2 ), with no apparent preference for any particular lipid species. Overexpression of ATM2 unexpectedly stabilized actin dynamics and altered pollen tube morphology. Actin dynamics remained unaltered upon overexpression of a lipid-binding-defective ATM2 variant. ATM2 effects on actin dynamics required PtdIns(4,5)P 2 , as artificial PtdIns(4,5)P 2 depletion in pollen tubes abolished the effects of full-length ATM2 on actin dynamics despite its retained PM association. ATM2 colocalized and interacted with the PI4P 5-kinase PIP5K2, which resides at actin–PM contacts and forms PtdIns(4,5)P 2 nanodomains facilitating rho-of-plants-dependent actin stabilization. Upon ATM2 overexpression, a fluorescent PtdIns(4,5)P 2 biosensor decorated an extended PM region in vivo, indicating a promoting effect of ATM2 on PtdIns(4,5)P 2 abundance. The catalytic activity of purified recombinant PIP5K2 protein was enhanced upon coincubation with a purified carboxy-terminal ATM2 fragment in vitro, suggesting that ATM2 contributes to the intrinsic regulation of PtdIns(4,5)P 2 formation. Actin dynamics are thus stabilized at the actin–PM interface by ATM2 locally promoting the formation of PtdIns(4,5)P 2 nanodomains, thereby self-reinforcing ATM2 recruitment while also facilitating rho of plants activation to stabilize membrane-proximal actin filaments.

10.5 Chain length-dependent fate and phytotoxicity of PFAS in plants.
Chen Y, Liu C, Guo D.
reviewCa2+GrowthCritical reviews in biochemistry and molecular biology · 2026-10-09
matched: reactive oxygen species, apoplast*, cell wall*, plasma membrane, vacuol*, chloroplast*, mitochondri*, root, roots, Casparian
abstract

Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) are persistent environmental contaminants that accumulate in plants, posing risks to terrestrial food webs. This review provides a mechanistic synthesis of the behavior of PFAS in plants, from root uptake to subcellular responses. Short-chain PFAS preferentially translocate acropetally via apoplastic and symplastic pathways, bypassing the Casparian strip, whereas long-chain congeners are largely retained in roots due to adsorption to cell wall polysaccharides (pectin and hemicellulose) via hydrogen bonding and hydrophobic interactions. Transmembrane transport involves passive diffusion, anion channels, aquaporins, and potentially carrier-mediated processes. At the subcellular level, PFAS primarily accumulate in the cell wall and plasma membrane, with a fraction sequestered into vacuoles, chloroplasts, and mitochondria. This compartmentalization triggers a cascade of phytotoxic effects, including reactive oxygen species burst, lipid peroxidation, membrane disruption, photosynthetic inhibition, and hormonal imbalance. Plants deploy detoxification strategies such as vacuolar sequestration and activation of enzymatic and non-enzymatic antioxidant systems, exhibiting hormetic dose-response. Advanced omics and imaging techniques have elucidated the molecular and spatial landscape of PFAS-plant interactions. This integrative framework enhances our mechanistic understanding of PFAS phytotoxicity, supporting ecological risk assessment and phytoremediation strategies.

10.0 The chloroplast-mitochondria interaction under water extremes: Insights into metabolic tug-of-war.
Ateeq M, Yun P, Ashraf MA … Liu J.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressCurrent opinion in plant biology · 2026-10-08
matched: kinase*, signaling, hormone*, drought, flooding, stress respon*, chloroplast*, mitochondri*, organelle*, growth
abstract

Plants respond to water extremes through dynamic, organelle-level reprogramming of resource allocation rather than metabolic shutdown. Recent advances suggest that drought and flooding induce coordinated metabolic reorganization between chloroplasts and mitochondria, shifting energy distribution from growth to stress acclimation via retrograde signaling pathways. These signals are integrated by the trehalose-6-phosphate (Tre6P)-sucrose non-fermenting 1-related protein kinase 1 (SnRK1)-target of rapamycin (TOR) network, a central regulatory hub that interprets carbon and energy status to modulate stress-responsive metabolic and physiological outcomes. This systemic coordination is also modulated by hormone reprogramming and shaping adaptive phenotypic responses. Emerging technologies, spanning from organelle-specific metabolomics to synthetic biology, provide a framework to engineer inter-organellar communication. Here, we review recent advances in chloroplast-mitochondria metabolic coordination, retrograde signaling, and energy-sensing networks under water extremes, and discuss future strategies to engineer metabolic crosstalk for improving crop stress resilience and growth under adverse environments.

9.5 Multiple evolutionary routes to cytoskeletal arborization revealed by the rhizarian amoeba Filoreta ramosa.
Guest SL, Dawson SC.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthTrees and woody plantsCurrent biology : CB · 2026-10-07 · also: Current Biology
matched: immun*, cytoskelet*, actin, microtubul*, organelle*, growth, tree, live imaging
abstract

The eukaryotic cytoskeleton generates remarkable diversity in cellular architecture despite being built from conserved actin and tubulin polymers. Diversification of cytoskeletal regulators, motors, and filament-organizing proteins produces varied morphologies, yet certain architectures repeatedly emerge in distantly related lineages. One notable example is cytoskeletal arborization, occurring in metazoan neurons and amoeboid lineages throughout the eukaryotic tree. Whether these similar branched architectures arise through conserved cytoskeletal organization, shared molecular reuse, or convergence driven by common physical constraints remains unresolved. Here, we investigate the rhizarian amoeba Filoreta ramosa, which forms a multinucleate reticulated network through branching and anastomosis. Using live imaging, immunofluorescence, morphometric analyses, and cytoskeletal drugs, we define how actin and microtubule systems organize branch formation, intracellular transport, and large-scale network architecture. Actin-rich protrusions initiate exploratory branchlets that become selectively stabilized through microtubule incorporation. Longitudinal microtubule arrays reinforce mature branches and support rapid bidirectional organelle transport, while branch nodes function as distributed sites of microtubule nucleation. These cytoskeletal features parallel key mechanisms underlying neuronal arborization, yet unlike neurons, Filoreta develops a decentralized reticulated network through repeated anastomosis and distributed microtubule organization, thereby scaling transport capacity and structural support with network growth. Our findings suggest that shared cytoskeletal mechanisms repeatedly support branching architectures, while distinct topologies and modes of organization allow evolution to reach arborization through different routes as increasing cellular scale imposes common demands on transport and structural integrity. Filoreta therefore provides a tractable model for investigating how conserved cytoskeletal systems generate arborized architectures that scale with cellular size.

9.0 A cytoskeleton-associated Plasmodium protein critical for organelle segregation.
Navarro FM, Wagner E, Kraemer S … Dvorin JD.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityMethodsNature communications · 2026-09-08
matched: immun*, mitochondri*, cytoskelet*, microtubul*, organelle*, microscop*, segmentation
abstract

The malaria parasite, Plasmodium falciparum, replicates asexually within red blood cells by schizogony, a mode of division in which organelles divide multiple times before a final coordinated cytokinesis event, called segmentation, partitions them into dozens of merozoites. The Plasmodium cytoskeleton functions critically during this process, facilitating one-per-cell organelle segregation. Here, we identify and characterize a cytoskeleton-associated protein, PfOGRE (organelle retention, PF3D7_1435600), required for segregation of the mitochondrion and apicoplast, a non-photosynthetic plastid. Immunofluorescence paired with ultrastructure expansion microscopy demonstrates that PfOGRE localizes alongside subpellicular microtubules in nascent merozoites, adjacent to the segregating apicoplast. Conditional knockout of PfOGRE produces organelle-less merozoites with abnormal subpellicular microtubules, impairing asexual replication. Immunoprecipitation and mass spectrometry identify several candidate interactors, including PfMyoF. Conditional knockdown of PfMyoF produces similar organelle segregation defects. Together, these results establish PfOGRE as a critical mediator of organelle segregation and define a non-structural role for Plasmodium subpellicular microtubules during this process.

8.5 Distinct root apoplastic barrier distribution and conserved peroxidase function in desert trees
Kise JG, Xiao TT, Xu X … Blilou I
Ca2+Abiotic stressGrowthPlant Physiology · 2026-10-14
matched: apoplast*, nutrient*, plasma membrane, Golgi, vesicle*, root, roots, xylem, epiderm*
abstract

Abstract The formation of extracellular barriers in roots plays a critical role in maintaining water balance and nutrient homeostasis; however, their molecular composition and physiological roles in desert-adapted plants remain largely unexplored. Here, we examine apoplastic barrier formation in the roots of two arid-adapted, ecologically and economically important tree species: date palm (Phoenix dactylifera) and grey mangrove (Avicennia marina). In date palm, lignin and suberin showed complementary distribution patterns within the outer root layers. In mangrove roots, lignin accumulated in the epidermis and exodermis, whereas suberin was concentrated in the exodermis. Label-free Raman spectroscopy imaging confirmed these spatial patterns. Noninvasive Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) revealed that water distribution mirrored barrier localization: date palms retained water in the vasculature and outer tissues, whereas mangroves concentrated water in the xylem. Transcriptome analysis identified a type III PEROXIDASE (PER) highly expressed in the exodermis of both species. The PER protein localized to Golgi-derived vesicles and the plasma membrane and interacted with a component of the Golgi apparatus. Expression of date palm and mangrove PER in Arabidopsis thaliana leads to ectopic lignin deposition in the root epidermis. Together, our findings suggest that type III PEROXIDASES are transported to the plasma membrane through the Golgi vesicles and promote lignin deposition. Overall, this study reveals distinct root barrier distribution and identifies a conserved molecular component underlying structural adaptations that enable plants to thrive in challenging environments.

GrowthThe Plant Cell · 2026-10-13
matched: cell wall*, Golgi, endosom*, trafficking, growth, development
abstract

Abstract The plant cell wall, composed primarily of polysaccharides, is essential for normal plant growth and development. Many of these polysaccharides are synthesized within and subsequently secreted via the endomembrane system. Mutations in genes including MURUS3 (MUR3), YPT/RAB GTPase-interacting protein 4 homologs (YIP4a/YIP4b), and ECHIDNA (ECH) result in dwarf plants, which is believed to be a consequence of impaired cell wall polysaccharide secretion. 7.0 PalKCH1 and PalKCH2 are required for tension wood formation and gelatinous layer deposition in Populus.
Li J, Qu K, Wang R … Zhou N.
Kinases and signal transductionGrowthMethodsFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-21
matched: signaling, cell wall*, trafficking, cytoskelet*, microtubul*, stem, xylem, growth, development, CRISPR
abstract

Tension wood (TW) is a graviresponsive xylem tissue exclusive to angiosperm trees that counteracts gravitational stress. Its hallmark is a gelatinous layer (G-layer) in fiber secondary walls, rich in cellulose and low in lignin. Although kinesins mediate microtubule-microfilament crosstalk, their role in G-layer biogenesis during gravistimulation is unclear in woody angiosperms. We studied two kinesin paralogs, PalKCH1 and PalKCH2, in Populus alba var. pyramidalis during TW development. 7.0 Sulfoquinovosyldiacylglycerol (SQDG) in Chloroplast-Poor Tissues: What Can Lipidomics Tell Us?
Potijun S, Sirikantaramas S, Schaller H.
reviewAbiotic stressGrowthTrees and woody plantsBiochimie · 2026-10-08
matched: nutrient*, chloroplast*, organelle*, roots, development, tree, woody
abstract

Sulfoquinovosyldiacylglycerol (SQDG) is an anionic sulfolipid of plastid membranes, classically associated with chloroplasts and thylakoids. Its detection by lipidomics in chloroplast-poor tissues raises a central problem: how can a reported SQDG signal be validated, and what evidence is required to determine its plastid context and biological meaning? Here, we critically reassess evidence beyond chloroplast-dominated tissues and evaluate both the strength of evidence for SQDG occurrence or function and the steps required to test lipidomics-derived hypotheses. The literature supports context-dependent rather than universal interpretations. Direct organelle-level lipid evidence from isolated non-green plastids remains limited; etioplast genetics supports an SQDG-related role in non-photosynthetic plastid membrane development; phosphate-deprived roots support phosphorus-economy remodeling; and sulfur-starvation studies establish regulated SQDG turnover and sulfur remobilization. By contrast, chromoplast and woody-tissue interpretations remain unresolved. These findings show that SQDG abundance alone is hypothesis-generating rather than diagnostic of function. We therefore propose a decision-tree framework that moves from chemical confirmation of the lipid signal to spatial localization, SQD1/SQD2-associated biosynthetic capacity, nutrient context, tissue-specific perturbation, and phenotype testing. This framework provides criteria for moving from lipidomic detection to supported biological interpretation in chloroplast-poor tissues.

7.0 Thylakoid membrane transporter SecE1 is required for chloroplast development and photosynthetic function in Arabidopsis.
Ren W, Xiao J, Wang X.
Kinases and signal transductionGrowthPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-06
matched: signaling, nucleus, nuclear, chloroplast*, leaf, growth, development
abstract

Chloroplast biogenesis depends on protein transport, pigment biosynthesis, and thylakoid membrane assembly, which are coordinated through anterograde (nucleus-to-plastid) and retrograde (plastid-to-nucleus) signaling. The chloroplast Sec translocon mediates the import of nuclear-encoded precursor proteins across the thylakoid membrane, yet the regulatory role of its core subunit SecE1 in chloroplast development and photosynthetic performance remains incompletely understood. Here, we characterized a chlorotic Arabidopsis mutant with partial loss of SecE1 function. Compared with the wild type and a complementation line, the sece1 exhibited retarded leaf growth, reduced chlorophyll accumulation, and diminished maximum photochemical efficiency (Fv/Fm), indicative of impaired photosynthetic competence. Biochemical analyses further revealed compromised assembly and stability of photosynthetic supercomplexes, along with decreased abundance of Sec1 transporter substrates and key PSII subunits. Collectively, our findings demonstrate that SecE1 is critically required for chloroplast biogenesis by sustaining photosynthetic protein transport and accumulation, as well as supercomplex assembly, and that its loss elicits retrograde signals that reshape nuclear gene expression to mitigate plastid dysfunction. This study highlights the interplay between thylakoid protein translocation and organellar-nuclear communication as a determinant of photosynthetic competence and plant growth.

6.5 Tvp38/DedA Family Protein mediates biogenesis of chloroplast peripheral reticulum and envelope-to-thylakoid contacts in Arabidopsis
Voelkel-Lambertz, M., Bykowski … O.
preprintGrowthMethodsbioRxiv · 2026-10-14
matched: chloroplast*, vesicle*, organelle*, growth, microscop*
abstract

Chloroplasts of land plants are dynamic organelles essential for photosynthesis, metabolism, and plant adaptation to changing environmental conditions. Within chloroplasts, the inner envelope and thylakoid membranes are spatially separated but functionally interconnected. The inner envelope harbors enzymes involved in lipid synthesis for internal membranes and controls the exchange of metabolites and proteins, whereas the thylakoid membranes accommodate the machinery of the photosynthetic electron transport chain responsible for converting light energy into chemical energy. Therefore, efficient communication between these membrane systems is essential for maintaining chloroplast homeostasis and sustaining photosynthetic activity. In this study we have characterized the role of Tvp38/DedA Family Protein (TVPFP) in mediating inner envelope-to-thylakoid communication in Arabidopsis thaliana. Despite exhibiting wild-type-like growth and similar accumulation of the major thylakoid-anchored photosynthetic complexes, tvpfp mutants displayed reduced photosynthetic activity and increased abundance of photorespiration- and pigment-related proteins. Our results revealed that TVPFP localizes to the chloroplast envelope and undergoes post-translational modification when localized to the thylakoids. The unmodified form of TVPFP is enriched in high-molecular-weight complexes, suggesting that TVPFP oligomers are partially dissolved upon thylakoid relocation. Transmission electron microscopy data showed reduced abundance of vesicles as well as chloroplast peripheral reticulum (CPR) structures connecting the envelope and thylakoids in tvpfp mutants. Together, our results demonstrate that TVPFP fine-tunes photosynthesis by regulating vesicle abundance and biogenesis of CPR structures. Our findings shed light on the importance of envelope-to-thylakoid communication and the role of CPR structures in land plants.

abstract

Tilia mandshurica is an important woody nectar plant, but the genomic and transcriptional basis of its nectar sugar accumulation remains poorly understood. abstract

Taro (Colocasia esculenta (L.) Schott) is an important perennial corm crop, and its corm expansion is accompanied by starch synthesis and accumulation. 6.0 Large-scale tubular and tubular-lamellar intracellular membrane systems of fungi.
Mazheika IS, Voronko OV, Kamzolkina OV.
reviewPlant-microbe interactions and immunityProtoplasma · 2026-10-14
matched: oomycete*, mycorrhiz*, plasma membrane, vacuol*, endoplasmic reticulum, mitochondri*
abstract

Filamentous fungi and oomycetes have four large intracellular membrane systems consisting of tubes, lamellae, or a combination thereof. Such elongated membrane structures can extend for tens of micrometers in hyphal cells, branching and connecting into bundles and networks. These systems are represented by tubular vacuoles, elongated mitochondria, endoplasmic reticulum, and macroinvaginations of the plasma membrane. They are most developed in xylotrophic and mycorrhiza-forming basidiomycetes. This review examines three membrane systems, excluding the macroinvaginations. Various morphotypes of these membrane systems, characteristic of certain taxonomic and ecological-trophic groups of fungi and oomycetes, are considered. Hypotheses explaining the role of large-scale membrane systems in the life processes of filamentous organisms are presented. These hypotheses are still relatively speculative because they rely more on morphology and topology than on the poorly studied physiology of these systems. The synthesizing hypothesis posits that, at least in the apical region of hyphae, tubular vacuoles, elongated mitochondria, and the endoplasmic reticulum interact closely and may facilitate intrahyphal solute transport, independent of mass flow and turgor gradients. Further studies of large-scale membrane systems are important for developing the structural physiology of filamentous fungi and other organisms.

abstract

The risk to agricultural production and human health from micro/nanoplastics (MNPs) exposure cannot be ignored, making it imperative to find effective mitigation strategies. 17.5 Concentration-Dependent Regulation of Growth and Development by Calliterpenone through Hormonal Crosstalk in Arabidopsis thaliana.
Gautam S, Ranjan A, Mishra P … Trivedi PK.

matched: signalling, auxin*, gibberellin*, GA, jasmon*, JA, hormone*, defense, defence, stress respon*, cell expansion, root
abstract

Plant growth and development are governed by intricate hormonal networks that integrate environmental and endogenous signals. Plant growth regulators can be natural or synthetic chemical compounds that mimic hormonal regulation to alter plant growth and development. Here, we identify Calliterpenone (CT), a tetracyclic diterpenoid isolated from Callicarpa macrophylla, as a novel regulator of growth-defense balance in Arabidopsis thaliana.
