15.0 Cell geometry and nuclear organization are associated with founder-cell competence for lateral root initiation in the Arabidopsis root.
Pasternak T, Paponov IA.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesCell biology and organellesPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-06
matched: signaling, auxin*, PIN\d+, nuclear, root, lateral root*, xylem, meristem*, endoderm*, development, organogenesis, differentiation
abstract

Lateral root initiation in Arabidopsis thaliana occurs in xylem pole pericycle (Px) cells, yet the structural features associated with founder-cell competence in differentiated root tissues remain insufficiently defined. Here, we combined spatial cell biology approaches, including three-dimensional analysis of cell geometry, nuclear morphometry, EdU labeling, and auxin-induction experiments, to determine whether Px cells exhibit a structural prepattern before visible organogenesis. Px cells were already distinguishable in the root apical meristem (RAM) by their shorter length along the longitudinal root axis and larger volume relative to the remaining non-xylem-pole pericycle cells (Pr), and this geometrical distinction persisted into the differentiation zone. In the same zone, Px nuclei were less elongated and more spherical than Pr nuclei, indicating a characteristic nuclear organization. In the absence of exogenous auxin, DNA replication activity was confined mainly to inner tissues, particularly the pericycle, vasculature, and developing lateral root primordia. Upon auxin treatment, PIN1 accumulation and EdU incorporation appeared in discrete domains rather than uniformly along the root axis, indicating localized auxin responsiveness and localized cell-cycle re-entry. Continuous auxin treatment for 36h induced formative divisions in the Px domain of the early differentiation zone, where lateral root initiation occurs. Early primordia further displayed internal geometric zonation and pronounced deformation of the overlying endodermis, indicating that internal organization and tissue accommodation arise early during primordium development. Together, these observations support a model in which Px-associated cell geometry and nuclear organization represent structural correlates of founder-cell competence, while emphasizing that functional tests in signaling mutants are required to determine causality.

14.5 Emerging Paradigms in Strigolactone Biosynthesis, Signaling, and Evolutionary Dynamics.
Wani KI, Manzoor MA, Aftab T … Brewer PB.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityPlant communications · 2026-10-13
matched: signaling, receptor*, strigolactone*, karrikin*, hormone*, mycorrhiz*, symbio*, root, root architecture, leaf, stem, shoot*
abstract

Originally identified as root-derived exudates that trigger the germination of parasitic weed seeds, strigolactones (SLs) were later discovered to be crucial facilitators of plant symbiotic associations with arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi and then found to be a bona fide plant hormone class in 2008. Thereafter, SLs have been revealed as versatile regulators of plant development, regulating processes as diverse as shoot branching, root architecture, leaf senescence, vasculature formation, and stem secondary growth. Beyond these developmental roles, they function as signaling molecules, enabling plants to adapt to and withstand environmental stresses. This review focuses on the evolutionary diversification of SL biosynthesis and perception, emphasizing how pathway specialization, receptor remodeling, hydrolysis, and downstream signaling generate distinct endogenous and interorganismal functions. It details the complex biosynthetic pathway of SLs, highlighting key enzymes that work together to convert carotenoid precursors into the diverse SLs. The review then explores SL signaling mechanisms, emphasizing the receptor DWARF14 and its interactions with downstream components, such as DWARF53 proteins, which influence how plants perceive and respond to SLs. It also examines the evolutionary aspects of SL biosynthesis and signaling, especially their dual roles in promoting plant-microbe symbiosis and regulating hormone activity. This comprehensive update enhances our understanding of SL functions at the molecular and ecological levels. Moreover, the review discusses the relationship between SLs and karrikins, focusing on DWARF14 and the KARRIKIN INSENSITIVE 2 receptor, and explores potential applications of SLs in agriculture to improve crop resilience and yields. This in-depth account of how SLs operate as master regulators in plant life provides insights into both their evolutionary significance and their potential utility in agriculture.

14.0 Crosstalk between strigolactone and KAI2/KL signalling in plant growth and stress responses.
Shen R, Gianoglio S, Visentin I … Cardinale F.
reviewKinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-21
matched: signal transduction, signalling, receptor*, strigolactone*, karrikin*, salinity, drought, nutrient*, stress respon*, root, root development, leaf
abstract

Strigolactones (SLs) and karrikins (KARs) are signalling molecules of distinct origin: SLs are produced through plant metabolism, whereas KARs arise from the combustion of plant carbohydrates and are thought to mimic an endogenous ligand, whose chemical identity remains unknown. Despite these differences, they share structural similarities and partially overlapping signalling pathways, which are central to understanding their crosstalk as well as their distinct vs shared effects. These similarities extend from structural features and sensing mechanisms to downstream signal transduction. Accordingly, SL and KAR pathways are not entirely independent; rather, they converge, interact, and mutually regulate one another. However, advancements as well as the interpretation of the existing literature are constrained by two unresolved issues. First, the effects of KARs are rightly tested against the effects of loss-of-function mutations of their receptor KAI2; but this does not allow discriminating against the effects of its endogenous, so far uncharacterized ligand. Second, the synthetic compounds most routinely used as analogues of SLs lack receptor specificity, with rac-GR24 activating both D14 (the proper SL receptor) and KAI2. This is a confounding factor that is often not considered when attributing phenotypes to either pathway. Drawing on current research, this paper reviews similarities and differences between the two pathways, with particular emphasis on their cooperative and regulatory roles in shoot, leaf and root development. It also reviews their functions and especially interactions under abiotic stresses such as drought, salinity, low-light conditions, nutrient limitation, and suboptimal temperatures, systematically examining the level of confidence and criticalities for each dataset. Finally, it discusses whether and how natural or synthetic KAR-related molecules may be used to enhance crop resilience under adverse environmental conditions.

13.0 LBD37, LBD38, and LBD39 are redundantly involved in regulating lateral root development and elongation in Arabidopsis thaliana.
Cho C, Kang NY, Lee DW, Kim J.
Cell biology and organellesJournal of plant physiology · 2026-10-08
matched: cell expansion, root, lateral root*, primary root*, root development, meristem*, growth, development
abstract

The LATERAL ORGAN BOUNDARIES DOMAIN (LBD) genes encode a family of transcription factors harboring a conserved LOB domain that regulate diverse aspects of plant growth and development. Arabidopsis LBD proteins are grouped into two classes according to their domain structures. Class I proteins, which function as typical DNA-binding transcription factors, are relatively well characterized, whereas the biological functions of class II proteins remain largely unknown. Here, we show that LBD37, LBD38, and LBD39-three class II LBD genes-redundantly regulate lateral root (LR) formation and elongation. Although single and double lbd mutants exhibited LR densities comparable to wild type, the lbd37,38,39 triple mutant displayed significantly reduced LR density with no change in primary root length. Additionally, the triple mutant showed shorter LR length and reduced LR primordium density at stage I relative to wild type. Furthermore, the triple mutant exhibited a reduced meristem size and fewer cells in the LR-with no alteration in cell size-indicating that LBD37/38/39 modulate LR elongation by promoting cell proliferation, rather than cell expansion, within the LR meristem. Taken together, these results demonstrate that LBD37/38/39 redundantly regulate an early stage of LR development and LR elongation.

12.5 Salt stress responses of Brassica napus roots and functional characterization of BnSAUR12 in salt tolerance.
Li O, Zhao J, Wang S … Wan H.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-13
matched: signaling, auxin*, hormone*, salt stress, stress respon*, root, roots, lateral root*, primary root*, root development, hypocotyl*, seed*
abstract

Salt stress severely limits crop productivity by affecting plant growth and development. Rapeseed (Brassica napus L.) shows considerable adaptability to saline environments; however, the molecular basis of root responses to salt stress remains largely unclear. In this study, we investigated the physiological, hormonal, and transcriptional responses of rapeseed roots under salt stress. Salt treatment altered root system architecture by inhibiting hypocotyl elongation and lateral root development while promoting primary root elongation. Targeted hormone metabolomic analysis revealed extensive changes in endogenous hormone profiles, with auxin-related metabolites showing the most pronounced changes. Transcriptome analysis further demonstrated that auxin-responsive pathways were strongly activated under salt stress, indicating an important role of auxin signaling in root adaptation. To explore the genes involved in this process, we performed a genome-wide characterization of the Small Auxin-Up RNA (SAUR) gene family in B. napus. A total of 225 BnSAUR genes were identified and analyzed based on their phylogenetic relationships, gene structures, conserved motifs, chromosomal distribution, evolutionary relationships, cis-regulatory elements, and potential microRNA regulation. Integrative analysis of transcriptome sequencing, published RNA sequencing, and assay for transposase-accessible chromatin sequencing data identified multiple salt-responsive BnSAUR genes, and their expression patterns were further confirmed by real-time quantitative polymerase chain reaction. Among them, BnSAUR12 was selected for functional characterization, and its loss reduced salt tolerance during seed germination in rapeseed. Together, our study expands our understanding of the molecular basis of salt tolerance in rapeseed and provides potential candidate genes for improving stress resilience in oil crops.

11.5 SbLOX3 modulates jasmonate-associated flavonoid accumulation, root growth, and drought-induced root hollow formation in Scutellaria baicalensis.
Geng D, Zhang Y, Zhao S … Shu J.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-22
matched: signaling, jasmon*, drought, root, roots, root growth, cambium, xylem, phloem, growth, development
abstract

The hollowed root formation and 4'-deoxyflavone accumulation in Scutellaria baicalensis are associated with jasmonate (JAs) signaling, but the upstream regulators integrating these processes with drought tolerance remain unknown. This study investigated whether the 13-lipoxygenase gene SbLOX3 may act as a modulator. Transgenic plants overexpressing SbLOX3 or with RNAi suppression were exposed to polyethylene glycol-simulated and long-term soil drought stress. Metabolite profiling, gene expression analysis, root morphological measurements, and histological observations were conducted. Overexpression of SbLOX3 elevated JAs and 4'-deoxyflavone levels, enhanced root growth and drought tolerance, and induced precocious hollow development in roots under water deficit. In contrast, RNAi lines showed reduced JAs and flavonoid contents, impaired drought tolerance, and absence of hollow formation. SbLOX3 was strongly expressed in xylem, cambium, and phloem under drought. The results suggest that SbLOX3 is associated with a coordinated responseregulate to drought by promoting defensive flavonoid biosynthesis and morphological adaptation, thereby potentially contributing to the accumulation of measured flavonoids.

11.0 RUBY expression alters development and metabolism in Arabidopsis.
Cheema A, Prasad KVSK, Huq E, Reddy ASN.
HormonesMethodsPlant molecular biology · 2026-10-08
matched: auxin*, IAA, gibberellin*, hormone*, root, roots, lateral root*, root architecture, leaves, seed*, growth, development
abstract

Genetically encoded reporters are indispensable tools for monitoring gene expression, transformation efficiency, and diverse cellular processes in plants. The betalain-based RUBY reporter has recently emerged as a convenient visual marker requiring no substrates or specialized equipment, and it has been widely used for a variety of applications. However, the developmental and metabolic consequences of its expression remain unexplored. Here, we show that independent transgenic lines of Arabidopsis thaliana expressing RUBY exhibit profound alterations in growth and reproductive development. RUBY-expressing plants were significantly smaller, produced fewer leaves, flowered later, and frequently developed malformed siliques with reduced seed yield. In addition, root architecture was severely impaired, with a marked reduction in the number of lateral roots. This phenotype was not rescued by indole-3-acetic acid (IAA). Targeted metabolomic profiling revealed widespread alterations in amino acid and hormone pools, including reduced indole-3-acetic acid and gibberellin, alongside accumulation of auxin precursors. Disruption of RUBY expression by suppressing the third enzyme (glucosyltransferase) restored the RUBY-associated phenotypes to wild-type. Together, our findings show that RUBY expression in Arabidopsis thaliana is not metabolically neutral; rather, it perturbs both development and metabolism by disrupting hormone and amino acid homeostasis. These results highlight reporter-associated trade-offs and underscore the necessity of regulated expression systems, such as inducible or tissue-specific expression, to expand the utility of RUBY in plant research and biotechnology.

11.0 Root Age Structure Governs Long-Term Architecture and Hydraulic Function in Perennial Plants: a Modelling Framework
Giraud, M., Fichtl … M.
preprintTrees and woody plantsbioRxiv · 2026-10-06
matched: root, roots, root development, root architecture, growth, development, woody, hydraulic*, dendro*
abstract

Summary Background and aims Perennial root systems develop over decades and exhibit continuous turnover of structural and absorptive components, yet most root architecture models treat woody roots as permanent and neglect root age structure. We developed an empirically constrained modelling framework that explicitly represents age-structured demography, growth regulation and dormancy in perennial root systems. Methods We extended the functional–structural plant model CPlantBox to simulate perennial root development over decades by integrating age-dependent survival, secondary radial growth and climate-driven dormancy. The model was parameterised using unique three-dimensional root system data from young and mature grapevines, together with dendrochronological root age measurements, and coupled to root hydraulic properties. Key results Empirical analyses revealed multi-cohort age distributions and substantial turnover of lignified structural roots, contradicting the assumption of a static woody scaffold. The model reproduced age distributions, order-specific survival probabilities and fine-to-structural root length ratios, and generated realistic, genotype-specific three-dimensional architectures. Simulated uptake profiles and hydraulic scenarios showed strong age- and architecture-driven effects. Conclusions Explicitly representing woody root age distributions and age-dependent processes may fundamentally change predictions of perennial root system architecture and function. The framework advances root modelling beyond annual paradigms and provides a transferable tool for analysing long-term architectural and hydraulic consequences of genotype × climate interactions in perennial crops.

10.5 Conserved molecular compatibility for MIZ1 function in cytokinin signaling in land plants
Smoljan, A., Das … J.
preprintKinases and signal transductionHormonesCell biology and organellesbioRxiv · 2026-10-13
matched: kinase*, signaling, receptor*, cytokinin*, endoplasmic reticulum, ER, root, roots, hydrotrop*, growth, development
abstract

Plant roots adjust their growth direction toward water through hydrotropism, a process that in Arabidopsis thaliana requires the endoplasmic reticulum (ER)-associated protein MIZU-KUSSEI1 (MIZ1). Nonetheless, MIZ1 orthologues occur throughout land plants, including lineages lacking true roots, raising questions about the ancestral function of this protein family. Here, we investigated the evolutionary conservation and functional diversification of MIZ1-like proteins across land plants. MIZ1 orthologues from evolutionarily distant land plants failed to restore hydrotropism or cytokinin sensitivity in the Arabidopsis miz1 mutant, despite conserved protein structure and ER localization. In the liverwort Marchantia polymorpha , loss of MpMIZ1a led to altered cytokinin sensitivity and cytokinin-mediated development in both the sporophyte and gametophyte. These phenotypes and gene expression analyses imply a conserved role of MIZ1 in cytokinin-related processes. Despite this functional similarity, protein– protein docking and bimolecular fluorescence complementation revealed species-specific interactions between MIZ1 proteins and their cognate cytokinin histidine kinase receptors in Arabidopsis and Marchantia. Together, our findings indicate that MIZ1 proteins represent ancient land plant regulators of cytokinin-dependent development whose molecular compatibility and developmental outputs have diverged during evolution. We propose that the role of MIZ1 in root hydrotropism represents a derived recruitment of this ancestral module into root-specific environmental response pathways.

10.5 Mechanisms underlying the defective development of xyloglucan-deficient root hairs in Arabidopsis thaliana
Zambare, V., McDermond … C. T.
preprintCell biology and organellesMethodsbioRxiv · 2026-10-15
matched: cell wall*, cell expansion, root, root hair*, growth, development, microscop*
abstract

Hemicellulosic xyloglucan is abundant in the primary cell walls of eudicots and non-gramineous monocots and contributes to their mechanical integrity. Its deficiency results in morphologically abnormal root hairs in Arabidopsis mutants lacking either two XYLOGLUCAN XYLOSYLTRANSFERASE (xxt1 xxt2) or five CELLULOSE SYNTHASE-LIKE C (cslc-∆5) genes, but the cellular and biomechanical mechanisms underlying these abnormalities have remained unexplored. We combined multiple imaging modalities and quantitative modeling to unmask these mechanisms, first observing that xyloglucan-deficient mutants produced fewer and shorter root hairs that grew slower than wild type. Xyloglucan-deficient trichoblasts showed root hair initiation defects, helping to explain the sparseness of root hairs in these mutants. The mutant root hairs also had lower turgor pressure, thicker walls, and wider cross-sections, that explain lack of anisotropic growth, but cellulose organization appeared normal in xyloglucan-deficient root hairs. Brillouin microscopy suggested that xyloglucan-deficient root hairs were softer than wild-type root hairs. Finite element modeling of mutant root hairs predicted lower surface stress and growth rates, which were directly proportional to turgor pressure and inversely proportional to wall thickness. Stress patterns on models hint at potential mechanisms of bulging in xyloglucan-deficient root hairs. We propose that though cellulose orientation specifies the axis of cell expansion, it cannot enforce it without xyloglucan that interlinks microfibrils into a load-bearing network, identifying matrix-cellulose interaction as a distinct and necessary determinant of anisotropic plant cell growth.

10.5 PUCHI controls MYB94 and MYB96 to form the root cap cuticle of developing lateral roots.
Beckers A, Vantroeyen O, Cazevieille C … Guyomarc'h S.
Plant physiology · 2026-10-01
matched: root, roots, lateral root*, primary root*, root development, meristem*, development, organogenesis
abstract

Lateral root organogenesis underlies root branching and plays a key role in plant root system architecture. While the transcriptional and hormonal control of lateral root development has been extensively studied over the past decades, an increasing number of studies highlight the importance of specific metabolic pathways contributing to the lateral root regulatory network. The AP2/ERF transcription factor PUCHI has recently been shown to orchestrate the induction of very-long-chain fatty acid biosynthesis in developing lateral root primordia. In parallel, a root cap cuticle, reminiscent of the very-long-chain fatty acid-rich cuticle covering the emerging primary root, was shown to cover developing lateral root primordia and facilitate their emergence. Here, we demonstrate that PUCHI plays an essential role in lateral root cap cuticle formation. We identified the transcription factors MYB94 and MYB96 as key intermediates in this PUCHI-dependent, cuticle regulatory network. Similar to PUCHI, MYB94 and MYB96 induce the expression of a set of very-long-chain fatty acid- and cutin-related genes in lateral root primordia. The loss of function of either PUCHI or that of MYB94 and MYB96 compromises lateral root cuticle deposition, delays lateral root emergence, and alters the expression pattern of lateral root meristematic genes. These results shed light on a PUCHI- and MYB94 and MYB96-dependent regulatory network controlling the lateral root cap cuticle formation and influencing lateral root primordium development and emergence.

10.5 ZmCASP1 is involved in Casparian strip formation and salt tolerance in maize.
Liu J, Cui Y, Sun Y … Yan D.
Ca2+Abiotic stressCell biology and organellesPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-10
matched: apoplast*, salt stress, NaCl, cell wall*, root, roots, endoderm*, Casparian, lignin*
abstract

Casparian strips (CSs) are lignin-based cell-wall modification located in the vascular plant roots. This structure acts as an impermeable barrier restricting the non-selective apoplastic transport of water and solutes. CS formation represents a highly ordered and tightly regulated biological process in plants. Nevertheless, the molecular mechanisms underlying this process in maize remain largely elusive. In this study, we performed a functional characterization of ZmCASP1 (CASPARIAN STRIP DOMAIN PROTEIN 1) in maize (Zea mays), which is ubiquitously expressed in all three root types. Sequence alignment analysis uncovered the natural variations between the two maize inbred lines B73 and B104. Propidium iodide penetration assays and lignin staining observations revealed that the B104 haplotype displayed impaired endodermal barrier function and altered CS organization in the root. Consistently, the loss-of-function casp1 mutant generated in the B73 background exhibited identical phenotypic defects, supporting the involvement of ZmCASP1 in CS formation. Salt stress assays revealed that both B104 and the casp1 mutant displayed significantly reduced tolerance to NaCl treatment compared with B73. In line with these phenotypic observations, both B104 and the casp1 mutant accumulated higher levels of Na+ in aerial tissues, suggesting that CS defects may disrupt Na+ homeostasis. Collectively, we identified a genetic variation of ZmCASP1 in maize, and our results suggest ZmCASP1 is involved in CS formation and contributes to salt stress-associated ion homeostasis.

9.5 Rol-Transformed Kalanchoë Shows Altered Root Allocation and Photosynthetic Responses to Nitrogen Deficiency.
Esposito A, Saccardo G, El Khoury YV … Paponov IA.
HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityPhysiologia plantarum · 2026-09-01
matched: auxin*, ABA, cytokinin*, rhizobi*, root, root development, leaf, stoma*, shoot*, growth, development
abstract

Ri/rol transformation by Rhizobium rhizogenes is well known to alter plant architecture, including shoot compactness, branching and root development. However, its significance for plant responses to nitrogen limitation remains unclear. Here, we investigated developmental, physiological and hormonal responses of an interspecific Kalanchoë laciniata × K. blossfeldiana hybrid to nitrogen deficiency using complementary pot and hydroponic experiments. Nitrogen deficiency strongly reduced leaf area and total biomass in both wild-type (WT) and rol-transformed (Rol) plants. Nevertheless, rol transformation consistently produced a more compact, highly branched phenotype, with increased leaf number, greater side-shoot formation, higher specific leaf area, lower leaf dry matter content, and, in hydroponics, a striking increase in root mass allocation. Rol plants also maintained higher chlorophyll and carotenoid concentrations than WT and showed altered carbon partitioning, with lower soluble sugars but a higher relative proportion of sucrose. Under nitrogen deficiency, Rol plants maintained positive net photosynthesis and higher electron transport, whereas WT plants showed near-complete suppression of CO2 assimilation despite high intercellular CO2 concentration, pointing to strong non-stomatal limitation. Rol plants were characterized by lower ABA and PA concentrations, reduced auxin pools and enhanced leaf cytokinin status. Under low N, greater root allocation in Rol plants was not accompanied by reduced overall growth when root expansion was unrestricted, suggesting compensation through higher specific leaf area and maintenance of photosynthetic function. The strong shift towards root investment highlights a potential role of rol transformation in applications where belowground biomass and root development are especially relevant.

9.5 Temporal dynamics of photosynthetic inhibition, antioxidant defense, and transcriptomic reprogramming in 'Crimson Seedless' grapevine under saline-alkali stress.
Liu C, Wang H, Gai K … Yu Z.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionHormonesFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-24
matched: ROS, MAPK*, signaling, hormone*, defense, salt stress, nuclear, stoma*, seed*, growth
abstract

Soil salinization alkalization is among the most critical abiotic stressors affecting crop productivity yet the molecular physiological mechanisms governing grapevine (Vitis vinifera L.) responses to combined saline-alkali stress remain poorly understood. This study investigated the temporal dynamics of 'Crimson Seedless' grapevine plants propagated from cuttings. Integrated morphological transcriptomic physiological data were collected over a 15-day period of progressive saline-alkali stress. The results showed that saline-alkali stress affected photosynthesis in two phases. During the initial days of stress stomatal closure reduced CO2 uptake as evidenced by parallel drops in Pn Gs Ci. In the later stages non-stomatal limitations dominated. Pn decreased by 88% by day 15 while Ci rose despite low Gs. In addition stress triggered ROS accumulation membrane damage osmotic dehydration. Transcriptomic analysis revealed 3,795 differentially expressed genes (DEGs) at 7 days 4,084 at 15 days. Further analysis revealed that stress upregulated MAPK signaling hormone transduction flavonoid biosynthesis while downregulating photosynthesis-related ribosomal genes. Weighted gene co-expression network analysis (WGCNA) identified VvWRKY47 as a candidate transcription factor. Quantitative real-time PCR (qRT PCR) validation confirmed that VvWRKY47 expression was induced under saline-alkali conditions. Subcellular localization assays showed the nuclear localization of VvWRKY47 phenotypic analysis of transgenic Arabidopsis plants overexpressing VvWRKY47 revealed enhanced growth performance under salt stress compared to empty vector controls.

9.5 Uncovering genetic determinants of rice root anatomy using genome wide analysis
Tran, H. L., Hoang … A.
preprintKinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesbioRxiv · 2026-10-19
matched: kinase*, receptor*, drought, nutrient*, cell wall*, cell elongation, root, root development, epiderm*, growth, development
abstract

Root anatomy plays a critical role in water and nutrients acquisition and influences plant adaptation to abiotic stresses. A better understanding of the genetic control of anatomical traits is important to improve rice production, a staple crop for half of the worlds population. In this study, we measured eleven root anatomical traits in a collection of 185 Vietnamese rice landraces, and conducted genome-wide association studies to uncover the genetic architecture underlying these traits. A total of 24 significant small nucleotide polymorphisms were identified, corresponding to 19 quantitative trait loci for traits such as root cross section area, cortex area, metaxylem area or theoretical axial conductance. Interestingly, several root anatomical traits correlated with previously characterized root architectural traits measured within the same Vietnamese rice collection, and some genetic regions associated with anatomical traits and drought-tolerance-related traits colocalized. Candidate genes potentially involved in root development and cell wall patterning were identified within these QTLs regions. In particular, the significant SNP in qRA10.1 associated with metaxylem area induces an amino-acid change in the kinase domain of a wall associated kinase (WAK) receptor-like protein kinase (OsWAK111) with an extracellular epidermal growth factor (EGT)-containing domain that bind pectin, which presumably sense perturbations in this component to influence cell elongation during plant development. This study reveal key determinants of the genetic control of root anatomy in rice, which could be exploited to enhance tolerance to abiotic stresses such as drought.

9.0 Cell‐type‐specific PIN‐FORMED 3‐mediated polar auxin transport regulates cambial activity and tension wood formation in Populus tomentosa
Xiang D, Ran L, Li Y … Xu C
HormonesCell biology and organellesNew Phytologist · 2026-10-17
matched: auxin*, PIN\d+, polarity, stems, cambium, xylem, phloem, endoderm*, growth, development, differentiation
abstract

Summary Auxin is a central regulator of tree secondary growth and forms maxima in the vascular cambium, yet how these maxima are established remains elusive. Here, we functionally characterized two redundant PIN‐FORMED 3 ( PIN3 ) paralogs from Populus tomentosa to define their roles in secondary growth and gravity‐induced tension wood (TW) formation. We employed fluorescent reporter lines, clustered regularly interspaced short palindromic repeats‐generated knockout mutants, phloem‐specific RNA interference transgenic plants and isotopic auxin transport assays to dissect PIN3 functions during secondary growth of poplar stems. Loss of function of PIN3 paralogs severely compromises cambial proliferation, secondary xylem differentiation and TW development. PIN3 proteins display cell‐type‐specific polar localization with basal polarity in phloem cells and outer lateral polarity in endodermal cells. Phloem‐expressed PIN3 facilitates basipetal auxin transport and is required for cambial auxin response. Upon gravistimulation, endodermal PIN3 repolarizes along the gravity vector, correlating with TW formation. Our findings identify a cell‐type‐specific, PIN3‐mediated auxin transport mechanism that contributes to auxin‐driven secondary growth of tree stems.

9.0 Red and blue light antagonistically regulate tomato root system architecture by the auxin-mediated SlARF18-SlDRO1 module.
Duan J, Yu M, Li M … Guo Z.
HormonesAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesJournal of plant physiology · 2026-10-03
matched: auxin*, hormone*, nutrient*, nucleus, root, lateral root*, primary root*, leaf, shoot*
abstract

Root system architecture (RSA) is critical for water and nutrients uptake and is affected by light. However, the precise mechanisms by which light quality regulates RSA remain obscure. Here, we showed that red light (R) caused shorter primary root, larger lateral root angle and lower expression of DEEPER ROOTING 1 (SlDRO1), while blue light (B) led to the opposite trend compared to white light. Integrative analyses of hormone quantification, gene expression, GUS staining and auxin transport inhibitor 1-N-naphthylphthalamic acid (NPA) application revealed that the antagonistic effects of R and B on RSA depended on long distance transport of leaf-synthesized auxin from shoot to root. Transcriptome analysis identified a novel auxin response factor (ARF) transcription factor, SlARF18, whose expression paralleled light quality-induced changes in auxin content. Subcellular localization and transcriptional activation assays showed that SlARF18 protein was a nucleus-localized transcriptional repressor. The functions of SlARF18 and SlDRO1 in RSA were verified by virus-induced gene silencing. Yeast one-hybrid, dual-luciferase reporter assays and electrophoretic mobility shift assays revealed that SlARF18 inhibited SlDRO1 transcription by binding to its promoter. Collectively, our results demonstrate that R and B antagonistically regulate tomato RSA by the auxin-mediated SlARF18-SlDRO1 module, providing a novel mechanism for light quality-regulated RSA.

8.5 Engineering root development with high precision
Bilas RD, Guiziou S
Kinases and signal transductionMethodsNew Phytologist · 2026-10-16
matched: signalling, root, root development, root architecture, development, spatial transcriptom*
abstract

Summary Root development is controlled by dynamic interactions between gene regulation, hormonal signalling, developmental history and environmental inputs, creating multiple layers of control that can be harnessed for engineering. Recent advances in single‐cell technologies, spatial transcriptomics and synthetic biology are beginning to resolve root development at the cellular level and enable increasingly precise manipulation of developmental programmes. Here, we discuss how lineage tracing, genetic recording of developmental history, cell‐type‐specific engineering and programmable genetic circuits can be integrated to understand and control root architecture. We highlight emerging strategies for engineering adaptive and context‐dependent developmental behaviours, including history‐dependent and reversible response programmes and rewired root‐environment interactions. Together, these approaches establish a foundation for programmable and adaptive root system engineering in plants.

8.5 Root sequestration and antioxidant defense underpin cadmium tolerance in Arundo donax under contrasting Cd concentrations.
Zhang L, Shi H, Wu T … Wei F.
Ca2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressEcotoxicology and environmental safety · 2026-10-05
matched: H2O2, defense, cadmium, root, roots, leaf, leaves, shoot*, seed*, growth
abstract

Cadmium (Cd) contamination poses a persistent threat to plant growth and environmental safety, yet plant responses to contrasting Cd exposure remain insufficiently understood. Here, Arundo donax seedlings were hydroponically exposed to low (0.1 mM) and high (1.0 mM) Cd stress. Physiological responses were evaluated after 14 d, while transcriptomic profiling at 3 d was used to characterize early molecular responses. Total chlorophyll content decreased by 30.5% and 63.9% under low and high Cd, respectively, and high Cd reduced major gas-exchange parameters by more than 50%. High Cd increased O2•- levels by 273.2% and 202.5% in leaves and roots, respectively, and H2O2 by 91.8% and 160.1%, accompanied by enhanced lipid peroxidation and DNA damage. In response, antioxidant and osmotic defenses were activated, with leaf SOD activity increasing by 366.37% under high Cd. Cd was predominantly retained in roots, and the root-to-shoot translocation factor decreased from 0.126 under low Cd to 0.051 under high Cd. Early transcriptomic profiling identified 9798 and 10,964 DEGs in roots under low and high Cd, respectively, compared with 1541 and 4198 in leaves, indicating a stronger early transcriptional response in roots. Changes in Cd transport-related genes were broadly consistent with restricted Cd uptake and translocation and the potential involvement of sequestration-related pathways. Overall, A. donax exhibits dose-dependent physiological and molecular responses involving antioxidant defense, osmotic adjustment, and root-dominant Cd retention, supporting its potential for phytostabilization of Cd-contaminated environments.

8.0 Melatonin Coordinates Root Cell-Wall Zinc Remobilization and Shoot Transport via Nitric Oxide Signaling Under Zinc Deficiency in Rice.
Huang J, Lu QC, Huang W … Zhu XF.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionCell biology and organellesJournal of pineal research · 2026-11-01
matched: apoplast*, signaling, cell wall*, root, roots, xylem, shoot*, seed*, growth
abstract

Although zinc (Zn) deficiency severely inhibits rice growth, it remains unclear how melatonin (MT) promotes adaptation under conditions of insufficient Zn supply. To address this issue, rice seedlings were treated with MT, the NO donor sodium nitroprusside (SNP), or the NO scavenger c-PTIO, alone or in combination, under Zn-deficient conditions. Zn starvation rapidly promoted endogenous MT accumulation in rice roots, together with a 90.2%-179.5% rise in the transcript levels of MT biosynthetic genes. Exposure to Zn deprivation reduced root/shoot length and root/shoot biomass, by 46.2%/31.3% and 27.5%/39.6%, respectively. Among the tested concentrations, 10 μM MT produced the strongest protective effect, increasing root length, shoot height, root biomass, and shoot biomass by 59.9%, 34.2%, 21.7%, and 51.8%, respectively, relative to plants that were merely deficient in Zn. In Zn-deficient plants, MT raised Zn concentration in root and shoot both by 16.2% and xylem sap Zn concentration by 15.6%. MT also increased root cell-wall Zn content, pectin abundance, and pectin-associated Zn by 20.0%, 39.3%, and 31.9%, respectively. At the expression level, MT moderated the induction of OsZIP4, OsZIP5, OsZIP8, OsZIP9, OsZIP10, and OsHMA3 by Zn deficiency and further repressed OsZIP1, implying feedback regulation of the Zn-homeostasis network instead of direct activation of Zn-transporter transcription. Root NO content rose by 29.8% after MT treatment, and SNP elicited increases in root and shoot Zn concentrations comparable to those associated with MT. Overall, the data suggest that an NO-associated response contributes to MT-mediated Zn-deficiency tolerance by restoring Zn homeostasis, promoting apoplastic Zn remobilization and root-to-shoot transport, and limiting oxidative damage.

8.0 Unravelling the Multifunctional Roles of Plant LIM Proteins in Growth, Development, and Stress Responses.
Kour R, Sharma A, Mishra S … Srivastava V.
reviewPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesPlant science : an international journal of experimental plant biology · 2026-10-07
matched: immun*, stress respon*, cytoskelet*, actin, growth, development, differentiation, single-cell, CRISPR
abstract

LIM (Lin-11, Isl-1, and Mec-3 domain) proteins are evolutionarily conserved proteins that regulate diverse cellular processes in plants, including actin cytoskeleton organization, organ size determination, protein stability, and transcription-associated regulation. Therefore, these proteins have emerged as important regulators of plant growth, development, and responses to biotic and abiotic stresses. Numerous genome-wide studies, functional analyses, and mechanistic investigations in diverse plant species have considerably expanded our understanding of this gene family. However, these advances remain dispersed across the literature, present in peace-meal and have not been critically synthesized. This review summarizes recent progress in the classification and evolutionary diversification of plant LIM proteins, genome-wide identification and comparative analyses, and their multifunctional roles in plant growth and development, including organ size regulation, pollen and fibre development, and vascular differentiation. The review further critically evaluates current evidence for the involvement of LIM proteins in plant immunity and abiotic stress responses by distinguishing expression-based, functional, and mechanistic studies. Finally, the review discusses major knowledge gaps and highlights emerging research directions, including single-cell and spatial omics, phosphoproteomics, interactomics, and CRISPR/Cas-based genome editing, to advance mechanistic understanding and facilitate the exploitation of LIM proteins for crop improvement.

7.5 A native Bacillus velezensis RB6 from the passion fruit rhizosphere suppresses Phytophthora-induced root rot and promotes plant growth under nursery conditions.
Thuy NT, Trung DQ, Nam HQ … Dong BX.
HormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityArchives of microbiology · 2026-10-10
matched: IAA, pathogen*, Phytophthora, root, roots, seed*, growth, development
abstract

Root rot caused by Phytophthora spp. is a major constraint to passion fruit production. This study evaluated indigenous rhizobacteria isolated from healthy passion fruit plants for antagonistic activity, plant growth-promoting traits, and suppression of Phytophthora root rot under nursery conditions. Ten bacterial isolates were obtained, of which RB6 showed the strongest in vitro inhibition of Phytophthora mycelial growth, together with high phosphate-solubilizing, siderophore-producing, and extracellular hydrolytic activities. The colorimetric assay for indole compounds produced an IAA-equivalent signal; because the assay was based on Salkowski reagent in tryptophan-supplemented medium, this result was interpreted as detection of Salkowski-reactive indole compounds rather than definitive confirmation of IAA biosynthesis. Multilocus analysis of gyrA and gyrB resolved RB6 as Bacillus velezensis. Across the 2,145-bp concatenated alignment, RB6 showed 100% nucleotide identity to the B. velezensis reference strains CR-502/LMG 22478ᵀ and FZB42, and the RB6-B. velezensis grouping received 100% bootstrap support. In nursery experiments, RB6 promoted seedling growth and markedly reduced disease development, root disease severity, root rot severity, and mortality compared with the pathogen control. RB6 treatment was also associated with increased peroxidase (POD), polyphenol oxidase (PPO), phenylalanine ammonia-lyase (PAL), and catalase (CAT) activities and total phenolic content following pathogen challenge. Persistence of the inoculated bacterium on passion fruit roots was independently assessed by re-isolation followed by gyrA/gyrB sequencing. Of 30 presumptive colonies recovered from RB6-treated roots, 29 (96.7%) showed the expected RB6 gyrA/gyrB sequence profile, whereas no RB6-positive colonies were detected among 30 colonies examined from uninoculated controls. Overall, these findings support B. velezensis RB6 as a multifunctional native rhizobacterium with biocontrol and plant growth-promoting potential against Phytophthora root rot under nursery conditions.

7.5 The interplay between photoperiod and the circadian clock during vegetative growth
Chen J, Mishra N, Romanowski A
Abiotic stressPlant and Cell Physiology · 2026-10-14
matched: salinity, drought, root, root growth, leaf, hypocotyl*, flowering, growth
abstract

Abstract Photoperiod, the duration of the daily light phase, is a key seasonal signal that shapes vegetative growth in plants. While its role in controlling the transition to flowering has been extensively characterised, the mechanisms through which photoperiod modulates processes such as hypocotyl elongation, leaf expansion, root growth, and carbon allocation during the vegetative phase are only now coming into focus. Understanding these mechanisms is critical, as the vegetative phase establishes the architecture and resources needed for reproduction. Recent work has revealed that plants possess distinct daylength-measurement mechanisms for flowering and vegetative growth, with twilight-length sensing as a complementary mechanism. The circadian clock translates these signals into daily growth rhythms by restricting growth-promoting factors to defined time windows that widen or narrow depending on daylength, contributing to different growth outcomes between short and long photoperiods. Photoperiod further shapes the daily carbon budget, with opposing energy-sensing pathways dynamically adjusting growth to match carbon availability across the day-night cycle. In this review, we examine how photoperiod regulates vegetative growth in Arabidopsis thaliana, from daylength-measurement systems through clock-controlled transcriptional cascades that drive organ elongation and competitive shade avoidance responses, to carbon economy and stress tolerance. We consider how photoperiod modulates responses to temperature extremes, drought, and salinity, and survey emerging evidence from crops and trees. Finally, we discuss chronoculture, propose experimental recommendations, and highlight outstanding questions for the field.

7.5 The preprotein translocase YajC mediates biofilm formation and symbiotic nitrogen-fixation in Mesorhizobium huakuii.
Ren Q, Zou Q, Yang S … Cheng G.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityMethodsMicrobiology spectrum · 2026-10-06
matched: rhizobi*, symbio*, root, root hair*, growth, development, microscop*
abstract

In bacteria, the auxiliary complex SecDF-YajC participates in the Sec system, facilitating post-translational translocation of preproteins across the membrane through the SecYEG channel. Although YajC homologs are strongly conserved in rhizobial strains and may play important roles in many processes, the functions and mechanisms by which they are involved in the interaction between rhizobia and host legumes are unknown. By constructing Mesorhizobium huakuii yajC mutant, we observed an increase in the strain growth rate, but a reduction in both the biomass and average thickness of the biofilm in the yajC gene mutant compared to the wild type. Deletion of yajC gene results in severe suppression of competitive ability in the plant rhizosphere, alongside a substantial reduction in root hair curling and infection thread initiation during the early infection stages. The yajC-deficient mutant formed more numerous but smaller root nodules, with a 77% decrease in nitrogen-fixing capacity. Scanning electron microscopy analysis revealed that yajC mutant bacteroids displayed, alongside clear hallmarks of cellular deformation, dissociation, and premature senescence. From nodule bacteroid proteomic analysis, we further identified 210 differentially expressed proteins, including 32 transport-related proteins, and 10 associated with nitrogen fixation. Altogether, our findings reveal the importance of YajC-mediated transport and biofilm formation in rhizobial infection and nodule development during M. huakuii-Astragalus sinicus symbiosis.ImportanceIn Mesorhizobium huakuii-Astragalus sinicus symbiosis, deletion of the conserved Sec subunit YajC delays early biofilm formation by impairing flagellar-driven attachment. However, the yajC mutant restores wild-type biofilm biomass later, via upregulation of EPS synthesis genes (exoA/exoY), the PTS(Ntr) regulator ptsN, and polysaccharide deacetylase. This recovery contrasts with permanent biofilm defects in other bacteria, underscoring the need for longer-term assessment. YajC loss also disrupts nitrogenase activity by impairing iron-sulfur cluster biogenesis, Nif complex assembly, and electron transfer. As a compensatory response, plants form more nodules, offsetting reduced nitrogenase activity per nodule. Thus, multipath processes like biofilm formation show adaptability to secretion defects, whereas essential, linear pathways (e.g., nitrogenase maturation) remain highly sensitive. Our work redefines YajC: beyond its canonical role in Sec-mediated secretion, it is a rhizobium-specific factor critical for symbiosis, advancing understanding of secretion system roles in host-microbe interactions.

7.0 2-Naphthoic acid induces systemic phytotoxicity and impairs photosynthetic performance in maize.
Greco MG, Silva JJSD, Mendonça APDS … Marchiosi R.
Ca2+Abiotic stressJournal of plant physiology · 2026-10-01
matched: reactive oxygen species, nutrient*, root, roots, root development, leaves, germination, growth, development
abstract

The discovery of phytotoxic compounds remains essential for the development of next-generation herbicides. Here, we investigated the physiological effects of 2-naphthoic acid on maize, a model species with C4 photosynthesis, and complemented this analysis with germination assays in maize and four agronomically relevant weed species to assess interspecific variation in sensitivity and its potential as a lead compound for herbicide discovery. Maize plants were cultivated for 14 days under hydroponic conditions, with 2-naphthoic acid supplied through the nutrient solution. Exposure severely impaired plant growth, particularly root development and architecture, and reduced photosynthetic performance, including carbon dioxide assimilation, apparent quantum efficiency, photosystem II photochemical function, and electron transport. Chlorophyll a fluorescence analyses, including fast fluorescence transients, confirmed impaired photochemical performance, whereas maximum photosystem II quantum efficiency and the contents of chlorophyll a, chlorophyll b, and carotenoids remained unchanged. At the cellular level, 2-naphthoic acid induced redox imbalance, characterized by increased reactive oxygen species in leaves, enhanced conjugated-diene formation, and reduced antioxidant enzyme activities in leaves and roots. Acute in vitro assays revealed pronounced sensitivity of catalase to 2-naphthoic acid, whereas superoxide dismutase and peroxidase were not significantly affected. The compound was also associated with changes in mineral nutrient composition. Germination responses were species-dependent, with Digitaria insularis showing the strongest inhibitory response. Collectively, these findings identify 2-naphthoic acid as a potent phytotoxic compound and support its further evaluation for herbicide discovery.

7.0 Cytokinin regulates the gravitropic setpoint angle of seminal roots in barley and wheat
Bansod, A., Farkas … G. K.
preprintHormonesAbiotic stressbioRxiv · 2026-10-12
matched: cytokinin*, nutrient*, root, roots, root growth, gravitrop*, meristem*, growth
abstract

Plant root growth angle determines the spatial distribution of roots in soil and thereby influences access to water and nutrients when these resources are heterogeneously distributed. In barley, multiple seminal roots emerge at distinct gravitropic setpoint angles (GSAs) and they reliably re-establish their characteristic growth angles following reorientation, indicating robust intrinsic control. However, the molecular basis underlying GSA variation among roots within a single root system remains unknown. Here, we combined individual root type-specific transcriptomics with laser-capture microdissection to profile gravity-responsive zones (root cap, meristem, and elongation zone). We identified two CYTOKININ OXIDASE/DEHYDROGENASE ( CKX ) genes whose expression in the root cap was associated with root-specific GSA. Functional analysis showed that ckx mutants, which show higher cytokinin levels, exhibit significantly steeper seminal root GSAs, implicating cytokinin catabolism in root angle regulation. Consistent with this, exogenous cytokinin treatment induced a dose-dependent steepening in root angle in barley. Analyses of gravitropic curvature after gravistimulation further showed that ckx mutants exhibit altered gravitropic responses, indicating that CKX -mediated regulation of the root angle depends on gravity-responsive pathways. Notably, GSA-associated CKX expression patterns and cytokinin-dependent root angle responses were also observed in wheat, suggesting a conservation of this mechanism across cereals. Together, these findings identify cytokinin as a key regulator of root specific setpoint angles within a root system architecture, with potential implications for optimizing crop resource acquisition and yield.

7.0 Multiparametric confocal fluorescence imaging for quantitative nanocarrier mapping in plant leaves
Cardoso BC, Preisler AC, Fraceto LF.
preprintCell biology and organellesMethodsResearch Square · 2026-10-14
matched: cell wall*, leaf, leaves, stoma*, epiderm*, confocal, microscop*
abstract

Abstract Background. Tracking fluorescently labeled polymeric nanocarriers in leaf tissue using confocal laser scanning microscopy is limited by strong autofluorescence from chlorophyll and cell wall compounds, and by the absence of a common spatial reference against which the position of the detected signals can be compared across samples. To address these challenges, we describe an integrated workflow that reduces autofluorescence interference and provides a consistent spatial reference for signal localization. This procedure involves weed cultivation, foliar application of rhodamine-labeled nanocarriers by droplet deposition, and leaf processing along three complementary routes, namely, fixed tissue for localization, cryosectioned tissue for morphoanatomical characterization, and fresh tissue for the oxidative-response assay with 2',7'-dichlorodihydrofluorescein diacetate. Results. Tissue autofluorescence is deliberately recorded and employed twice: cell wall and cuticle emission reconstruct leaf architecture, while the intensity-weighted centroid of chlorophyll emission offers an internal axial reference. Axial intensity profiles of the same optical planes were used to derive two parameters: accumulated fluorescence, which integrates the profile after subtraction of paired untreated controls, and axial displacement (|Δz|), which is the distance between centroids of the label and chlorophyllous tissue. Because both centroids are derived from the same series, |Δz| is independent of the acquisition starting point relative to the leaf surface. The applicability of the approach is validated in a monocot ( Eleusine indica ) and a dicot ( Amaranthus viridis ), and two nanocarrier systems. The two species exhibited distinct surface distribution patterns consistent with their epidermal organization, displayed stomatal labeling, and were quantitatively differentiated using both parameters. Conclusions. Leaf autofluorescence, typically considered an obstacle to fluorescence-based nanocarrier tracking, can instead serve as a reference for localizing nanocarriers within leaf tissue. Accumulated fluorescence and axial displacement provided complementary information, quantifying signal intensity and its position relative to the photosynthetic tissue; notably, these two parameters were dissociated in one of the species tested. As the reference is biological, requires no additional labeling, and is acquired with the label, the workflow can be readily applied to other nanocarrier compositions and leaf species, thereby facilitating nanoherbicide design guided by the barriers and entry routes of target plants.

7.0 Phenotypic Discrimination of Routinely Identified Haploid and Diploid Sweet Corn Seedlings Using Multivariate Classification
Laurenze R, Chang PPC, Azrai M.
preprintResearch Square · 2026-10-12
matched: root, root development, root architecture, leaf, leaves, stoma*, guard cell*, shoot*, seed*, growth, development
abstract

Abstract Early discrimination of haploid seedlings is an important step in doubled haploid (DH) breeding because haploid seedlings are typically identified among a much larger population of predominantly diploid progeny. Phenotypic traits expressed during early seedling development have been proposed as useful indicators of haploidy, but their combined discriminatory value across sweet corn breeding materials remains insufficiently characterized. This study evaluated the potential of integrated seedling phenotyping to discriminate seedlings routinely classified as haploid or diploid in sweet corn. Seedlings derived from controlled donor × inducer crosses were characterized for shoot growth, leaf development, root architecture, chlorophyll content, and stomatal traits. Principal component analysis (PCA), pearson correlation analysis, random forest, and linear discriminant analysis (LDA) were used to characterize multivariate phenotypic patterns, identify informative traits, and evaluate classification relative to the routine haploid/diploid classification. Haploid-classified seedlings generally exhibited lower plant height, shorter and narrower leaves, reduced root development, and smaller guard cells, whereas stomatal number showed the opposite pattern. Multivariate analysis indicated that shoot growth, root development, and stomatal characteristics contributed jointly to separation between the two routinely classified groups. Random forest identified leaf length, plant height, stomatal number, and leaf width among the more informative traits, while LDA provided multivariate discrimination between the two groups. These findings indicate that early seedling phenotypes contain information that can support preliminary discrimination of routinely identified haploid and diploid seedlings.

7.0 Radial Water Loss Assay in Rice Roots Grown in Different Media.
Peralta Ogorek LL, Jamieson S, Pandey BK.
Ca2+Abiotic stressTrees and woody plantsBio-protocol · 2026-10-05
matched: apoplast*, drought, flooding, root, roots, growth, development, lignin*, microscop*
abstract

Plants growing in soil are exposed to several environmental stresses, such as soil compaction, flooding, or drought, and need to quickly acclimate to these conditions to survive. Such acclimations include suberization and lignification of the outer and inner parts of the root. Visualization of suberin and lignin depositions in roots can be done with several well-established staining techniques followed by microscopy, but these methods are mostly qualitative. Radial water loss assays provide a quick, robust, and quantitative method to evaluate the formation of suberized and/or lignified outer apoplastic barriers and how tightly they regulate water loss. Here, we provide a detailed step-by-step protocol, from harvesting the roots from soil conditions and cleaning them, to preparing them for water loss measurements, while using basic lab equipment. Since the data obtained is water loss as mass, quantitative and statistical comparisons can be performed. Finally, while in this protocol roots were grown in soil, the method can be applied to roots growing in hydroponics, agar plates, or any other growth conditions that allow non-destructive harvesting of roots. In summary, the protocol provides a fast and reliable method to assess differences in outer apoplastic barrier development of root tissues, allowing for fast root tissue screenings while obtaining quantitative data without expensive or highly specialized equipment. Key features • Ideal for root tissues of similar diameter. • Few preparation steps, and only simple lab equipment is needed. • Data can be obtained in three days.

7.0 Root responses to mechanical heterogeneity are shaped by pore geometry and root-soil biomechanics
Yao, J., de las Heras Martinez … L. X.
preprintCell biology and organellesMethodsbioRxiv · 2026-10-16
matched: cell wall*, root, roots, root growth, growth, live imaging, image analysis
abstract

Mechanical limitations to root growth occur when pressure exerted by turgor within root cells is counteracted by excessive resistance of the surrounding soil as well as by that of the cell walls. In a natural soil, however, heterogeneity allows roots to find paths of least resistance, though the governing principles behind this process have yet to be identified. Here, we combined artificial substrates, high-throughput live imaging, advanced image analysis, and mechanical modelling to uncover the principles governing the ability of a root to penetrate or deflect from zones of high mechanical impedance within a heterogeneous substrate. Our findings show that root responses to substrate heterogeneity can be accurately predicted from the pore-matrix geometry when both the mechanical properties of the substrate and those of the roots are known. Consequently, a crucial step toward understanding the physical limitations to root growth may lie in a detailed characterisation of soil mechanical properties.

7.0 The chloroplast-localised metalloprotease EGY1 and Mg-dechelatase SGR1 control chloroplast development and senescence by modulating photosynthetic functions
Shapiguzov A, Trotta A, Vainonen JP … Brosché M
HormonesCell biology and organellesJournal of Experimental Botany · 2026-10-14
matched: ethylene, chloroplast*, gravitrop*, leaves, guard cell*, development
abstract

Abstract The chloroplast plays an essential role in the production of biochemical energy and metabolism, as well as in plant development, including senescence. We describe a mutant with unusual progression of senescence and identify it as a new allele of ethylene-dependent gravitropism-deficient and yellow-green 1 (egy1-5). EGY1 encodes a chloroplast-localized metalloprotease that regulates chlorophyll metabolism. The single egy1-5 mutant exhibits various defects in chloroplast function, including abnormal accumulation of photosynthetic complexes and defects in photosynthetic parameters, as well as altered responses to the chemicals lincomycin and methyl viologen. Furthermore, chlorophyll fluorescence could not be detected in egy1 guard cells. We performed a suppressor mutant screen in the egy1 background to identify novel regulators of senescence and chloroplast development. We found two suppressors that restored green leaves, and both suppressors had mutations in STAY-GREEN 1 (SGR1). SGR1 encodes the Mg2+ dechelatase that catalyses the initial step in chlorophyll breakdown. The defects of the egy1 mutant were either fully or partially suppressed in egy1 sgr1. This allowed us to isolate the function of EGY1, independent of the pleiotropic effects of chlorophyll degradation. Our findings suggest that chlorophyll homeostasis regulates the overall development and function of the chloroplast as well as plant senescence.

6.5 Amorphous Silica and Drought Stress Mitigation: Unraveling Phenological Dynamics, Carbon-Water Balance, and Crop Resilience
Hamwi WA, Hoffmann M, Schaller J … Dubbert M.
preprintHormonesAbiotic stressTrees and woody plantsResearch Square · 2026-10-14
matched: abscisic acid, drought, leaf, stem, stoma*, flowering, growth, development, phenolog*
abstract

Abstract Amorphous silica (ASi) is increasingly recognized for its potential to improve plant resilience to abiotic stress, especially drought. However, because drought affects crop physiology as well as its carbon and water cycling differently depending on when drought occurs during plant development, the effects of ASi across key phenological stages remain poorly understood. In a greenhouse experiment, we investigated whether ASi as a soil amendment mitigates drought stress in spring barley (Hordeum vulgare L., variety: Planet) during three key phenological stages: stem elongation, flowering, and grain filling. A factorial design was used, combining two levels of ASi (0 as control and 1 g per 100 g of dry soil) with four water regimes: a well-watered reference and drought imposed during stem elongation, flowering, or grain filling. Carbon and water flux dynamics were monitored throughout the experiment by measuring CO 2 exchange and evapotranspiration using an automated soil-plant-atmosphere enclosure system, while soil water dynamics were tracked continuously with TOMST sensors. Plant responses to drought were assessed by daily measurements of stomatal conductance, leaf water potential, abscisic acid, and soil matric potential during drought. At the end of the growing period, biomass and grain yield were determined. ASi increased soil water retention over time and reduced drought-induced stress across growth stages. Drought effects were strongest during flowering and grain filling, whereas plants exposed to drought at stem elongation recovered fully and showed no yield reduction, irrespective of their ASi treatment. In contrast, drought during reproductive stages caused early senescence, poor recovery, and strong yield loss in control plants. These effects were substantially reduced by ASi, which maintained higher leaf water potential, stomatal conductance, evapotranspiration, and CO 2 uptake, while reducing abscisic acid accumulation. ASi also improved post-drought recovery and largely preserved yield under reproductive-stage drought. These results show that ASi can mitigate drought effects, especially during drought-sensitive reproductive stages, by helping sustain plant function during water shortages and delaying the onset of physiological stress.

6.5 Foliar polystyrene nanoplastics modulate cadmium uptake and root-to-shoot translocation in hydroponic Spinacia oleracea L.: Time- and concentration-dependent responses.
Dehghani MK, Ghasemi-Fasaei R, Abbasi S, Mojiri A.
Abiotic stressPloS one · 2026-10-08
matched: heavy metal*, cadmium, root, roots, shoot*, seed*, growth
abstract

The accumulation of plastics and heavy metals (HMs) in the environment has become a significant global concern due to their persistence, ubiquity, and potential ecological impacts. Atmospheric micro- and nanoplastics (MP, NP) are capable of undergoing long-range transport and can be deposited onto plant shoots through precipitation, thereby exposing aerial plant tissues to these particulate contaminants. In this study, the effects of varying concentrations of polystyrene nanoplastics (PSNPs) (0, 10, 50, 100, 200, and 400 mg L-1), applied via foliar spraying, and cadmium (Cd) (0 and 5 mg L-1) were evaluated on the growth parameters and chemical composition of spinach (Spinacia oleracea L.) grown under hydroponic conditions. The experiment was carried out over three exposure durations-2, 4, and 6 weeks-designated as t1, t2, and t3, respectively. Fifteen-day-old seedlings were transplanted into hydroponic containers and sampled at the end of each exposure period. The results demonstrated that PSNPs had a significant impact on plant growth and biomass accumulation. At t3, exposure to 200 mg L-1 of PSNPs resulted in an 83.33% and 87.34% increase in the dry weight of shoots and roots, respectively, compared to the control. However, this positive trend reversed at higher concentrations; at 400 mg L-1, shoot and root dry weights decreased by 49.87% and 27.87%, respectively, relative to the 200 mg L-1 treatment. Cd concentration in the shoot at t3 increased by 72.28% at 50 mg L-1 PSNPs compared to the control but declined by 31.32% at 400 mg L-1. The Cd translocation factor (TF) from root to shoot rose sharply with increasing PSNPs concentrations, reaching a peak increase of 194% at 400 mg L-1 at t3. Additionally, TF values exhibited a positive correlation with plant age, suggesting enhanced TF in older plants. These results highlight the potential risk of PSNPs in Cd-contaminated environments, where they may modify Cd uptake, distribution and root-to-shoot translocation in spinach plants. By facilitating Cd transfer to shoots, particularly under prolonged exposure, PSNPs could increase the likelihood of Cd accumulation in edible tissues. This interaction raises concerns regarding the safety of leafy vegetables grown in agricultural systems co-contaminated with MPs/NPs and HMs.

6.0 Comparative chromosome-scale genomics of the ayahuasca liana Banisteriopsis caapi reveals paleopolyploid ancestry and recent tandem-gene expansions as candidate signatures of ethnovariety differentiation
Trinca, V., Prosdocimi … A. M.
preprintKinases and signal transductionHormonesCell biology and organellesbioRxiv · 2026-10-16
matched: signaling, receptor*, hormone*, cell wall*, nuclear, stem, meristem*, development, differentiation
abstract

Background: Banisteriopsis caapi is the Amazonian liana that provides the {beta}-carboline-rich vine component of ayahuasca, a traditional Amazonian psychoactive preparation used in Indigenous and ceremonial contexts. The species encompasses culturally recognized ethnovarieties, some differing in stem architecture. Despite its cultural, ecological, and pharmacological importance, B. caapi remains largely unexplored at the genomic level. Here, we generated chromosome-scale genome assemblies for the caupuri and tucunaca ethnovarieties and used them to investigate genome evolution, structural variation, repeat composition, and candidate genomic features associated with ethnovariety-level differentiation. Results: Caupuri and tucunaca assemblies span 1.16 and 1.13 Gb, respectively, each organized into ten high-quality chromosome-scale pseudomolecules. Both genomes retain signatures of two ancient post-gamma duplication components, with related signals also detected in Malpighia emarginata, suggesting that at least part of this paleopolyploid history may predate the divergence of these Malpighiaceae lineages. Against this conserved chromosomal framework, the sampled caupuri and tucunaca genomes differ in structural variation, repeat composition, and gene-family profiles. Transposable element-associated insertions and deletions dominate the structural-variant landscape, while both genomes are highly repetitive, with approximately 78% of their assemblies composed of repetitive DNA largely shaped by LTR retrotransposons, including Gypsy/Retand and Copia/Ale, Copia/Tork, and Copia/Ivana lineages, together with non-autonomous large retrotransposon derivatives (LARDs). Most genes belong to shared orthogroups, indicating a largely conserved gene repertoire. However, candidate ethnovariety-biased differences emerge from the combined remodeling of gene families, LARD and LTR-associated genomic neighborhoods, and comparatively recent local expansions of tandem-duplicated gene arrays encoding signaling receptors, transporters, hormone-associated genes, cell wall and secondary wall functions, and meristem or organ-development pathways. Conclusions: These results indicate that the sampled caupuri and tucunaca genomes differ mainly through localized remodeling of shared genomic modules, including comparatively recent repeat turnover and local tandem-gene expansions. These assemblies provide the first large-scale nuclear genomic resource for B. caapi and a foundation for investigating paleopolyploid evolution in Malpighiaceae and candidate mechanisms of ethnovariety diversification.

6.0 Constitutive elevation of cAMP in Arabidopsis thaliana enhances plant growth, development, and productivity.
Li B, Guo Y, Xu S, Xu R.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesPlant-microbe interactions and immunityFunctional plant biology : FPB · 2026-10-01
matched: signalling, ethylene, pathogen*, salinity, stress respon*, seed*, growth, development
abstract

Cyclic adenosine 3',5'-monophosphate (cyclic AMP; cAMP) has emerged as a bona fide signalling molecule in plants. Although its regulatory roles in diverse cellular processes are increasingly recognised, the transient nature of cAMP signalling has limited our understanding of its systemic functions at the whole-plant level. In this study, we engineered Arabidopsis thaliana plants to constitutively express adenylate cyclase (AC) activity, thereby enabling sustained elevation of endogenous cAMP levels. The AC transgenic lines exhibited a 1.3- to 1.8-fold increase in endogenous cAMP content relative to wild-type plants. Elevated cAMP promoted accelerated growth and developmental progression, enhanced photosynthetic performance, increased cellular metabolic activity, and improved productivity, as evidenced by 13-14% greater plant height, 12-17% higher 1000-seed weight, and 36-41% greater seed yield per plant. However, these growth advantages were accompanied by increased susceptibility to salinity stress, pathogen infection, and exogenous ethylene treatment compared with wild-type plants. Collectively, our findings establish cAMP as a central regulator of plant physiology that coordinates growth, development, metabolism, and stress responsiveness. While sustained elevation of endogenous cAMP enhances plant growth and productivity, it also increases sensitivity to environmental stimuli, highlighting the importance of precise spatiotemporal regulation of cAMP signalling in maintaining optimal plant performance.

6.0 Explainable growth stage classification of cacao (Theobroma cacao L.) leaves and key feature visualization using vision transformer and transfer learning.
Ahn E, Park ES, Kim MS … Baek I.
Trees and woody plantsMethodsFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-21
matched: leaf, leaves, phloem, growth, development, hydraulic*, deep learning
abstract

Accurate and objective plant phenotyping is crucial for optimizing agricultural practices, understanding plant development, and enabling rapid responses to environmental changes. Traditional methods, often relying on visual observation, can be subjective, time-consuming, and may overlook subtle but important differences. This study demonstrates the power of combining digital imaging with deep learning to classify plant material with high accuracy, even when visual differences are minimal. We focused on differentiating between stage D (light green) and stage E (dark green) leaves of cacao (Theobroma cacao L.), which are visually very similar in size and overall structure. Using cleared and stained leaves of the SCA 6 genotype to highlight the venation network, we trained a Vision Transformer (ViT) model, a deep learning architecture, on image patches. At the patch level, the model achieved an overall accuracy of 97.03% on an independent test set, with a recall of 96.0% for stage D and 98.3% for stage E. At the whole-leaf level, majority voting correctly classified 14 of 15 independent test leaves (93.3%). Attention maps indicated that image regions containing the midrib and primary lateral veins contributed strongly to classification. These attention maps identify discriminative image regions, but they do not by themselves determine the biological mechanism underlying the signal. The major-vein signal may reflect developmental differences in vein-associated structure, stage-associated differences in Safranin O uptake or optical density, tissue thickness, or a combination of these factors. Because vascular anatomy, lignification, hydraulic conductance, phloem loading, and source-sink status were not directly measured, these mechanisms are treated as hypotheses requiring future anatomical, histochemical, and physiological validation. Thus, this study provides a proof-of-concept for interpretable image-based classification of stage D and stage E leaves within greenhouse-grown SCA 6 cacao. Extension to other cacao genotypes, field-grown plants, independent seasons, staining batches, stress detection, species identification, genotype discrimination, or precision-agriculture deployment will require external validation.

6.0 Impact of macronutrients and monsoon on the AM fungal community structure within lateral root systems of Hevea brasiliensis.
Vinod A, Ravindran M, Joseph P … Uthup TK.
Ca2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressWorld journal of microbiology & biotechnology · 2026-10-14
matched: calcium, mycorrhiz*, symbio*, nutrient*, root, roots, lateral root*
abstract

Arbuscular mycorrhizal fungi (AMF) are important root symbionts that enhance nutrient acquisition, water uptake, stress tolerance, and soil ecosystem functioning in terrestrial plants. In perennial plantation systems like Hevea brasiliensis, understanding the dynamics of root-associated AMF communities under varying seasonal and nutrient conditions is essential for developing sustainable nutrient management strategies, particularly in emerging rubber growing regions. In the present study, we investigated the, composition, diversity, and seasonal dynamics of AMF communities directly associated with the roots of 12 year old H. brasiliensis plants maintained under long term nutrient withdrawal treatments. AMF specific barcoding regions were selectively amplified and sequenced from root samples collected during pre- and post-monsoon seasons. Species level analysis revealed seasonal differences in AMF community structure, with post-monsoon samples having comparatively higher abundance and species richness. Pre-monsoon communities were largely dominated by members of the Glomeraceae, whereas post-monsoon samples showed greater phylogenetic diversity, including representatives of Diversisporaceae, Acaulosporaceae, and Entrophosporaceae. Nutrient omission treatments showed variation in root AMF community composition with respect to the availability of phosphorus, nitrogen, potassium, calcium, and magnesium. The treatment specific occurrence of Acaulospora scrobiculata, Entrophospora infrequens, Acaulospora fragilissima, and Dominikia bernensis identified them as potential candidates for further ecological investigation under nutrient deficient conditions, whereas the consistent occurrence of Glomus, Rhizophagus intraradices, Dominikia disticha, and Dominikia gansuensis across multiple treatments suggests that they may constitute a core component of the symbiotic fungal community within rubber roots. Overall, the findings suggest that seasonal changes and nutrient stoichiometry may be associated with variation in root-associated AMF community structure in Hevea. The study also identified candidate AMF species for further investigation toward sustainable rubber cultivation under changing tropical conditions.

6.0 Mapping the epigenetic regulatory network of cotton single‐cell fiber elongation mediated by H3K4me3
Yu M, Liu L, Guo Y … Yang Z

matched: immun*, cell elongation, seed*, trichome*, epiderm*, development, morphogen*, CRISPR
abstract

Summary Histone modifications are central to plant development, yet how they coordinate cellular morphogenesis remains poorly understood. Cotton fibers, as single‐celled trichomes derived from seed epidermal cells, provide an ideal model to address this question. Multi‐omics approaches were used, including cleavage under targets and tagmentation for genome‐wide H3K4me3 profiling and transcriptome sequencing. These were integrated with molecular and genetic validation experiments such as virus‐induced gene silencing, overexpression, CRISPR/Cas9, yeast one/two‐hybrid, electrophoretic mobility shift assay, co‐immunoprecipitation, and split‐luciferase complementation assays.
