6.0 Nitrogen form regulates ginsenoside biosynthesis in Panax ginseng adventitious roots: comparative transcriptomics of ammonium- and nitrate-mediated pathways.
Zhang S, Yuan Q, Qiu R … Li X.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-28
matched: signal transduction, signaling, IAA, hormone*, nitrate, root, roots, root growth, growth, development
abstract

Panax ginseng C. A. Mey. is a valuable Chinese herbal medicine. Saponins are one of its main active ingredients, possessing various important pharmacological activities and serving as key indicators for evaluating ginseng quality. Due to the depletion of wild ginseng resources, adventitious root culture has emerged as an effective approach for ginsenoside production; however, the ginsenoside content in cultured adventitious roots remains relatively low. Nitrogen, as an essential macronutrient for plant growth and development, plays a vital role in both biomass accumulation and secondary metabolite biosynthesis. Different nitrogen forms exert distinct regulatory effects on the synthesis of triterpenes, but the molecular mechanism of ammonium nitrogen and nitrate nitrogen regulating the accumulation of ginsenosides in ginseng adventitious roots is not clear. In this study, we combined physiological measurements with comparative transcriptomic analysis to investigate the regulatory pathways underlying adventitious root growth and differential ginsenoside accumulation under nitrate-only, ammonium-only, and nitrogen-free conditions at the optimal nitrogen concentration. Physiological results showed that nitrate nitrogen supply significantly increased adventitious root biomass and total ginsenoside content compared with ammonium nitrogen. Further analysis showed that the core genes of ginsenoside synthesis pathway were upregulated under nitrate nitrogen conditions. Nitrate nitrogen can promote endogenous nitric oxide (NO), and further pharmacological experiments suggest that NO likely acts as an important intermediate signal contributing to nitrate-driven promotion of adventitious root growth and ginsenoside synthesis. The results clarify the molecular mechanism of nitrogen form regulating ginseng secondary metabolism and provide a theoretical basis for optimizing the nitrogen strategy of ginseng adventitious root culture and improving the yield of ginsenosides.

6.0 Systematic Analysis of the DOF Gene Family in Flowering Chinese Cabbage (Brassica campestris) and the Role of BcDOF56 in Abiotic Stress Adaptation
Liu J, Liu J, Chen C … Zhu Y.
preprintKinases and signal transductionHormonesAbiotic stressPreprints.org · 2026-10-12
matched: signaling, hormone*, drought, stress respon*, nucleus, flowering, growth, development
abstract

DNA-binding with One Finger (DOF) transcription factors play crucial roles in modulating plant growth, development, and environmental stress responses. However, the DOF family in flowering Chinese cabbage (Brassica campestris) remains poorly characterized. Here, 67 BcDOF genes were identified in B. campestris, distributed nonuniformly across ten chromosomes and classified into nine subfamilies. Structural analyses revealed that all BcDOF proteins harbor a conserved zinc-finger DOF (zf-DOF) domain, with members within individual subfamilies generally sharing similar motif architectures and exon-intron structures. Evolutionary analyses indicated that whole-genome triplication and segmental duplication were the major drivers of BcDOF family expansion, while purifying selection contributed to the evolutionary conservation of duplicated gene pairs. Promoter analysis revealed abundant cis-acting elements related to hormone signaling and abiotic stress responses. Collectively, these findings advance our understanding of the evolutionary and functional divergence of the BcDOF family and highlight BcDOF56 as a potential target for improving abiotic stress tolerance in B. campestris.

6.0 Zinc homeostasis in plants: the role of ZIP, MTP and HMA transporters and chelating agents
Borzęcka E, Barabasz A.
reviewCell biology and organellesTrees and woody plantsPostepy biochemii · 2026-10-06
matched: vacuol*, organelle*, xylem, phloem, shoot*, seed*, growth, development
abstract

Zinc (Zn) is an essential micronutrient involved in numerous physiological processes in plants, serving catalytic, structural, and regulatory functions. Maintaining its appropriate concentration requires precise control of uptake, transport, distribution, and storage, as both Zn deficiency and excess lead to disruptions in plant growth and development. This paper presents the mechanisms regulating zinc homeostasis in plants, with a particular focus on the role of membrane transporters and chelating compounds. The ZIP, MTP, and HMA protein families play a key role, being responsible for cellular Zn uptake, its sequestration within organelles, and inter-tissue transport, respectively. Low-molecular-weight chelating ligands maintain safe zinc levels in the cytosol, and some of them facilitate its storage in vacuoles. Furthermore, they enhance its symplastic mobility and enable zinc transport in the xylem and phloem, thereby facilitating its distribution to shoots and seeds. Overall, this demonstrates the complex and multilevel nature of zinc homeostasis regulation in plants

17.5 CRISPR/Cas9 knockout of PtaAGL16 improves post-drought recovery and shoot vitality in hybrid poplar
Fendel, A., Wodtke … T.
preprintAbiotic stressGrowthMethodsbioRxiv · 2026-10-06
matched: drought, stress respon*, leaves, stem, stoma*, shoot*, growth, poplar*, Populus, tree, trees, woody
abstract

Drought stress severely limits tree growth and survival, a problem intensifying under climate change due to the rising frequency of extreme weather events. Identifying genetic variants that modulate drought stress responses and enhance resilience is therefore of great interest for developing climate-adapted trees. While the MADS-box transcription factor AGL16 has been implicated in drought responses and stomatal regulation in herbaceous species, the function of its homolog in woody perennials remains largely unexplored. Here, we investigate the role of PtaAGL16 in the poplar hybrid Populus tremula × P. alba . We generated CRISPR/Cas9 knockout lines of PtaAGL16 in the poplar clone INRA 717-1B4. Plants were subjected to a severe drought stress regime followed by a re-watering recovery phase. Morphological and physiological traits, including growth, stomatal conductance, shoot vitality, and stomatal density, together with stress-associated metabolites and drought-responsive gene expression, were analyzed. All agl16 knockout lines exhibited significantly reduced drought-induced damage compared to the wild-type. These findings indicate that the disruption of PtaAGL16 is associated with enhanced drought resilience in a woody perennial species and highlight its potential as a genetic target for improving climate adaptation in fast-growing forest trees.

17.0 Fluid-structure interactions in the xylem: A nonlinear reaction-diffusion model of hydraulic segmentation and pressure-actuated microvalves.
Hernández-Hernández LA, Pérez-España VH, Hernández-Hernández A … Martínez-Farías FJ.
reviewAbiotic stressGrowthMethodsBio Systems · 2026-10-13
matched: drought, stem, stems, xylem, conifer*, tree, trees, hydraulic*, microfluidic*, segmentation
abstract

While the Cohesion-Tension theory explains primary advective sap ascent, standard continuous models often neglect the mechanical regulatory role of bordered pits during secondary transport, such as capillary refilling. To address this, we model the conifer xylem as a network of pressure-actuated elastic microvalves governed by fluid-structure interactions. By coupling Darcy flow with state-dependent hydraulic capacitance and aquaporin-mediated transcellular refilling, we derive a macroscopic, asymmetric Fisher-Kolmogorov reaction-diffusion equation. Analytically, unimpeded fluid recovery propagates as coherent saturation wavefronts bounded by a Kolmogorov-Petrovsky-Piskunov critical velocity, which places the threshold on the same scale as overnight hydraulic recovery, although a front velocity for conifer stems is not at present well constrained empirically. If macroscopic diffusivity or elastic buffering rates drop below this structurally invariant limit, the wavefront collapses, providing a theoretical analog for catastrophic hydraulic failure. Conversely, once a refilling front separates a continuous liquid column from an embolized compartment held near atmospheric pressure by its gas phase, the membrane between them sustains the full head of that column-as tension developed at the column's own top, not as a weight resting upon it-and the microvalves actuate when this differential exceeds the elastic restitution limit of the margo. Crucially, our framework evaluates this topological shift via a piecewise integral: valve closures generate structural septa that physically isolate the lower fluid column and mathematically reset the pressure field for subsequent ascent. This provides a formal mathematical demonstration of hydraulic segmentation-the iterative stacking of mechanically decoupled fluid reservoirs, each of which need only sustain the tension generated over its own height rather than over the whole tree. Furthermore, by deducing a state-dependent capacitance factor to enforce absolute fluid-volume conservation, the closed-valve asymptotic limit yields an exact localized profile that functions as a threshold rather than an attractor: it sets a minimum sequestered volume, 4D/γ, below which an isolated reserve is reabsorbed by the surrounding living tissue. These profiles represent hydraulically sequestered capacitive buffers, mathematically mirroring how trees sequence drought responses to prioritize stem elastic water storage. Because the hydrostatic multiplier is fixed by the specific weight of water and the margo threshold has been measured directly, the predicted spacing of these septa follows with no adjustable parameter, and places segmentation in the range 33-97 m for the Pinaceae, its onset governed by the weakest pits of the population-within the range of heights attained by the tallest conifers. We emphasise that this remains a mechanism permitted by the physics and compatible with the known biology rather than one yet demonstrated: its distinctive signature, a discrete vertical periodicity in the aspirated state of the pit population, has not been looked for. Ultimately, this perspective on vascular fault tolerance offers scalable blueprints for resilient, biomimetic microfluidic technologies.

13.0 Hydraulic, Gas-Exchange and Leaf-Trait Variation Across Hydrologically Contrasting Sites and Tree Sizes in Populus euphratica.
Zhang S, Yang X, Li Z … Lv G.
GrowthPhysiologia plantarum · 2026-09-01
matched: leaf, stoma*, xylem, epiderm*, Populus, tree, trees, hydraulic*
abstract

Plant water status, hydraulic transport and carbon exchange can respond differently across natural hydrologic contrasts. We measured hydraulic, gas-exchange, carbon, morphological and anatomical traits in 33 Populus euphratica trees distributed across four fixed sites (D1-D4) and three tree-size classes. Site and size associations were evaluated with additive models, heteroskedasticity-consistent inference, false-discovery-rate control and a balanced D1-D3 sensitivity analysis. Midday leaf water potential became progressively more negative from D1 to D4, whereas branch xylem hydraulic conductivity was higher at D3-D4. The correlated instantaneous responses of assimilation (A), stomatal conductance (gs), and transpiration (E) peaked at D3 and declined at D4. Within the balanced D1-D3 subset, site effects for midday water potential, hydraulic conductivity and the A-gs-E response group remained supported. Larger trees had lower specific leaf area, soluble sugar and total non-structural carbohydrates, but higher leaf water content and palisade tissue thickness; starch showed a consistent site association. After adjustment for site and size, no anatomy-function association based on total palisade tissue thickness (PT) or epidermis thickness (Epi) retained false-discovery-rate support. Hydrologic context and tree size were associated with partly distinct functional dimensions, indicating multidimensional and context-dependent intraspecific variation in P. euphratica.

13.0 Quantifying Dynamic Changes in Stem Storage‐Water Use and Rehydration in Mature Trees: A Study on Pinus sylvestris Trees Exposed to Increased Atmospheric and Soil Drought
Peters RL, Buras A, Grichting SA … Zweifel R
Abiotic stressGrowthPlant, Cell & Environment · 2026-10-11
matched: drought, stem, stoma*, growth, Pinus, tree, trees, sap flow, dendro*
abstract

ABSTRACT Forests increasingly experience hot droughts, characterized by rising vapour pressure deficits (VPD) and declining soil moisture, highlighting the need to better understand water fluxes within trees. While stomatal conductance and transpiration are well studied, upstream fluxes such as internal stem water storage use and rehydration dynamics remain poorly quantified, despite their importance for maintaining tissue hydration and growth. Here, we quantify storage‐water use and rehydration dynamics by combining high‐resolution sap flow and dendrometer measurements in mature Pinus sylvestris at the VPDrought experiment of the Pfynwald research platform, where rain‐water input and VPD are independently manipulated. Fluxes were analyzed over the 2024 growing season in relation to VPD and soil water potential ( ψ soil ). The relative contribution of internal stem water storage to daily transpiration increased with drought, rising from about 10% under well‐watered conditions to up to 55% under dry conditions, mainly caused by reduced transpiration while storage water use is sustained. Notably, under mild soil drought, high nighttime VPD substantially constrained nighttime tissue rehydration fluxes. Overall, our novel framework highlights the dynamic role of stem water storage in modulating water potentials during drought and demonstrates the importance of explicitly considering temporal stem water storage dynamics when assessing tree water‐use strategies.

12.5 A Novel In Vitro Regeneration System Reveals Tissue-Specific Dynamics of Wound Repair in Poplar Stems
Wang X, Hu J, Zhu M, Zhang J.
preprintHormonesCell biology and organellesGrowthPreprints.org · 2026-10-10
matched: auxin*, cell division, stem, stems, cambium, phloem, development, poplar*, woody, lignin*
abstract

Plants are prone to injury in nature, yet how woody stems repair damaged protective and vascular tissues remains poorly understood. We established an in vitro bridge-culture system in hybrid poplar to track regeneration after longitudinal stem cutting. Lignin deposition began in diverse cells beneath the wound by 2 days after cutting (DAC), marking an early wound response. Repair then followed distinct tissue-specific trajectories. In the cortex and secondary phloem, cell division generated callus-like tissue that covered the wound by 2–4 DAC. New phellogen subsequently formed beneath the callus and produced phelloderm and ligno-suberized phellem, forming a wound periderm resembling native periderm by 12 DAC. In vascular cambium and young phloem, cells expanded and proliferated to reconnect the wound by 4 DAC, while restoration of cambium- and phloem-associated gene expression by 8–12 DAC indicated vascular recovery. Auxin responses also differed between tissues. PtaDR5::GUS activity was transiently induced during periderm regeneration but persisted in vascular regions until vascular development resumed. Exogenous auxin promoted periderm regeneration but had little effect on vascular cell divisions, whereas inhibiting polar auxin transport impaired vascular wound closure. Thus, periderm and vascular tissues undergo coordinated yet distinct repair processes regulated by tissue-specific auxin responses and transport.

12.5 Regeneration After Severe Wildfire: Hotter Microclimates Exceed Hydraulic and Thermal Tolerances in Sierra Nevada Conifer Seedlings.
Kerr KL, Trugman AT, Anderegg LDL.
Abiotic stressGrowthPlant, cell & environment · 2026-10-06
matched: drought, heat stress, leaf, stoma*, seed*, growth, conifer*, tree, hydraulic*
abstract

Tree seedling regeneration after wildfire will determine the long-term trajectory of forests under climate change, yet increasing drought and heat extremes can suppress regeneration and result in forest type conversion. We combined field observations and a controlled drought-heat growth chamber experiment to investigate how these stressors independently and jointly affect drought- and heat-tolerance physiology in seedlings of montane conifer species from drought- and wildfire-prone regions in the southern Sierra Nevada. In both the field and growth chamber experiment, we found that exposure to elevated leaf temperatures in post fire-like environments dwarfed the effect of physiological adjustments in heat tolerance (temperatures causing initial impairment (Tcrit) or 50% loss (T50) of photosystem II capacity) and drought tolerance (turgor loss point). In the field, this resulted in narrower thermal safety margins across five conifer species in burn scars with no remaining canopy, while in the growth chamber experiment three species showed narrower or even negative thermal and hydraulic safety margins. In the growth chamber experiment, leaf temperatures were also frequently decoupled from air temperature, highlighting that biophysical feedbacks between leaf temperature and transpiration may amplify seedling water loss even under stomatal closure. For both the field and growth chamber experiment, we found no evidence of a trade-off between drought and heat tolerance within or among species. Among species, seedlings of the range-restricted Sequioadendron giganteum (giant sequoia) were observed to be the most vulnerable to combined drought and heat stress in the growth chamber experiment. Our results highlight that direct thermal stress may strongly constrain post-fire conifer regeneration and underscore the need to integrate seedling energy balance and microclimate into predictions of post-fire recovery and management strategies, especially for vulnerable species such as giant sequoia.

12.5 Single-nucleus RNA sequencing reveals a key regulator RsHB8 affecting vascular cambium activity and xylem differentiation during radish taproot thickening.
Cui F, Li J, Bi H … Liu L.
Cell biology and organellesGrowthMethodsJournal of integrative plant biology · 2026-10-14
matched: nucleus, cambium, xylem, development, differentiation, single-cell
abstract

Radish taproot thickening is primarily driven by vascular cambium (VC) activity. However, the transcriptional regulatory mechanisms underlying taproot thickening at a single-cell resolution remain unclear. In this study, single-nucleus isolation coupled with fluorescence-activated cell sorting was used to construct a cell atlas of radish taproots using microdroplet-based single-nucleus RNA sequencing. Through canonical marker gene profiling and validation using laser capture microdissection transcriptome data, 12 cell types were identified, and the first single-nucleus transcriptomic atlas was constructed for radish taproots. VC cells exhibited heterogeneous transcriptional states with varying levels of expression of cell cycle-associated genes. Pseudotime analysis inferred a transcriptional trajectory from VC cells toward xylem parenchyma cells in radish taproots. Integrating pseudotime analysis with predicted protein-protein interaction networks highlighted HOMEOBOX 8 (RsHB8) as a candidate regulator of the inferred VC-to-xylem parenchyma (XP) trajectory. RsHB8m overexpression increased taproot width and promoted xylem differentiation, whereas RsHB8-silenced plants exhibited a reduced taproot width and narrower xylem region. Xyloglucan endotransglucosylase/hydrolase 22 (RsXTH22) was identified as a target gene of RsHB8. Silencing RsLBD21 significantly increased taproot and xylem width, whereas silencing RsXTH22 resulted in the opposite phenotypes. Together, these findings establish a single-nucleus transcriptomic atlas of radish taproot development and provide insights into the transcriptional regulation of VC differentiation during taproot thickening.

12.5 Thioredoxin h1-targeted proteins in European beech (Fagus sylvatica L.): Insights into metabolic control, stress response, and seed longevity.
Ratajczak E, Staszak AM, Vargas P … Fuchs H.
Ca2+Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressPloS one · 2026-10-05
matched: calcium, calmodulin*, signaling, stress respon*, seed*, embryo*, beech, Fagus
abstract

Seed viability and longevity underpin forest regeneration under changing environmental conditions. European beech (Fagus sylvatica L.) produces intermediate seeds that gradually lose viability during storage, largely through oxidative stress, which makes redox homeostasis central to their longevity. Here we investigated the role of thioredoxin h1 (TRX h1), a cytosolic thiol-disulfide oxidoreductase, in the embryonic axes of beech seeds. Using a His-tagged monocysteinic (Cys to Ser) trapping mutant of TRX h1 combined with data-dependent acquisition (DDA) proteomics, we identified 161 putative TRX h1 target proteins, which fall into functional categories related to genetic information processing, metabolism, environmental and cellular signaling, and protein homeostasis. Western blotting confirmed that target capture proceeded through DTT-reducible mixed-disulfide complexes, supporting a redox-based interaction. The targets include heat shock proteins (HSP70 and HSP90) and other chaperones, calcium-binding proteins (calmodulin and calreticulin), a methyl-CpG-binding protein, glycolytic and tricarboxylic acid (TCA) cycle enzymes, and antioxidant enzymes such as superoxide dismutase, glutathione peroxidase and glutaredoxin. To link these targets to seed physiology, we quantified the thiol redox status of fresh and long-term stored seeds: total thiols were approximately 2.2-fold lower in stored than in fresh axes (21.0 ± 0.5 vs 45.9 ± 1.3 nmol GSH equivalents per axis), indicating that the reduced thiol pool, the substrate of the TRX h1 system, becomes depleted and more oxidized during storage. Together, these results suggest that TRX h1-dependent redox maintenance may contribute to redox homeostasis, energy metabolism and protein stability in beech embryonic axes, and provide a basis for improving seed storage and conservation strategies.

11.5 A high spatial resolution transcriptomics of tension wood reveals the genetic program of contractile G-layer formation.
Kumar M, Grones C, Allen H … Mellerowicz EJ.
Kinases and signal transductionAbiotic stressCell biology and organellesFrontiers in plant science · 2026-09-23
matched: signaling, stress respon*, cell wall*, cytoskelet*, differentiation, Populus, woody, tension wood, microscop*
abstract

Tension wood (TW) formation in Populus involves the differentiation of G-fibers that deposit a cellulose-rich, contractile gelatinous layer (G-layer). Despite extensive study, the biosynthetic programs underlying G-layer formation have remained incompletely resolved, in part due to imprecise sampling of developmental stages. Here, we generate a high spatial-resolution transcriptome atlas of developing Populus tremula wood by tangential cryo-sectioning of TW and normal wood (NW) forming tissue. We overlaid microscopically determined cell wall thickening profiles with whole-transcriptome data, resulting in seven distinguishable TW and three NW stages and a clear separation of early and late G-layer-specific transcriptional programs during TW formation. Across 22,363 expressed genes, 90 co-expression clusters captured major programs defining cell wall biosynthesis, signaling, cytoskeleton dynamics, and stress responses. Differential expression analysis identified 2,898 genes specifically regulated during early or late G-layer formation-indicating a massive transcriptional reorganization unique to TW. Genes involved in cellulose, rhamnogalacturonan I, arabinogalactan II, and callose metabolism showed distinct induction patterns, including CAZymes not previously implicated in G-layer formation. We also identified G-layer-specific genes, including XTH26 (PtXTH5), SCL18, AGL29, and genes encoding FLA12-like AGPs and LIM protein. Thirty transcription factors different than the secondary wall-inducing NACs and MYBs, and several genes involved in hormonal regulation specifically induced at the onset of G-layer formation represent novel putative regulators of the tertiary G-layer differentiation. Together, this spatially resolved transcriptome provides the most detailed map to date of the molecular program underlying TW formation, offering new insights into G-layer biosynthesis, signaling networks, and cell wall specialization in woody plants.

11.5 Mapping wood formation across scales: Models, methods and experiments
Hartmann FP, Cabon A, Eckes-Shephard A … Silvestro R
GrowthQuantitative Plant Biology · 2026
matched: cambium, xylem, phloem, woody, wood formation, cambial
abstract

Abstract The formation of xylem and phloem by the vascular cambium is a cellular process that is influenced by biotic and abiotic factors at sub-seasonal timescales. However, the significance of cambial activity extends across space (up to ecosystems) and time (up to centuries). This relates to the functional importance of xylem and phloem and the extremely long persistence of woody tissues, which are also a major carbon sink. Hence, studying this phenomenon involves a wide variety of approaches and methods that fundamentally vary in their temporal and spatial scales. From global vegetation dynamics to maple syrup production, the articles in the special collection ‘Advances in Xylem and Phloem Formation Research’ well reflect this diversity. Collectively, they reveal the complexity of underlying mechanisms and the potential for implementation of simpler schemes into global models.

11.5 Stemflow as a Storm-Driven Pathway for Fungal eDNA Transport
Valdman, B. W., Jusko … J. T.
preprintPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthMethodsbioRxiv · 2026-10-09
matched: endophyt*, stem, bark, poplar*, beech, Fagus, tree, trees, woody, microscop*
abstract

Stemflow transports water and biological material from forest canopies to the stem base, yet fungal studies of this flux have largely relied on microscopy of conidia. We used ITS2 metabarcoding to characterize fungal environmental DNA (eDNA) in stemflow from 15 trees – five each of American beech (Fagus grandifolia), sugar maple (Acer saccharum), and tulip poplar (Liriodendron tulipifera) in a closed-canopy forest in northeastern Ohio, USA. Stemflow was sampled during eight rain events in the leafless season (November 2024–May 2025), along with simulated bark and gutter washes to assess near-collar and collector signals. Natural stemflow communities were compositionally distinct from both controls and occupied greater ordination space. Ascomycota dominated across all samples, with Parafenestella, Cytospora, Microcera, Nectria, and Aureobasidium among the most abundant resolved genera and 13 of the 25 most abundant taxa differed significantly among host species. Woody-plant-associated endophytes, canker fungi, and saprobes dominated ecological guild composition. Storm event explained 28.6% of taxonomic variation, host tree species 24.1%, and their interaction 10.0%. Guild composition was more strongly structured by host species (35.4%) than storm event (20.4%). Lichenized fungi and lichen-associated fungi were detected. Thus, leafless-season stemflow integrates broad bark-associated fungal eDNA, including recurrent lichen-derived material, beyond the morphologically distinctive conidia captured by traditional methods. Stemflow eDNA therefore offers a minimally invasive means of monitoring canopy fungal diversity and the strong host and temporal filtering of DNA exported from woody surfaces.

Abstract Cadmium (Cd) contamination threatens soil ecosystems and food security. 11.0 Invasive Chinese tallow is associated with distinct rhizosphere microbial communities, core microbiomes, and functional potential relative to native Loblolly pine
Khan, M., Pant … A. H.
preprintPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-16
matched: pathogen*, rhizobi*, microbiome*, nutrient*, growth, pine, Pinus, lignin*, ectomycorrhiz*
abstract

Plant invasions frequently reshape belowground microbial communities, yet their effects on rhizosphere microbiome structure and function remain poorly understood. We compared the rhizosphere bacterial and fungal microbiomes of invasive Chinese tallow (Triadica sebifera) and native Loblolly pine (Pinus taeda) using 16S rRNA gene and ITS amplicon sequencing, together with microbial, functional, and soil analyses. Chinese tallow exhibited significantly greater bacterial and fungal richness and distinct microbial community composition than Loblolly pine. The invasive rhizosphere was enriched in plant-beneficial bacterial taxa, including Streptomyces, Bradyrhizobium, and Rhizobium, and ectomycorrhizal-associated fungi such as Russula and Cenococcum. This enrichment is consistent with enhanced nutrient acquisition, plant growth promotion, and stress tolerance, potentially favoring Chinese tallow establishment and persistence. Further, core microbiome and co-occurrence network analyses revealed extensive reorganization of persistent (Streptomyces, Russula) and highly connected microbial taxa that may be associated with Chinese tallow invasion. Functional predictions indicated greater representation of carbohydrate degradation, nitrogen fixation, plant growth promotion, nutrient mineralization, and ectomycorrhizal guilds in the Chinese tallow rhizosphere, whereas lignin degradation and saprotrophic or pathogen-associated fungal guilds were relatively more abundant under Loblolly pine. Soil physicochemical properties were also strongly correlated with dominant bacterial and fungal taxa, indicating that host-mediated microbial recruitment and environmental filtering jointly shaped rhizosphere community assembly. Overall, our findings identify rhizosphere microbiome reassembly as an important belowground feature associated with Chinese tallow invasion.

11.0 Regional species turnover and local host and forest stand characteristics jointly structure early foliar mycobiomes of Scots pine seedlings.
Wahlman W, Velmala S, Sutela S, Terhonen E.
GrowthFEMS microbiology ecology · 2026-10-05
matched: seed*, birch, pine, Pinus, spruce, conifer*, tree
abstract

The foliar mycobiome plays an important role in tree health and stress resilience, yet factors shaping foliar fungal communities in boreal conifer seedlings remain poorly understood. We investigated how geographic region, seedling origin (planted vs. naturally regenerated), needle nitrogen status, and local stand structure influence needle-associated fungal communities of Scots pine (Pinus sylvestris) seedlings. Needle samples were collected from seedlings without visible disease symptoms across 23 forest stands in southern Finland. Fungal communities were characterized using Illumina ITS2 amplicon sequencing and clustered into operational taxonomical units (OTUs). Alpha diversity was analyzed via linear mixed models (LMMs), and community composition via PERMANOVA and NMDS using Bray-Curtis dissimilarity. Geographic region was the strongest driver of fungal community composition among stands, whereas within stands, community composition was primarily associated with seedling height and birch abundance. Alpha diversity was higher in the Southeastern region compared to the Southwest, and tended to be lower in planted than naturally regenerated seedlings. Moreover, diversity decreased with higher proportions of birch and spruce but increased with higher needle C: N ratios. These findings suggest that early foliar mycobiomes reflect regional conditions and local forest structure, highlighting potential forest management impacts on fungal communities.

11.0 The role of environmental gradients in the ontogenetic shift of wood traits from seedlings to adults in two temperate tree species
Hickey H, Prendin A, Messier J.
preprintGrowthEcoEvoRxiv · 2026-10-13
matched: seed*, development, birch, Betula, tree, trees, hydraulic*, embolism
abstract

Tree development, or ontogeny, involves concurrent changes in plant size and environmental conditions, both of which can influence wood structure and function. Disentangling the factors underlying ontogenetic variation in wood traits remains a major challenge. This is important because wood underpins hydraulic efficiency, hydraulic safety, and mechanical support - functions critical for tree performance. In this study, we sampled twigs from a fixed distance from the apex from sugar maple (Acer saccharum) and yellow birch (Betula alleghaniensis) to quantify how wood traits, light availability, and water availability change across seedling, sapling, and adult developmental stages. Across both species, wood structure shifted toward greater hydraulic safety with ontogeny, demonstrated through decreased vessel diameter (Dh), increased vessel number (VN) and increased vessel reinforcement ((t/b)2) in adult trees. Despite this shift, hydraulic efficiency (Ks) was maintained across developmental stages, indicating that increases in VN and lumen fraction (F) compensated for reductions in efficiency associated with smaller Dh. Wood trait covariation was structured by a hydraulic efficiency–safety trade-off, however traits such as F varied more independently, enabling compensations such that this trade-off did not constrain tissue-level hydraulic function. Contrary to expectations, we did not detect differences in water availability across developmental stages. Although light availability increased from seedlings to adults, it explained little variation in wood traits, while height was associated with only a subset of traits in sugar maple and none in yellow birch. The shift toward smaller Dh and maintained Ks in adult apices contrasts with expectations of increasing transport efficiency in taller trees but is consistent with selection for resistance to freeze–thaw embolism in temperate environments.

10.5 A Robust Machine-Learning Classification Framework for Early Detection of Charcoal and Bleeding Canker in Cork Oak Bark
Yang J, Salotra G, Pinsky E.
preprintPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthMethodsPreprints.org · 2026-10-10
matched: pathogen*, Phytophthora, bark, oak, Quercus, machine learning
abstract

The cork oak (Quercus suber) is an ecologically and economically significant species, supporting forest biodiversity, carbon sequestration, and the global cork industry. Climate-driven pathogens, specifically Biscogniauxia mediterranea and Phytophthora cinnamomi, increasingly threaten cork oak forests with charcoal and bleeding cankers, respectively, leading to rapid forest decline. Early, reliable identification of these pathogens in the field is critical for effective management, but distinguishing visually similar diseases remains challenging for non-specialist personnel. This study investigates whether a small set of representative bark images can provide an interpretable, field-oriented approach to cork oak disease classification. Using a dataset of 1,198 bark images including healthy, B. mediterranea-infected, and P. cinnamomi-infected samples, we extracted features with MobileNet-v3 Small and applied K-Means clustering to identify pseudo-centroids, or real, representative images closest to each cluster’s center. These pseudo-centroids serve as interpretable reference points that field workers can visually compare with new samples, rather than relying solely on "black box" model outputs. A k-Nearest Neighbor (KNN) classifier evaluated against these pseudo-centroids achieved 74.90% accuracy across the three classes. To assess resilience to human labeling errors, a common source of error in field-collected datasets, we simulated noise by randomly swapping pseudo-centroid labels, which resulted in only a 5.44% decline in accuracy, demonstrating that the framework degrades gracefully rather than catastrophically under noisy conditions. This robustness, combined with the interpretability of the pseudo-centroid approach, makes the framework practical for forest rangers and conservation workers to prune or treat infected limbs based on direct visual similarity ranking, without requiring deep machine learning or biological expertise. Given the environmental and economic importance of cork production, this approach offers a scalable, field-ready tool for monitoring the health of cork oak forests. Future work will expand the framework to larger, more diverse datasets and additional pathogens, broadening its applicability across cork-producing regions.

10.5 Drought induces oak growth synchrony at stand level but amplifies individual variability across the Romanian Carpathians.
Nechita C, Iordache AM, Camarero JJ.
Abiotic stressGrowthJournal of environmental management · 2026-10-14
matched: drought, growth, oak, Quercus, tree, trees
abstract

Heightened drought stress affects growth synchrony among trees in broadleaf oak forests. These effects differ by region and species but remain poorly understood and are not well connected to drought stress. This study compared synchrony that captures high-frequency variation among coexisting trees with drought-induced tree growth synchrony while controlling for age, diameter, and species. The analysis was conducted on an extensive database comprising annually resolved tree rings from 5815 individuals across 132 sites, representing two principal European oak species, Quercus robur and Quercus petraea, which were sampled across the Eastern, Southern, and Western Romanian Carpathians. Different climatic characteristics across regions are reflected by varying breakpoints in temperature trends, buffering intervals, and steeper heating gradients. The Western region experienced the highest number of months characterized by extreme drought conditions per site during the 1960s and 2000s. Throughout these decades, the growth synchrony among coexisting trees, particularly Q. petraea, rose in the West and remained comparatively low in the East. Q. robur highlighted the lowest synchrony amplitude and trend variability, with nearly uniform values across all regions. Climatic aridity increases stand synchrony while promoting tree-level growth asynchrony. This pattern is more evident in the West, where spring drought limited Q. petraea growth, while summer droughts negatively impacted Q. robur, with no statistical influence of tree age or diameter. In the South, both spring and summer droughts limited growth, but older Q. robur trees showed slow post-drought growth recovery. In the Eastern region, summer drought constrained oak growth, particularly in Q. robur. Growth gradually increased in trees with larger diameters, masking site synchrony, while individual trees remained decoupled. The impacts of increasing drought stress on tree growth synchrony partly explain the overall common heteroscedastic patterns observed across regions, species and populations.

10.5 Enemy escape to enemy accumulation: fungal pathogens are associated with mortality in the invasive conifer Pinus contorta
Marion Z, Cox G, Dickie IA.
preprintPlant-microbe interactions and immunityAuthorea Preprints · 2026-10-09
matched: pathogen*, pines, Pinus, conifer*, tree
abstract

Invasive species are predicted to escape their natural enemies, yet whether this release is overcome by co-invasion of specialist enemies and/or gain of diverse assemblages of generalist antagonists remains unresolved. Here we describe a natural experiment where invasive Pinus contorta conifers experienced a die-off in the Mackenzie Basin on the South Island of New Zealand. Using a combination of visual surveys, culturing, and next-generation sequencing, we show that fungal pathogens likely contributed to this mortality event this mortality event. The fungal communities of living pines were significantly more diverse than dead pines, characterized by many low abundance fungal OTUs alongside a few dominant species. Indicator OTUs of living tree communities were most closely related to a diverse suite of putative plant pathogens, some of which are known to infect pines; dead pines were associated with saprotrophic white and brown rot fungi. Critically, no single pathogen explained the mortality; living vs. dead status accounted for less than 3% of fungal community variation. We argue that these invasive conifers have accumulated a disparate assemblage of both co-invasive and novel latent pathogens that act opportunistically in concert with harsh abiotic conditions, suggesting enemy accumulation may predispose invasive species to population-level mortality.

10.0 A North American mycorrhizal fungus displays zinc tolerance intraspecific variation and protects pines from zinc toxicity.
Fletcher J, Wang H, Zhang K … Branco S.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthCurrent biology : CB · 2026-10-14 · also: Current Biology
matched: mycorrhiz*, symbio*, seed*, pine, pines, Pinus, ectomycorrhiz*
abstract

Soil zinc (Zn) pollution, largely caused by anthropogenic activities such as mining and metal processing, can disrupt metabolism and reproduction, often leading to death. Some fungi can tolerate elevated Zn concentrations, but the mechanisms of Zn tolerance and if and how they affect symbionts are still unclear. To fill these gaps in knowledge, we investigated Zn tolerance in the ectomycorrhizal fungus Suillus tomentosus and its host pine Pinus contorta, two widespread symbiotic partners in American Western forests. Using a combination of whole-genome resequencing, in vitro Zn fungal assays, fungal-pine partner bioassays, transcriptomics, and metabolomics, we found an absence of S. tomentosus population structure based on Zn tolerance, and that the trait is highly polygenic, constitutive, and associated with Zn exclusion and antioxidant responses to mitigate toxicity. We also found that S. tomentosus protects P. contorta from Zn toxicity by increasing seedling performance under Zn stress, with Zn-tolerant S. tomentosus providing slightly more benefit compared with Zn-sensitive S. tomentosus. Our results illustrate that fungal metal tolerance is a complex trait, and that intraspecific variation should be considered for understanding the impacts of environmental stress on biodiversity, as well as for designing effective biotechnological tools and conservation/reforestation strategies.

10.0 A multi-tissue transcriptome strategy for genomic resource development in Pinus edulis
Macias, D. S., Gyllenhaal … H. E.
preprintPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-11
matched: defense, root, roots, phloem, seed*, germination, development, pine, Pinus, conifer*
abstract

ABSTRACT Premise Conifers comprise one-sixth of global forests and landscape genomic studies are urgently needed to understand their biology, yet relatively few conifer species have reference genomes. Targeted exon-capture is a practical alternative to whole-genome sequencing, but selecting loci without a reference genome remains a barrier. We developed a multi-tissue reference transcriptome for piñon pine ( Pinus edulis ) to ask how multi-tissue sampling improves probe design, and what is lost under a conventional single-tissue approach. Methods We assembled a reference transcriptome from four diploid tissues (needle, phloem, root, vegetative bud) and one haploid tissue (megagametophyte), using the latter as a pseudo-guide. We designed a target-capture probe-set by prioritizing highly expressed P. edulis transcripts and transcripts matching a conifer exon database. Results We recovered 76% of conserved plant genes with few duplicated transcripts. Filtering yielded 15,664 transcripts totaling 31.4 Mb, ∼0.1% of the genome. Tissue-specific expression was substantial: 27.4% of transcripts occurred exclusively in non-needle tissues. Functional annotation confirmed complementary contributions, including seed-desiccation and germination genes from megagametophytes, and defense- and stress-associated genes from roots and phloem. Discussion We built a transcriptome and efficient probe set using a roadmap suitable for other non-model plants with large genomes; keys to success were prioritizing complementary tissues and using expression-based filtering.

10.0 Assessing Future Maladaptation Risk in the Keystone Species Populus tremuloides.
Zacharias M, Goessen R, Wehenkel C … Porth I.
Abiotic stressGrowthEvolutionary applications · 2026-10-13
matched: drought, growth, Populus, aspen, tree, trees, forest tree*
abstract

Understanding the adaptive potential of widespread trees is critical for predicting species resilience under climate change and informing their conservation strategies. We assessed the genomic offset in Populus tremuloides Michx. (aspen) using 10,937 genotyping-by-sequencing derived, high-quality SNPs from 891 individuals sampled across the species' range, from Mexico to both the eastern and western regions of North America. Gradient forest models were applied using multiple SNP datasets and climate scenarios, with the genomic offset standardized by aspen population genetic clusters and ecological regions to identify future habitat suitability (recipient importance) and potential sources of reproductive material (donor importance) for assisted migration. Moisture-related variables exerted consistently stronger selective pressure on genetic variation than temperature, reflecting the riparian ecology of aspen. Populations from Mexico and the southwestern U.S.A. exhibited the highest offsets, suggesting elevated vulnerability despite apparent drought adaptations, whereas northern populations showed the lowest offsets, consistent with projected northward range shifts. Traits not captured by genomic offset models, such as polyploidy, clonality, hybridization, and epigenetics, may buffer environmental stress and enhance resilience, underscoring the limitations of purely genomic predictions. To complement offset estimates, we tested linear models using phenotypic data (growth, polyploidy), but the limited representation of aspen populations in common garden trials restricted validation. Nevertheless, a positive association between polyploidy and offset emerged, suggesting a potential adaptive or buffering mechanism that warrants further investigation. Together, our results illustrate both the promise and the limitations of genomic offset as a predictive framework for widely distributed species. By presenting the first approach to scale the genomic offset to a broadly-distributed forest tree, this study provides a foundation for integrating genomic, phenotypic, and ecological data to inform climate-resilient forest management. Range-wide common garden and provenance trials remain essential, yet they remain scarce for broadleaf species such as aspen. Integrating multiple data sources is critical for developing effective climate-resilient forest management strategies.

abstract

Abstract Aims Cotinus coggygria , a pioneer tree species in the Three Gorges Reservoir Area, is increasingly constrained by progressive phosphorus (P) limitation under long-term monoculture. Mixed-species plantations may alleviate this stress by reshaping the root–soil microbiota, but the underlying microecological mechanisms remain unclear. Methods Here, we examined soil properties, extracellular enzyme stoichiometry, and microbial community assembly across bulk soil, rhizosphere, and endosphere niches in pure C. coggygria stands (C) and two mixed stands ( C. coggygria + Quercus acrodonta , CQ; C. coggygria + Pinus massoniana , CPM). Zhu P, Zimmer I, Padmanaban PBS … Schnitzler JP.

matched: pathogen*, root, roots, leaf, leaves, poplar*, Populus, trees, ectomycorrhiz*
abstract

Although trees encounter diverse soil-associated fungi, it is unclear how they adjust their physiology in response to volatile organic compounds (VOCs) from fungi with contrasting ecological strategies before physical contact. Here, we tested whether VOCs emitted by fungi are sufficient to induce systemic, fungus-dependent metabolic responses in poplar. Populus × canescens roots were exposed to VOCs from a pathogen (Heterobasidion annosum), a saprotroph (Postia placenta), or an ectomycorrhizal mutualist (Laccaria bicolor) for 6 weeks in a contact-free pot-in-pot system. 9.0 Biome-scale heterogeneity in Scots pine drought resilience across Eurasia
Castillo EMd, Torbenson M, Reinig F … Esper J.
preprintAbiotic stressGrowthResearch Square · 2026-10-12
matched: drought, growth, pine, Pinus, tree
abstract

Abstract The stability of global forest ecosystems is challenged by the accelerated pace of climate change, raising questions about their role as future carbon reservoirs. Intensifying drought events affect forest productivity and dieback at regional scales, yet how drought sensitivity and resilience components vary across the full distribution of widespread tree species remains insufficiently quantified. Here, we compile a pan-Eurasian network of 534 Pinus sylvestris tree-ring chronologies spanning the species’ full ecological range and quantify growth departures and resilience components during the most extreme summer droughts of the past century. Drought impacts diverge systematically along major climatic gradients. Growth reductions exceed 70% in cold-dry and warm semi-humid regions, while cool–humid forests show muted and even positive departures. Resistance is lowest under prolonged droughts in continental cold–dry interiors and under short summer droughts in semi-humid lowlands, while recovery capacity is highest where growth losses are most severe, reflecting local adaptations in drier regions. Despite coherent biome-scale patterns, substantial site-level variability indicates that growth legacies often persist beyond the event year. Nonlinear models show that resilience-climate relationships are shaped not only by mean climate conditions, but also by atmospheric water demand, site-level water balance, and thermal stability across climatic domains. Together, these results provide a range-wide assessment of drought impacts, demonstrating how prevailing climate conditions modulates forest stability and carbon sequestration capacity under intensifying climate extremes.

matched: pathogen*, defense, development, differentiation, conifer*, cambial, single-cell
abstract

Resin canals are tunnel-like tissue structures primarily responsible for synthesizing and secreting resin. They consist of specialized secondary metabolite-synthesizing epithelial cells and supportive subsidiary cells, which are arranged to form distinctive intercellular spaces. In conifers, resin canals can develop during normal development or be induced by environmental stimuli, which often form an interconnected network and play a crucial role in defense against pathogens and insects. Despite their importance in resin secretion and defense, the mechanisms underlying their formation and function remain poorly understood. In this review, we summarize recent advances in the study of resin canals in conifers from the perspectives of cellular development, defense mechanisms, and molecular regulation. We analyze the distribution patterns, subcellular characteristics and formation and developmental processes of resin canals across different conifer species. Furthermore, we focus on resin canal-specific genes and analyze the functions and expression patterns of identified related genes. With the advancement of conifer genome sequencing and single-cell technologies, future research on resin canal regulatory mechanisms and functions is facing unprecedented opportunities. We propose a strategic framework aimed at enhancing conifer breeding, particularly for traits such as stress resilience, disease resistance and wood quality.

Folorunso T, Silva G, Olatinwo R … Willoughby J.

abstract

Sydowia polyspora is a widespread conifer-associated fungus that can function as an endophyte, saprophyte, or opportunistic pathogen, but its occurrence in southeastern pine systems remains poorly characterized. During surveys of symptomatic loblolly pine (Pinus taeda) in Washington and Cullman counties, Alabama, fungal isolates morphologically consistent with Sydowia spp. were recovered from needles exhibiting foliar disease symptoms. abstract

IntroductionRhizome-driven spatial expansion in bamboo and other clonal plants is governed jointly by architectural patterning, belowground bud bank demography, hormonal crosstalk, carbon allocation, environmental plasticity and genomic regulation. This integrative review synthesises those strands and then asks a question the field has not yet answered explicitly: how much of the mechanistic framework now invoked for programmable rhizome control has actually been demonstrated in a woody bamboo?MethodsLiterature was retrieved from Web of Science, Scopus, PubMed and CNKI and appraised along two axes - the biological provenance of each observation (woody bamboo; other rhizomatous or stoloniferous perennial; annual, herbaceous or model system) and its causal strength (perturbation-based, correlative, or conceptual).ResultsThe appraisal shows that architectural description, running-versus-clumping contrasts and clonal integration are supported by causal or quantitative evidence in bamboo itself, whereas the auxin -cytokinin -strigolactone module, BRANCHED1/TB1 repression, KNOX-mediated architectural control and the Tre6P -SnRK1 -TOR growth switch are supported in bamboo correlatively or not at all, having been imported from Arabidopsis, rice, potato, poplar and Nicotiana.DiscussionOn that basis we propose a three-tier genome-editing framework ordered by intervention depth, and argue that in bamboo the binding constraint is delivery rather than target choice: because gregarious monocarpic flowering precludes segregation-based transgene removal, ribonucleoprotein editing of cis-regulatory elements in a transformable Dendrocalamus or Bambusa background is presently the only route to a transgene-free edited plant, and vegetative containment necessarily dominates reproductive containment. We identify five quantities that the current corpus does not report, including lateral extension rate with variance, barrier breach probability as a function of embedment and inspection interval, and the minimum regenerative rhizome fragment size, and convert them into a staged, falsifiable research agenda with explicit go/no-go criteria.

8.5 Gene expression dynamics through time reveal low pleiotropy genes underlying local adaptation in silver birch (Betula pendula)
Siren, J., Vuosku … T.
preprintGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-16
matched: flowering, growth, differentiation, birch, Betula, trees
abstract

Understanding the molecular mechanisms underlying local adaptation in boreal trees is important for assessing their adaptive potential under climate change. The role of pleiotropy in polygenic local adaptation remains poorly understood. Here we studied growth and temporal transcriptomic responses to photoperiod in three photoperiod treatments in three populations of Betula pendula within a two-week time period to identify genes and co-expression networks underlying local adaptation and to estimate the level of pleiotropy in population specific gene expression. We used RNA sequencing to characterize the temporal transcriptomic responses which were analyzed with variance partitioning, differential gene expression and weighted co-expression network analyses. Photoperiod treatment and sampling time were the main drivers of gene expression variation both at the co-expression module and single gene level. Our findings suggest that local adaptation to growing season length among populations exhibiting very low genetic differentiation along a latitudinal cline in B. pendula is likely polygenic and potentially governed through low pleiotropy genes.

8.5 Modeled Leaf Assimilation Increases Under Rising CO₂ in Anatolian Forest Trees Despite Warming and Drying, While Thermal Threshold Exceedance Increases
Yazar E, Akgün B, Babur E.
preprintGrowthPreprints.org · 2026-10-12
matched: leaf, stoma*, tree, trees, forest tree*, hydraulic*
abstract

Rising atmospheric CO₂ suppresses photorespiration, whereas warming and drying can limits leaf carbon gain. We coupled a Farquhar-von Caemmerer-Berry photosynthesis model with an ATP/NADPH budget, soil-plant hydraulics, and leaf energy balance, assigning photosystem II thermal thresholds based on leaf habit. Using ISIMIP3b forcing from five CMIP6 models, we simulated eight tree species across 27 species-site combinations at six locations in Türkiye, comparing the periods 2020–2030 and 2070–2080 under the SSP5-8.5 scenario. With soil moisture reset each season, the mean seasonal net assimilation increased by 130.3 gC m⁻² of leaf area (95% CI: 76–187 gC m⁻²). The forcing-channel contributions were +219.5 gC m⁻² from CO₂, −66.7 gC m⁻² from temperature/VPD, −38.8 gC m⁻² from precipitation, and −2.8 gC m⁻² from radiation (residual: +19.1). Species and sites accounted for 81% of the fitted variance, compared to 3.1% for GCMs. Under year-round soil water tracking, the mean increase was 106.5 gC m⁻² (95% CI: +43 to +167), with declines in 11 of 135 cells. Days with peak leaf temperature exceeding assigned thermal thresholds increased from 1.5 to 12.2 per season, primarily in broad-leaved species. These counts were sensitive to thermal parameters and stomatal assumptions and do not directly quantify tissue damage. Within the tested configurations, higher modeled leaf assimilation coexisted with more frequent threshold exceedance. Neither outcome alone establishes stand productivity or forest resilience.

abstract

Under the background of global climate change, frequent and severe drought have led to forest decline and tree mortality, ultimately threatening the structure, function, and stability of forest ecosystems. However, forest ecosystems and individual trees exhibit a certain degree of growth resilience to the impacts of drought. A profound understanding of the growth resilience of forest ecosystems and individual trees to drought is of great significance for maintaining the stability of forest ecosystems, particularly for plantation ecosystems. Based on an improved resilience assessment framework (maximum averaged growth reduction and recovery rate with the flexible time window), this study compared the radial growth resilience of major tree and shrub species between planted forests across different sub-climatic zones of the west Loess Plateau and corresponding natural forest species in adjacent area. In contrast, Picea crassifolia and Pinus tabulaeformis showed lower resilience and should be confined to high altitude, semi-arid zones with higher precipitation. 3) In terms of plantation management in areas with insufficient precipitation, appropriate silvicultural interventions, such as rainwater harvesting, soil moisture conservation, and stand tending, must be supplemented to maintain the healthy and sustainable development of planted forests. These findings provide a scientific basis for species selection and adaptive plantation management on the Loess Plateau.

8.5 Studying the role of climate and sex in flowering dynamics of black poplar across time and space
Cazenave, X., Marc … H.
preprintGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-06
matched: flowering, poplar*, Populus, tree, trees, phenolog*
abstract

Global change has a major impact on rivers and their associated terrestrial compartment, the riparian forest. The black poplar ( Populus nigra L.), the dominant species of the European riparian forest, is considered an indicator of the biodiversity dynamics of these ecosystems, but is threatened with extinction in several parts of its range. The species is dioecious and shows wide variability in many traits, enabling it to adapt to a broad range of environmental conditions. A detailed characterization of this natural variability is therefore a key issue both for the conservation of natural populations and for biological engineering aimed at restoring riverbanks. Phenology is both an indicator of climate change and an adaptation lever for populations, particularly in perennial species such as trees. In this study, we characterized the spatial and temporal variability in flowering phenology by studying 13 natural black poplar populations along the Loire River, France. Flowering date and duration showed substantial variability both within and between populations, and males generally flowered earlier than females. Most male-female pairs shared several days of overlapping flowering each year, and this overlap was moderately stable across years. Flowering dates advanced by about five to six days per degree Celsius during early spring, with no detectable difference in sensitivity between sexes. This work contributes to the characterization of flowering variability in an economically important tree species and may inform both the conservation of genetic resources and breeding. Highlights Flowering phenology varied substantially within and among black poplar populations. Male black poplars generally flowered earlier than females. Flowering advanced by 5–6 days per °C increase in early spring temperature. Phenological diversity informs black poplar conservation and breeding.

8.0 Extremely low light promotes adventitious shoot regeneration via RpPHYA/RpHYH-antagonized auxin biosynthesis and transport through dual regulation of RpPIN1 in Robinia pseudoacacia.
Han J, Tian Y, Liu J … Zhao Y.
Kinases and signal transductionHormonesGrowthHorticulture research · 2026-05-25
matched: signaling, auxin*, PIN\d+, shoot*, Robinia, woody
abstract

Extremely low light (ELL) is widely applied as a strategy to enhance adventitious shoot (AS) regeneration in woody plants. However, how ELL acts as an environmental signal to reprogram regenerative competence remains unclear. Here, we show that ELL for 14 days (ELL14) markedly promotes AS regeneration across multiple genotypes of Robinia pseudoacacia. Transcriptomic and functional enrichment analyses revealed that auxin biosynthesis, transport, and signaling constitute the core pathways activated under ELL14. Consistently, auxin levels increased, and the inhibition of biosynthesis or transport largely abolished the regeneration-promoting effect. Integrative weighted gene co-expression network analysis and regulatory analyses identified RpPHYA as a key integrator of ELL-responsive networks. Under ELL14, the RpPHYA-associated module promotes auxin biosynthesis by upregulating RpAMI1 and coordinates auxin transport through RpPIN1 via transcriptional regulation and RpROS1-mediated CHG demethylation, forming a dual regulatory mechanism. In contrast, under normal light, RpHYH antagonizes this regulatory program, thereby restricting regenerative capacity. Overexpression of RpPHYA, RpAMI1, RpPIN1, and RpROS1 significantly enhanced AS regeneration, functionally validating this ELL14-induced module. Collectively, our findings demonstrate that ELL functions as an environmental signal that promotes AS regeneration in R. pseudoacacia through coordinated transcriptional and DNA demethylation of auxin transport, providing mechanistic insights for improving regeneration in woody plants.

8.0 Flooding with brackish water disrupts Liriodendron tulipifera sap flux in an ecosystem-scale experiment.
Srinivasan RV, McDowell N, Pennington SC … Morris KA.
Abiotic stressOecologia · 2026-10-07
matched: drought, flooding, Fagus, tree, woody, hydraulic*
abstract

Coastal forests are retreating due to sea‑level rise and extreme precipitation events, yet the physiological mechanisms that precede woody plant decline remain poorly constrained. We used the ecosystem‑scale TEMPEST (Terrestrial Ecosystem Manipulation to Probe the Effects of Storm Treatments) experiment; annual, plot‑level freshwater and saltwater flooding of a mid‑Atlantic deciduous forest, to quantify short‑ and long‑term responses of sap flux in three tree species: Acer rubrum, Fagus grandifolia, and Liriodendron tulipifera. We analyzed five years of sap flux density measurements (2021-2025) using a Before-After Control-Impact (BACI) framework and linear mixed‑effects models to assess short and long-term changes in tree hydraulic function. Across the study period, species differed markedly in their responses to repeated flooding. A. rubrum showed no significant change in sap flux density under any treatment, while F. grandifolia exhibited plot‑specific increases or decreases consistent with relief from drought in the freshwater treatment plot. L. tulipifera sap flux density increased over time in all plots, but increases were dampened in the freshwater plot and strongly suppressed under saltwater flooding. Soil electrical conductivity rose progressively in the saltwater plot and emerged as a key predictor of both immediate and cumulative reductions in L. tulipifera sap flux density. As L. tulipifera constitutes a large share of canopy basal area in this coastal forest, continued saltwater intrusion is poised to accelerate mortality and drive ecosystem state-change. Our findings illustrate how ecosystem‑scale manipulations can uncover physiological thresholds relevant to coastal forest vulnerability under future sea‑level rise.

8.0 Rapid evolutionary responses to climate change in maritime pine (Pinus pinaster Ait.) despite widespread adaptive lag
Francisco, T., Lesur-Kupin … S. C.
preprintbioRxiv · 2026-10-16
matched: pine, Pinus, tree, trees
abstract

Climate change is profoundly affecting species worldwide and poses a major threat to their long-term persistence. Although trees may have the potential for rapid adaptation to changing climates, empirical evidence remains limited, and whether these long-lived organisms can adapt quickly enough to keep pace with ongoing climate change is still an open question. To investigate real-time responses to contemporary climate change, we combined genomic data from 82 range-wide populations of the widespread tree Pinus pinaster Ait. with intensive sampling of two successive cohorts from two natural populations. Using an original genotype-environment association (GEA) approach to quantify climate (mal)adaptation, we found that most individuals from both target populations showed evidence of an adaptive lag to current climatic conditions, and are predicted to face substantial risk of maladaptation under future climates. Importantly, changes in genomic composition at climate-associated loci between cohorts were consistent with rapid evolutionary responses to ongoing climate change and could not be explained by neutral demographic processes alone, as supported by forward-time genetic simulations. We also observed a lower realised genetic load in the younger cohort, suggesting that the dynamics of deleterious mutations may change with climatic pressure. Our work provides evidence suggesting genetic responses to contemporary climate change within a single generation, including signals of rapid evolutionary changes in genomic composition at climate-associated loci and signatures of purifying selection, despite widespread adaptive lag to current climates. These findings challenge the view that trees only respond slowly to environmental change and highlight the need to integrate evolutionary dynamics into predictive models of species persistence under future climates.

8.0 Significant effects of host hydraulic traits and host nitrogen-fixing ability on functional traits in the mistletoe Taxillus chinensis
Ma R, Ma X, Wang X … Zhang Q.
preprintAbiotic stressGrowthAuthorea Preprints · 2026-10-07
matched: drought, nutrient*, leaves, stem, xylem, development, hydraulic*
abstract

Mistletoes are hemiparasitic plants that rely on hosts for water and nutrients. How host functional traits influence mistletoe’s physiology, especially the interplay between hydraulic architecture and nitrogen acquisition, remains unclear. This study examined the effects of host hydraulic traits and host nitrogen-fixing ability on the mistletoe Taxillus chinensis across seven hosts. We quantified key hydraulic traits in both hosts and parasites, including predawn water potential (ΨPD), minimum water potential (Ψmin), vessel diameter (D), vascular fraction (VF), parenchyma fraction (PF) and fiber fraction (FF). Wood density (WD) and saturated water content (SWC) were measured only in the hosts. Measurements specific to the mistletoe included chlorophyll content (SPAD), sapwood-specific hydraulic conductivity (Ks), and photosynthetic rate (A). The mistletoe maintained lower water potentials than hosts, ensuring unidirectional resource flow, and its ΨPD was only weakly correlated with that of the host, indicating some hydraulic autonomy. Correspondingly, the mistletoe had a smaller D, consistent with a developmental response to the lower water potential at the distal end of the water-transport pathway. Host xylem anatomy further shaped mistletoe water status. Hosts with higher VF provided better water supply. Consequently, the mistletoe's ΨPD was less negative. Meanwhile, a lower host WD was associated with a higher saturated water content in the host stem, which buffered drought stress and mitigated declines in the parasite's Ψmin. This improved water status consequently supported the development of larger leaves. Mistletoes on the leguminous hosts Bauhinia purpurea and Erythrina variegata had higher chlorophyll content than those on non-fixing hosts. Mistletoes on Acacia confusa had an intermediate chlorophyll content. Photosynthetic rate was significantly higher in mistletoes on the three nitrogen-fixing hosts than in those on non-fixing hosts. These results are consistent with the host-quality hypothesis and the nitrogen-parasitism hypothesis.

We present a genome assembly of the tetraploid Salix cinerea (2 n = 4 x = 76; grey willow; Streptophyta; Magnoliopsida; Malpighiales; Salicaceae). The submitted sequence comprises two assemblies designated haplotype 1 and haplotype 2, with total lengths of 386.72 megabases and 1,004.23 megabases, respectively. Haplotype 1 represents a single monoploid chromosome complement; 87.7% was scaffolded into 19 chromosomal pseudomolecules, including the Z sex chromosome. Haplotype 2 was assembled to scaffold level. The mitochondrial genome assembly has a length of 686.79 kilobases and the plastid genome assembly has a length of 155.63 kilobases.
