This assembly was generated as part of the Darwin Tree of Life project, which produces reference genomes for eukaryotic species found in Britain and Ireland.

8.0 Vessels occlusion frequency as cultivar-associated xylem trait in Olea europaea.
Barozzi F, Panna R, Angilè F … Di Sansebastiano GP.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthMethodsPlant physiology and biochemistry : PPB · 2026-10-08
matched: pathogen*, defense, leaf, xylem, woody, hydraulic*, confocal, microscop*
abstract

Xylem vessel occlusions emerge in response to abiotic and biotic stresses, contributing both to hydraulic protection and to defense against vascular pathogens. However, their constitutive occurrence and inter-cultivar variability have rarely been quantified as a phenotypic trait in woody crops. In this study, we evaluate constitutive vessel occlusion frequency as a stable anatomical trait in Olea europaea and assess its potential as a predictive varietal marker for biotic stress tolerance. Using a field collection of 97 olive cultivars grown under uniform conditions at the CREA-OFA Mirto Crosia site, we quantified the number of occluded vessels in one-to two-year-old branches by combining optical and confocal microscopy on Safranin/AstraBlue-stained transverse sections. Occlusion values were used to classify cultivars from low to high occlusion frequency, and a subset of 40 representative cultivars was selected for detailed analyses. To explore the metabolic background associated with this trait, 1H-NMR profiling was performed on leaf extracts from the same plants. Our results show that readiness to vessel occlusion formation is a constant, cultivar-specific anatomical feature, with consistent differences among genotypes despite interannual variability and environmental susceptibility. We propose that occlusion frequency integrates both xylem morphology and the plant's physiological capacity to activate occlusion processes, thereby complementing mean vessel diameter as a determinant of vascular resistance. This conceptual framework is particularly relevant in the context of Xylella fastidiosa outbreaks in Mediterranean olive groves, where constitutive xylem traits may predispose cultivars to differential levels of tolerance. By treating vessel occlusion frequency as a measurable phenotypic trait, our study provides a basis for integrating xylem anatomy into selection strategies aimed at enhancing resilience to vascular pathogens in olive.

8.0 Wood Anatomical Traits and Their Influence on Carbon Sequestration, Hydraulic Efficiency and Climate Resilience of Indigenous Tree Species in Southern Africa: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis
Nsofu BM.
preprintAbiotic stressGrowthResearch Square · 2026-10-06
matched: drought, growth, tree, trees, hydraulic*
abstract

Abstract Climate change is increasingly affecting forest ecosystems worldwide through rising temperatures, prolonged droughts, altered precipitation patterns, and increasing frequencies of extreme climatic events. These environmental changes influence tree growth, mortality, carbon sequestration, water relations, and ecosystem resilience. Understanding the biological traits that enable tree species to adapt to climatic stress has therefore become a major priority in forestry and ecological research. Among these traits, wood anatomical characteristics play a critical role in regulating hydraulic conductivity, carbon storage, mechanical support, and drought tolerance. Anatomical features such as vessel diameter, vessel density, vessel arrangement, fibre morphology, ray dimensions, and parenchyma abundance influence how trees transport water, store carbon, and respond to environmental disturbances. Southern Africa contains extensive woodland and forest ecosystems, including miombo, mopane, Kalahari, and dry evergreen forests, which support numerous indigenous tree species of ecological, economic, and conservation importance. However, information linking wood anatomical traits to carbon sequestration, hydraulic efficiency, and climate resilience remains fragmented across multiple disciplines and geographic regions. Consequently, there is limited synthesis of how anatomical adaptations influence forest responses to climate change and their implications for climate-smart forestry and sustainable forest management. This systematic review and meta-analysis synthesizes available evidence on the influence of wood anatomical traits on carbon sequestration, hydraulic efficiency, and climate resilience among indigenous tree species in Southern Africa. Following the PRISMA framework, published journal articles, forestry reports, dissertations, conference proceedings, and institutional publications were reviewed. Data were extracted on vessel characteristics, fibre properties, ray dimensions, parenchyma abundance, wood density, biomass accumulation, carbon storage, hydraulic conductivity, cavitation resistance, drought tolerance, and climate adaptation indicators. The review demonstrates that wood anatomical traits are strong predictors of ecosystem functioning and climate adaptation. Vessel architecture emerged as a primary determinant of hydraulic efficiency and drought resistance, while fibre characteristics and wood density were strongly associated with biomass accumulation and long-term carbon sequestration. Species possessing narrow vessels, high vessel density, thick fibre walls, abundant parenchyma, and dense wood generally exhibited greater hydraulic safety, drought tolerance, and carbon storage potential. The findings highlight the importance of integrating wood anatomical information into climate-smart forestry, forest landscape restoration, carbon accounting systems, species selection programmes, and sustainable forest management strategies. The review further identifies critical research gaps, including limited species-specific anatomical databases, inadequate long-term hydraulic monitoring, and insufficient integration of anatomical traits into climate adaptation models.

8.0 Wood type influences tree responses to recurring hot and dry summer tree water deficits across European forests.
Nikinmaa L, Stegehuis AI, Lindner M … Muys B.
Abiotic stressGrowthEuropean journal of forest research · 2026-10-09
matched: drought, stem, growth, conifer*, tree, trees, dendro*
abstract

Increasingly frequent summer droughts are intensifying stem dehydration across European forests, constraining tree growth and resilience. Even mild water-related stress can inhibit tree growth, although its impact is species-specific. Tree water deficit (TWD), a measure deduced from radial stem contraction during drought, reflects stem water status and can be used as a proxy for the physiological drought stress experienced by trees. Using high-resolution dendrometer measurements representing 29 tree species from 130 forest sites across Europe from 2003 to 2018, we examined how the length of TWD periods during summer is related to their recurrence within the same year, to TWD intensity, to climate factors, and to wood type (i.e., tracheid-based coniferous, ring-porous or diffuse-porous anatomy). A greater degree of incomplete rehydration, indicated by highest minimum TWD during TWD periods (70% increase of expected response for conifers and ring-porous species, 42% increase of expected response for diffuse-porous), together with warmer temperature (44% increase of expected response for all wood types) and stronger drought, prolonged the length of TWD periods. However, the recurrence of TWD periods only increased the length of the subsequent TWD period within the same year for diffuse-porous tree species (12% increase of expected response). Our results show that wood type and its associated functional traits influence tree responses to recurring stem dehydration periods. The greater sensitivity of diffuse-porous species to recurring TWD periods suggests that wood type anatomy may play an important role in determining how tree species cope with increasingly frequent summer droughts.Supplementary informationThe online version contains supplementary material available at https://doi.org/10.1007/s10342-026-01956-9.

7.5 Coarse Woody Debris of Mixed Tree Species Overrides Soil Matrix Constraints on Microbial Richness and Abundance in an Ancient Broad-Leaved Forest
Khanina, L. G., Bobrovsky … P. V.
preprintCa2+Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-12
matched: pH, microbiome*, growth, Quercus, tree, woody
abstract

Annotation The mechanisms by which coarse woody debris (CWD) affects soil microbial diversity across different spatial scales and forest disturbance regimes remain poorly understood. This study investigated the impact of CWD from multiple tree species on forest floor bacterial and fungal communities under varying landscape-scale conditions, specifically comparing old-growth forests situated on Phaozems and Retisols with mass-windthrow areas located on loamy Retisols and sandy Podzols. We analyzed both chemical compound and metagenomic profiles of 63 CWD-associated soil samples collected from the Kaluzhskie Zaseki Nature Reserve (European part of Russia). High-throughput Illumina sequencing of the 16S rRNA gene and the ITS region was used to profile both bacterial and fungal communities, respectively. Scale-dependent diversity patterns and taxonomic accumulation were assessed using GLM, NMDS, dbRDA, and iNEXT frameworks. Our findings revealed a profound spatial decoupling in forest floor microbial communities, where a localized reduction in alpha-diversity under CWD drove a sharp increase in landscape-scale gamma-diversity. While standard soil physicochemical properties rigidly dictated baseline matrix richness, CWD operated as a powerful micro-environmental hotspot overriding these constraints. Deadwood inputs filtered out dominant matrix-associated taxa, creating highly specialized micro-refugia characterized by high compositional dispersion. Ultimately, iNEXT accumulation profiles confirmed that deadwood does not merely multiply common soil taxa, but enriches the forest landscape by injecting critical beta-level heterogeneity into the soil matrix. These insights emphasize the role of diverse CWD retention in buffering forest microbiomes against large-scale environmental disturbances across heterogeneous soil landscapes. Highlights Sandy soils suppress bacterial abundance but trigger massive fungal proliferation. Bacterial richness is determined by soil pH; fungal diversity responds to nitrogen. Soil pH and nitrogen shift fungal composition along decoupled orthogonal axes. Coarse woody debris acts as hotspots of micro-environmental diversity. Post-windthrow microbial hotspots are strongly shaped by tree species.

7.5 Distinct NIR Reflectance Spectra Associated with Foliar Symptoms of Beech Leaf Disease.
Moore E, Dabydeen LC, Salha R … Borsuk AM.
GrowthPlant physiology · 2026-10-08
matched: leaf, leaves, development, beech, Fagus, trees
abstract

Beech leaf disease (BLD) is an epidemic spreading among American beech (Fagus grandifolia) populations, with characteristic symptoms including dark green, yellow, or brown bands of thickened leaf tissue between secondary veins. Because near infrared (NIR) light is reflected by leaf tissue structure, we used multispectral NIR imaging reflectance to distinguish symptomatic from asymptomatic leaves amongst affected trees, as well as to distinguish between different expressions of symptoms (dark green, yellow, and brown bands) within symptomatic leaves and symptom severity (low, moderate high) at the whole leaf scale. Key findings include significantly higher NIR reflectance in dark green bands of symptomatic tissue compared to asymptomatic tissue amongst affected trees (λ = 840, 860, 900, 940, and 980 nm; ∼26.2%, 25.9%, 35.0%, 35.3%, and 32.5% increase respectively). A partial least squares regression model predicted 97% of variation in symptomatic vs. asymptomatic NIR reflectance was attributed primarily to the increase in spongy mesophyll thickness and overall leaf thickness in symptomatic tissue. Whole leaf symptom severity (asymptomatic, moderate, severe) was most clearly diagnosed at λ = 860 nm. Water infiltration of the intercellular airspaces in symptomatic dark green bands and asymptomatic leaves removed differences in NIR reflectance, supporting a mechanistic link between increased NIR reflectance and the foliar symptoms of BLD. Our results highlight the interaction of leaf internal architecture with NIR wavelengths and inform the development of targeted remote sensing tools.

7.5 Provenance and environment jointly shape early regeneration in fir and beech: evidence from distributed participatory trials across Europe
Csilléry K, Charlet de Sauvage J, Caduff M … Wegmann D
GrowthNew Phytologist · 2026-10-16
matched: seed*, germination, development, differentiation, beech, Fagus, phenolog*
abstract

Summary Forest regeneration is shaped by strong demographic filters during germination and early establishment, yet the relative contributions of seed provenance and environment remain poorly understood under natural conditions. We combined climate‐chamber trials with a continental‐scale, field‐based citizen science seed‐sowing experiment to quantify germination, early phenological development, and 3‐yr postgermination survival of fir ( Abies spp.) and beech ( Fagus spp.) provenances across Europe. We estimated germination metrics, reconstructed phenological trajectories, and quantified successive demographic filters from seed to established seedling. Germination was structured by provenance‐by‐environment interactions in both genera, but with contrasting expression. Fir showed broadly parallel environmental responses among provenances and partial concordance between climate‐chamber and field results, whereas beech exhibited strong provenance‐by‐environment responses and weak predictability from controlled conditions. Seed weight was a key axis of variation in beech (lighter seeds germinated more frequently and developed faster), but its effects in fir were confounded with geographic differentiation. Post‐establishment survival was dominated by site‐level environmental filtering and site‐by‐provenance interactions, with limited consistent provenance effects. This reveals a life‐stage shift from fine‐scale provenance‐by‐environment effects during germination and early development to broader environmental control of survival – an important consideration for regeneration‐based management and assisted migration.

7.5 Tree rings record fossil-fuel CO2 emission reduction and reveal structural responses in street trees during the COVID-19 lockdown.
Miyahara AAL, Filho MT, Olmedo GM … Locosselli GM.
GrowthEnvironmental pollution (Barking, Essex : 1987) · 2026-10-14
matched: leaf, stoma*, tree, trees, xylogenesis
abstract

Air pollution ranks among the top causes of premature death worldwide. In cities, air pollution mitigation depends on curbing emissions and fostering ecosystem services, particularly through tree planting. Whereas the role of urban trees in air pollution mitigation is well established, trees' responses to reduced air pollution stress remain poorly quantified. Air pollution can limit trees' benefits by constraining stomatal conductance, photosynthesis, and xylogenesis. This study assessed the impacts of acute air pollution reduction during the COVID-19 lockdowns on Tipuana tipu trees growing in a park and on a main avenue in São Paulo, Brazil. To test whether air pollution-driven physiological and structural limitations were reduced during the lockdowns, we assessed high-resolution tree-ring stable carbon isotope composition (δ13C) and wood density (WD) across 15 evenly-spaced intra-annual segments from 2018 to 2021. Avenue trees exhibited lower δ13C values than park trees, indicating greater exposure to fossil-fuel CO2 emissions, and the higher WD was consistent with water stress. The most restrictive lockdown (based on pollution and CO2 concentration data) at the end of the 2020/2021 growing season coincided with a shift in the typical decreasing intra-annual δ13C trend in the avenue trees to an increasing trend at the end of the tree ring, a pattern not observed in the park trees. This increasing shift was not readily explained by improved weather conditions or leaf-physiology responses. Instead, the δ13C fractionation model simulations suggest a strong source signal, reflecting an increase in the atmospheric δ13C signature during the lockdown, more so than the reduction in CO2 concentration. Although the intra-annual WD trends remained consistent, increasing from earlywood to latewood, avenue trees displayed a steeper increase in WD across the 2020/2021 tree ring, possibly associated with increased stomatal conductance. These results suggest that avenue trees recorded changes in fossil-fuel emissions and showed structural responses.

Cooke collected in Ijevan, Aparan and Yerevan floristic regions of Armenia from Acer platanoides, Carpinus betulus, Fagus orientalis, Populus nigra and Salix alba host trees. In total, 25 (14 saturated and 11 unsaturated) from 30 FA were detected. Heneicosanoic, arachidic, tricosanoic, dihomo-γ-linolenicand and eicosapentaenoic FA were not found in studied samples. Four saturated (caproic, myristic, palmitic, stearic) and two unsaturated (oleic and linoleic) FA were present in all samples in more than 5% and may be considered as chemotaxonomic criterion. The palmitic, stearic, oleic and linoleic acids were detected up to 37.76, 22.53, 27.97 and 38.83%, respectively. The total contents of saturated and unsaturated FA were 51.87-70.92% and 30.02-48.51%, while the ratios of unsaturated/saturated and saturated/unsaturated FA 0.4-0.93 and 1.1-2.4, respectively. Among unsaturated FA the MUFA comprise 30.01-46.43%, while PUFA 0-2.08% which is 0-4% of the total content of unsaturated FA. The average total content of saturated and unsaturated FA was 3.93-5.02% and 2.73- 4.41%, respectively, while the range of average total content 8.28-8.53%. From 25 detected FA 19 were present in analyzed basidiocarps. The number of saturated and unsaturated FA was 4-11 and 4-8 with unsaturated/saturated and saturated/unsaturated ratio 0.35-0.8 and 1-1.83, respectively. The saturated FA prevails in F. inzengae basidiocarps. No significant correlation between the total content and occurrence of saturated and unsaturated FA of F. inzengae was found. The revealed differences are possibly related to ageing process of perennial basidiocarps, rather than host tree condition (trunk, stump or living tree) and wood type (soft or hard). No significant difference between quantitative FA profiles of Fomes fomentarius (L.) Fr. from central Europe and F. inzengae from Armenia was revealed. However, the content of oleic and linoleic acids was higher in Armenian samples, while the difference in palmitic and stearic acids content was insignificant. The results will contribute to defining optimal growth conditions to obtain biomass and understand the mechanisms of biochemical transformation of unsaturated into saturated FA during mycelium during mycelium growth and aging. Further assessment of the impact of phyto-geographical distribution and ecological condition on the FA profile in Fomes species are warranted.

7.0 Reading Climate in Wood: A Systematic Review of Dendro-Anatomical Proxies for Climate Reconstruction, Forest Health, and Sustainable Forest Management
Nsofu BM.
preprintPlant-microbe interactions and immunityCell biology and organellesGrowthResearch Square · 2026-10-06
matched: defence, cell wall*, xylem, growth, hydraulic*, dendro*
abstract

Abstract Dendrochronology is increasingly moving from measurements of ring width and density toward cellular-scale records of xylem formation. Wood anatomical elements—including tracheid dimensions, cell-wall characteristics, rays, resin canals, earlywood–latewood structure, and density profiles—can retain environmental information at annual and intra-annual scales. This systematic review synthesizes evidence on how dendro-anatomical proxies contribute to climate reconstruction, forest-health assessment, disturbance history, and sustainable forest management. Following the review protocol described in the supplied manuscript, literature published primarily between 1950 and 2026 was searched across Scopus, Web of Science, ScienceDirect, SpringerLink, Wiley Online Library, CAB Abstracts, JSTOR, and Google Scholar, supplemented by authoritative forestry sources and citation tracking. The selection process identified 3,274 records, removed 728 duplicates, screened 2,546 records, assessed 812 full texts, and retained 287 publications. The synthesis shows that ring width provides a broad annual growth signal, whereas anatomical traits can add information on water availability, temperature, hydraulic adjustment, defence investment, and disturbance timing. Quantitative wood anatomy (QWA) is particularly valuable because it links dated rings to cellular traits such as lumen area and cell-wall thickness, while densitometry and maximum latewood density provide established high-resolution temperature information in appropriate settings. Resin canals can preserve records of defence investment and disturbance but require careful attribution because multiple stressors can produce similar anatomical responses. The review identifies a central research opportunity: integrating anatomical, density, isotopic, remote-sensing, and computational evidence into standardized multi-proxy chronologies, with greater representation of tropical and African forests.

7.0 Species-specific budburst responses to diurnal temperature cycles during chilling and forcing in temperate deciduous trees
Vitasse Y, Hoch G, Pepin S, Giardina F
Tree Physiology · 2026-10-17
matched: Fagus, Quercus, tree, trees, phenolog*
abstract

Abstract Cold temperatures (‘chilling’) perceived by tree buds progressively release winter dormancy and initiate sensitivity to warmer temperatures (‘forcing’) required for budburst. Phenology of temperate trees may also respond to daylength, but it remains unclear whether chilling and forcing accumulation is linked to the diurnal cycle. Here, we tested whether chilling accumulation is influenced by light–dark cycle frequency, and whether forcing is more effective during daylight. We harvested twig cuttings from four temperate tree species (Fagus sylvatica, Carpinus betulus, Quercus robur and Acer pseudoplatanus) with contrasting chilling and forcing requirements. In Experiment 1, twigs were harvested in November and exposed to 3°C under three light–dark cycle frequencies: 12h light/12h dark, 6h/6h, and 4h/4h (1, 2 or 3 cycles/day) for one or two months (short vs. long chilling). Twigs were then transferred to two forcing regimes with the same daily mean but different day/night temperatures (25°C/10°C and 20°C/15°C). In Experiment 2, twigs were harvested after substantial chilling and exposed to opposite day/night forcing regimes (25°C/10°C vs. 10°C/25°C). In Experiment 1, shorter light–dark cycles under long chilling delayed budburst in C. betulus, whereas effects on budburst success depended on chilling duration in C. betulus and Q. robur. Interestingly, budburst occurred significantly earlier at 25°C/10°C than at 20°C/15°C in all species, up to 12 days in F. sylvatica, suggesting more effective daytime forcing or non-linear forcing accumulation. Experiment 2 demonstrated that forcing was more effective during daylight, with 10%, 19% and 51% more degree days required for budburst under warm nights in Q. robur, C. betulus and F. sylvatica, respectively, but no difference in A. pseudoplatanus. Our study shows that light–temperature interactions and non-linear forcing responses can shape budburst timing, implying that tree phenological responses to warming may depend not only on its magnitude but also on its timing within the diurnal cycle.

7.0 The core taxa connectivity in leaf microbiomes in ash trees stands is modulated by host-related and environmental factors.
Burghard V, Wende S, Kahlenberg G … Ulrich A.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthEnvironmental microbiome · 2026-10-07
matched: microbiome*, leaf, leaves, tree, trees, forest tree*
abstract

BackgroundForests cover large areas of the terrestrial land surface and play a key role in climate regulation and biodiversity conservation. Tree-associated microbial communities are integral components of forest ecosystems and contribute to plant functioning, stress tolerance, and interactions with the environment. However, the factors shaping the phyllosphere microbiome of forest trees across large environmental gradients remain less explored. Here, we investigated the bacterial leaf microbiome of Fraxinus excelsior across different climatic regions in Germany to identify its core microbiome and determine the relative importance of environmental and host-related factors shaping the phyllosphere microbiome of forest trees across large environmental gradients assembly. Therefore, 30 ash trees were sampled at each of the 12 sites across Germany, at which we assessed their leaf microbiome, leaf physio-chemical properties and a set of site parameters.ResultsIn total, 755 genera were detected. Only three genera Hymenobacter, Massilia and Sphingomonas were predominant across all sites. Geographical position, precipitation, tree age and the leaf and soil chemistry parameters were significant factors that explained 22.1% of variance between microbiome structures between the different sites. Seven genera were identified as the core leave microbiome of F. excelsior. The core microbiome assessed for each sampling site was mostly more diverse and differed between sites. Complex co-occurrence networks separated by sites with the identified six hub bacterial taxa proved that they were essential for the local core microbiomes. However, the network complexity differed significantly between the sampling sites.ConclusionOur findings clearly indicate the existence of an ash core microbiome on leaves that occurred at all sites but was locally differently interconnected to further microbiome members. The network analyses revealed that less frequent genera play important roles as hub taxa. Furthermore, we were also able to prove the modulating role of biotic and abiotic factors on the ash phyllosphere microbiome. An optimized core microbiome may be considered crucial for improving tolerance against plant diseases, such as ash dieback, in future.

6.5 Broad-scale shift in dominance of arbuscular mycorrhizal trees along the Japanese archipelago associated with tree diseases and climate warming.
Schaefer H, Yamashita N, Hashimoto S … Imaya A.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityAbiotic stressNature communications · 2026-09-11
matched: mycorrhiz*, symbio*, nutrient*, tree, trees
abstract

Distribution shifts of mycorrhizal tree symbioses are predicted to alter the nutrient cycling in temperate forests worldwide. Yet, our understanding of the drivers and specific impacts of such shifts remains limited. Here, we assign mycorrhizal types to trees at the species and genus level in >13,000 forest plots to assess the dominance of arbuscular mycorrhizal trees based on basal area proportions along the Japanese archipelago in temperate East Asia. We show a significant increase in arbuscular mycorrhizal tree dominance within 15 years associated with climate warming and the spread of tree diseases, contributing to a more important role of arbuscular mycorrhizal symbiosis in forest ecosystems than previously predicted. Arbuscular mycorrhizal tree dominance is negatively related to the ratio of soil carbon and nitrogen stocks, suggesting that tree symbiosis shifts will affect soil nitrogen availability and retention. In light of the global surge of biotic forest disturbance, our results stress the importance of forest pest and disease control to decelerate tree symbiosis shifts and their impact on ecosystem functioning.

6.5 Crown-based growth strategies explain the distribution and co-occurrence of tree functional groups along environmental gradients
Htoo, K. K., Onishi … Y.
preprintGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-16
matched: leaf, leaves, growth, conifer*, tree, trees
abstract

Elucidating interspecific variation in growth strategies across environments is critical for understanding tree diversity and distribution. However, a systematic analysis across diverse forests remains scarce due to limited large-tree crown measurements. We analysed 3,814 trees from 107 canopy species in 23 natural forests using forest census data and unmanned aerial vehicle. Relative growth rate (growth rate/biomass) was decomposed into growing space efficiency (growth rate/crown area) and crown area ratio (crown area/biomass). Relative growth rates were similar among functional groups (deciduous broadleaf, evergreen broadleaf, evergreen conifer), but their underlying components differed. Deciduous trees exhibited 34-43% higher crown area ratio but 24-27% lower growing space efficiency than evergreen broadleaf and conifer trees. Across environments, growing space efficiency correlated positively with mean annual temperature, likely from extended growing seasons while crown area ratio correlated negatively, particularly in deciduous species. Snow loading limited crown area ratio in evergreen species, while deciduous achieved higher values through avoiding snow loads. Correlation between crown area ratio and specific leaf area indicated a coordinated strategy between crowns and leaves. Different functional groups achieve comparable growth efficiency either through crown expansion or biomass production, explaining their distinct distribution as well as co-occurrence across broad environments.

6.5 Decomposition trajectories in mixed-species leaf litter in cool-temperate deciduous forest with understory dwarf bamboo
Yoshitake, S., Asai … T.
preprintAbiotic stressGrowthbioRxiv · 2026-10-13
matched: nutrient*, leaf, oak, birch, tree, lignin*
abstract

Litter decomposition plays a central role in nutrient cycling in forest ecosystems, but the effects of species mixing on decomposition of individual litter species remain poorly understood. We conducted a two-year litterbag experiment in a cool-temperate deciduous forest in northern Japan using litter from two dominant canopy tree species (oak and birch) and the dominant understory bamboo (Sasa). Single-species and mixed-species litterbags were used to examine litter mass loss, litter chemistry (carbon, nitrogen, and lignin), and microbial communities assessed by phospholipid fatty acid (PLFA) analysis. Oak and birch litter decomposed significantly faster than Sasa litter, whereas decomposition rate constants did not differ between the two deciduous tree species. Changes in litter chemistry relative to mass loss were similar between oak and birch but differed markedly in Sasa, indicating a distinct decomposition trajectory of Sasa. Total microbial biomass increased rapidly during the early stage of decomposition in all litter types owing to fungal colonization, whereas the fungal-to-bacterial PLFA ratio was substantially lower in Sasa litter than in deciduous tree litter. Species mixing had no significant overall non-additive effect on decomposition, although mixtures of oak and birch tended to accelerate decomposition, whereas mixtures including Sasa tended to suppress it. These changes in decomposition rate were accompanied by corresponding changes in microbial biomass, while decomposition trajectories remained largely unchanged within each litter species. Our results suggest that Sasa contributes a distinct decomposition process in cool-temperate forests and that species mixing primarily modifies decomposition rates rather than the sequence of chemical transformations during decomposition.

6.5 Genomic models for accurate estimation of breeding values in Sitka spruce (Picea sitchensis Bong. Carr).
Woolliams JA, Janes M, Tumas H … Gorjanc G.
GrowthHeredity · 2026-10-13
matched: growth, spruce, Picea
abstract

Genomics is advancing estimation of breeding values for selective breeding. Traditional pedigree information used in the estimation of breeding values can still be competitive to genomic information when markers do not adequately capture genetic variance or genomic datasets are not sufficiently large. Using the UK Sitka spruce breeding population as a study system, we assessed the accuracy of estimated breeding values using pedigree or genomic information. Nine phenotypic traits associated with growth and wood quality were measured across 1295 individuals in 50 full-sib families replicated across two plantation sites. Pedigree-based estimation was compared to genome-based estimation using identity by state (IBS) or identity by descent (IBD) information from 4123 SNPs. Accuracy of estimated breeding values was assessed using model-based standard errors, which assumes the fitted model and parameters are correct, and more objective 12-fold cross-validation by withholding some phenotypes from the model fitting. Accuracies obtained directly from the model were very similar across different types of information, but cross-validation showed genomic information gave highest accuracies for all but one of the traits. No bias was observed for any of the trait- information combinations. Results demonstrate that the estimation of breeding values with genomic information in this study system is already competitive with the pedigree information, despite evidence that the marker panel did not capture the full genetic variance. Furthermore, accuracies with genomic information have the potential to significantly improve, unlike pedigree, by increasing the size of the training set and marker density.

6.0 Dormancy type, seed traits, and species identity shape seed‐associated fungal community composition in tropical pioneer trees
McCulloch LA, Arnold AE, Dalling JW … Zalamea P
GrowthNew Phytologist · 2026-10-16
matched: seed*, germination, tree, trees, forest tree*
abstract

Summary Seed germination is regulated in many plants by seed dormancy, while postdispersal survival is often determined by soilborne fungi that infect seeds. In tropical forests, seeds in each of three dormancy types (physical, physiologically dormant, or quiescent) often share similar physical and chemical traits. We hypothesized that variation in seed traits aligned with seed dormancy should shape seed‐associated fungal communities, linking dormancy to seed survival. Seeds of 18 pioneer tree species were buried in each of five common garden plots in a lowland tropical forest in Panama for up to 36 months. Seeds were screened for viability and fungal infection at intervals throughout the experiment. Plant species with different dormancy types harbored distinct fungal communities. Dormancy type explained 6.5% of variation in fungal community composition after accounting for the far stronger signature of plant species identity (34%). Within dormancy types, differences in seed traits among species explained 49–56% of the variation in fungal communities, suggesting that seed traits drive niche partitioning beyond what is imposed by dormancy type alone. This study shows that variation in seed traits within a dormancy type filters seed‐associated fungal communities, which likely shapes the demography of tropical forest trees by influencing seed survival and germination.

Kamil-Sardar M, Laiho R, Jauhiainen J … Soosaar K.

Background and aimsPeatland forests play a significant, yet highly variable, role in atmospheric methane (CH4) and nitrous oxide (N2O) emissions. 6.0 Intraspecific leaf morphometric diversity in the endangered and narrow endemic Quercus trojana subsp. euboica (Fagaceae): a quantitative dataset from three natural populations in Euboea, Greece.
Lyrou F, Tourvas N, Aravanopoulos F.
GrowthBiodiversity data journal · 2026-09-29
matched: leaf, leaves, oak, Quercus
abstract

This study provides the first quantitative dataset on leaf morphometric diversity for Quercus trojana subsp. euboica (Papaioannou) K.I.Chr., a narrow endemic oak of Greece. This subspecies is of high conservation priority, listed in the Red Data Book of Rare and Threatened Plants of Greece and its restricted range in Northern Euboea was heavily impacted by a catastrophic wildfire in 2021. We sampled 301 leaves from three natural populations (Kerasia1, Kerasia2 and Prokopi) and processed them using a custom Python pipeline to extract 14 size and shape variables. Statistical analyses (PCA and LDA) showed that, while leaf shape remains relatively stable across populations, there is significant variation in leaf size. These data establish a baseline for monitoring the species' recovery and will be used alongside future genomic data to better understand the role of phenotypic plasticity in its conservation.

6.0 Silvicultural Management, Tree-Ring Analysis and Timber Quality Improvement in Zambian Forest Plantations: A Systematic Review
Nsofu BM, Hatimbula K.
preprintGrowthResearch Square · 2026-10-06
matched: stem, growth, development, Pinus, tree, wood formation, dendro*
abstract

Abstract Forest plantations play a critical role in meeting demand for timber, poles, pulpwood, fuelwood and other forest products while reducing pressure on natural forests. In Zambia, plantation forestry is dominated by exotic species, particularly Pinus and Eucalyptus, but timber quality is influenced by silvicultural practices, site conditions, climate variability, pests, diseases and management interventions. This systematic review synthesizes evidence on silvicultural management, timber quality improvement and dendrochronological applications in Zambian forest plantations. Following the PRISMA 2020 framework, literature published between 1980 and 2026 was identified from Scopus, Web of Science, ScienceDirect, SpringerLink, Wiley Online Library, CAB Abstracts, Google Scholar and additional forestry and institutional sources. Integrating silvicultural management with tree-ring monitoring offers a practical framework for improving timber quality, growth modelling and climate-adaptive plantation management in Zambia.

6.0 Stand and Landscape Drivers of Trap Captures of the Pine Wilt Disease Vector Monochamus alternatus (Coleoptera: Cerambycidae) in the Qinling Mountains.
Qi J, Zhang X, Zhao M … He H.
Ecology and evolution · 2026-10-11
matched: pine, Pinus, conifer*
abstract

Monochamus alternatus (Coleoptera: Cerambycidae) is the principal vector of pine wilt disease, one of the most destructive forest diseases affecting conifer ecosystems in East Asia. However, the relative roles of stand structure and surrounding landscape context in shaping spatial variation in trap captures of this vector in mountainous forests remain poorly understood. The associations of stand- and landscape-level factors with trap captures of M. alternatus were assessed in the Qinling Mountains of central China, a topographically complex region experiencing rapid expansion of pine wilt disease. Twenty pine forest plots were monitored with pheromone traps from April to October 2023, and stand characteristics and landscape variables were quantified at multiple spatial scales. Stand and landscape predictors contributed comparably to explaining spatial variation in trap captures. These results indicate that local host composition and fine-scale anthropogenic disturbance are associated with spatial variation in M. alternatus adult activity in mountainous forest landscapes. The identification of 250 m as the spatial scale of strongest landscape association provides a spatially explicit reference for improving surveillance and management strategies for pine wilt disease in topographically complex forest systems.

6.0 Variation among populations of Quercus macrocarpa in a reciprocal transplant experiment across a latitudinal gradient.
Rea LMS, Ostrowsky L, Mohn R … Cavender-Bares J.
Abiotic stressGrowthEcology · 2026-10-01
matched: nutrient*, seed*, growth, oak, Quercus, tree
abstract

Ongoing climate change will negatively impact tree populations unless they are able to acclimate to the changes in their local environment. Effective planning for climate adaptation management requires an understanding of the current state of local adaptation and physiological performance to assess whether populations are at risk of local extinction, to determine if seed movement is appropriate, and to select appropriate seed sources if intervention is needed. We established a new reciprocal transplant experiment (Adaptation to Climate and Environment [ACE]) across a latitudinal gradient in North America spanning a range of climate and soil conditions to examine the impacts of warming on three bur oak (Quercus macrocarpa) populations across much of the species' range. We established common gardens in Minnesota, Illinois, and Oklahoma in 2021 grown from seeds collected in 2018-2019 within 50 km of each of those locations from a total of 60 maternal families. In 2023, we measured physiology and 3-year growth and survival to understand local adaptation in bur oak populations. We aimed to (1) assess local adaptation in each of the populations using survival and size as fitness metrics and (2) evaluate physiological responses to different environments along the latitudinal gradient. We found evidence for local adaptation of the southern population in the southernmost garden, but that the northern populations were maladapted to high levels of warming, as evidenced by their low survival, growth, and photosynthetic rates in the warmest common garden. However, mild climate and relatively high nutrient conditions in the middle garden resulted in the highest fitness and best physiological performance for all populations. Growth and survival were correlated in the middle garden but were decoupled in the northern and southern gardens, likely due to stress associated with more extreme climates at the ends of the gradient. Our results suggest that southern seed sources may perform well in warmer conditions in the north brought on by climate change, which has important implications for managers assisting broadly ranged tree species in adapting to climate change.

10.5 STADIA: A Spatio-Temporal Deep-Learning Framework for Cross-Stage Integration and Batch Correction of Plant Developmental Spatial Transcriptomics.
Zhi D, Guan X, Wang L, Chen W.
GrowthTrees and woody plantsPhysiologia plantarum · 2026-09-01
matched: stem, cambium, shoot*, development, poplar*, deep learning, spatial transcriptom*
abstract

Spatial transcriptomics enables the spatial architecture and transcriptional dynamics of plant organ development to be resolved in situ. However, integrating plant spatial transcriptomic data across developmental stages is challenging because genuine developmental variation can be confounded with technical variation arising from section source, capture region and sequencing batch, making it difficult to attenuate technical effects without compromising developmental signals. To address this challenge, we developed STADIA, a spatio-temporal deep-learning framework for plant developmental spatial transcriptomics. STADIA combines a graph-attention autoencoder with structural-context-conditioned batch-predictability suppression, stage-order constraints, PCGrad-based gradient coordination and hierarchical unbalanced optimal transport to learn an integrated latent representation that attenuates batch-associated variation while preserving spatial organization and developmental continuity and accommodating non-equivalent tissue transitions. In a semi-synthetic benchmark, STADIA maintained balanced performance across batch mixing, anatomical-domain preservation and developmental-information preservation under strong batch effects and high sparsity. In the same-stage replicate sections from the soybean early-maturation stage (EMS), STADIA increased local between-section mixing while retaining major anatomical compartments. In a poplar stem developmental series spanning the shoot apex to internode 7, the integrated representation showed increased cross-stage continuity while retaining stage-specific organization and spatially coherent tissue compartments, and supported exploratory identification of cambium-associated trajectory candidates. Together, these results support STADIA as a framework for cross-stage integration of plant spatial transcriptomic data and for downstream analysis of spatial domains and developmentally ordered transcriptional patterns.

9.0 Centrifugal Microfluidic Soil-to-DNA Preparation for the Portable Detection of Phytophthora infestans Using a CRISPR/Cas12a-Based Quantum Dot Fluorescent Lateral Flow Assay.
Xu C, Cao L, Li S … Wang Y.
Plant-microbe interactions and immunityACS sensors · 2026-10-10
matched: pathogen*, Phytophthora, microfluidic*, CRISPR
abstract

Portable detection of soil-borne pathogens is constrained by laborious soil sample preparation and nucleic acid extraction. Here, we report a centrifugal Soil-on-a-Disc (SoaD) microfluidic chip for rapid soil-to-DNA preparation with automated on-chip clarification and DNA purification, combined with a CRISPR/Cas12a-based quantum dot (QD) fluorescent lateral flow assay (LFA) and smartphone-based fluorescence analysis for the portable detection of Phytophthora infestans (P. infestans). The seven-layer PMMA disc integrates cascaded 5 and 0.8 μm membranes for stepwise particle filtration, together with a silica membrane and active puncture valves for sequential DNA capture, washing, and elution under centrifugal control. The SoaD chip reduced the turbidity of soil suspensions significantly from 12,980 NTU to below the detection limit (∼0 NTU), yielding a clarified sample matrix for downstream DNA extraction, and completed DNA extraction within 20 min. Compared with a commercial soil DNA kit, the relative total DNA recovery efficiency for P. infestans-spiked soils was 94.9 ± 3.3%. Coupled with downstream recombinase polymerase amplification and smartphone-assisted fluorescence readout, the system achieved a limit of detection of 0.135 ng/μL for PiNW-derived RPA products and detected P. infestans in soils spiked at levels as low as 1 spore/g. As a secondary validation of extraction compatibility, 16S rRNA/ITS amplicon sequencing showed that chip-prepared DNA yielded bacterial and fungal community profiles highly consistent with those obtained using the commercial soil DNA kit (r = 0.996/0.923). This work advances portable CRISPR-based sensing toward complex soil samples and provides a field-oriented strategy for molecular surveillance of soil-borne plant diseases.

7.0 Benchmarking ensemble and deep learning models for drought-responsive gene prioritisation in rice using multi-omics data.
Govaichelvan KN, Pathmanathan D, Zainal-Abidin RA, Abu A.
Abiotic stressTrees and woody plantsPloS one · 2026-10-09
matched: drought, tree, deep learning, machine learning
abstract

Identifying drought-responsive genes is critical for climate-resilient crop breeding, yet computational gene-trait association often relies on single-omics or generalized genomic datasets. Optimal algorithm selection for high-dimensional, small multi-omics datasets also remains a persistent challenge. We present a machine learning framework that extends the QTG-Finder feature set by integrating transcriptomic and proteomic features to classify drought-responsive genes in rice. Using MBKBase annotations, we evaluated the ability of these models to prioritise 336 drought-responsive genes among 2,416 abiotic stress-associated genes, of which the remaining 2,080 constituted an operational background set. We tested four algorithms: Random Forest, XGBoost, one-dimensional convolutional neural network, and multilayer perceptron, while addressing severe class imbalance and assessing model stability. Tree-based ensemble methods achieved higher performance than the deep learning models in this dataset, which showed early overfitting. The fine-tuned Random Forest achieved a recall of 0.67 and an AUC-ROC of 0.87 under nested cross-validation, conditional on a feature matrix assembled before gene-level data splitting. Across repeated evaluations of a small internal holdout of 10 genes, mean accuracy and recall were 0.90 and 0.63; this set is too small to support a generalizability claim and is reported only as a consistency check. The newly added transcriptomic and proteomic features improved recall from 0.47 to 0.67 compared with genomics alone. Feature importance and ablation analyses indicated that transcriptomics-derived features, particularly measures of expression distribution, magnitude, and direction, contributed most to predictive performance. Functional enrichment analysis supported the biological relevance of the prioritised genes. These results demonstrate the value of multi-omics integration for gene prioritisation in small tabular datasets while highlighting current limitations of deep learning in this setting.

6.5 Comparative Analysis of Classical Machine Learning and Deep Learning Regression Models for Codon-Level k-mer Composition Analysis of the Nipah Virus Genome
Abid¹ A, Bilal¹ M, Khan SUR.
preprintPlant-microbe interactions and immunityGrowthTrees and woody plantsResearch Square · 2026-10-05
matched: pathogen*, root, tree, deep learning, machine learning
abstract

Abstract The Nipah virus (NiV) is highly virulent zoonotic paramyxovirus, where a complete genome sequence resource is relatively limited compared to other emerging pathogens. In this study, the codon level (3-mer) k-mer composition analysis of 93 complete Nipah virus genome sequences retrieved from the NCBI Nucleotide database was presented. The genomes were encoded into a vector of 64 elements representing the number of appearances of each of the 64 three-nucleotide combinations. The feature set was subjected to descriptive statistics, covariance and Pearson correlation between each feature, which showed very high degrees of pairwise collinearity in the set of codon-count features. Using a correlation-based feature-selection procedure (|r| > 0.99), the feature space was reduced from 64 to 5 almost independent codon counts (AAA, TAG, TCG, CGC and CCG). We considered seven regression algorithms across three learning paradigms (classical models: Linear Regression, K-Nearest Neighbors, Support Vector Machine/SVR, an ensemble model: Random Forest, deep sequential models: Long Short-Term Memory network and Simple Recurrent Neural Network, and a Decision Tree baseline) and compared their performance using the number of codons CCG as a regression target and the remaining four codon counts as predictors. The 70/20/10 train/validation/test split was used to train all models, and they were compared using the coefficient of determination (R²), root mean squared error (RMSE), and mean absolute error (MAE). Decision Tree Regression achieved the best performance (R² = 0.9945), followed by Linear Regression (R² = 0.9908), Random Forest (R² = 0.9892), LSTM (R² = 0.9731), and SimpleRNN (R² = 0.9718), while KNN (R² = 0.8038) and SVM/SVR (R² = 0.1038 with feature scaling) underperformed. The results are evaluated with the knowledge of the large compositional constraints on codon usage over a set of Nipah virus genomes, the known sensitivity of instance- and margin-based regressors to unscaled, collinear small-sample data, and the limited benefit of deep sequential architectures on a pseudo-sequence of a very small tabular sample (no temporal dimension). This paper presents in a transparent manner the pipeline results, statistical results and comparative modeling results, along with the limitations of the dataset, as a baseline for future codon-composition based computational studies of Nipah virus.

6.0 Automating the bottleneck: a scalable deep learning pipeline for phytolith detection, imaging, and curation across single-cell and multi-cell morphotypes.
Pugliese M, Kyle-Robinson L, Elgamal H … Ramsey MN.
Frontiers in plant science · 2026-09-25
matched: microscop*, deep learning, machine learning, single-cell
abstract

Deep learning offers powerful tools for automating phytolith classification. Although foundational models have enabled image classification with little or no task specific training, specialized domains such as phytolith images still require a labor-intensive process to acquire labelled training data. This article presents a novel, scalable imaging workflow that addresses this bottleneck by integrating low-magnification slide scanning with artificial intelligence assisted phytolith detection and targeted high-magnification 3D Z-stack capture optimized for both multi-cell and single-cell phytoliths. Automated pre-processing enables imaging of 16 slides per week generating 100's of curated phytolith training images per slide. Developed in collaboration with Nikon Imaging Specialists and validated on 34 comparative grass and rush taxa, the system combines expert-guided sampling with customized automated acquisition routines to capture, segment, tile and curate images for deep learning input. Key workflow optimizations, including hardware upgrades, asynchronous file conversion, and iterative refinement of the artificial intelligence detection model, achieved a 350% improvement in extended depth of field rendering speed and a 55,450% improvement in TIFF export speed, enabling sustained high-throughput acquisition without performance degradation. The workflow was designed to optimize imaging of mixed multi-cell and single-cell phytolith assemblages and is currently generating training data for DL models targeting wild versus domesticated wheat discrimination in addition to grass species identification from multi-cell husk phytolith morphology. Beyond phytoliths, the pipeline is modular, replicable across microscopy platforms, and readily adaptable to other plant microfossils and microstructures, including pollen, starch grains and other silica bodies, through targeted artificial intelligence retraining, making large-scale machine learning applications in plant science, paleoethnobotany and related fields more accessible and reproducible.
